Arc User Guide

How to get around the program, then hands-on lessons you follow with it open.

1. How to use this guide

Prefer to watch? The video lessons show Arc being used, start to finish, in a minute or two each. In the program they play in a small player over your drawing (from the welcome screen or Ctrl+K → videos), so you can follow along: drag it by its header, resize it from the corner, ⤢ makes it bigger, ⧉ pops it out into a floating window that stays on top and can go to a second screen, and ↗ opens it in its own window, carrying on from the same moment. The quick reference card fits the keys, gestures and typed sizes on one printed page.

Part 2 to Part 7 are a tour: read them once to learn where things are. Part 8 has the hands-on lessons (a small house, 3D printing, and the newer tools), with a panel in the program that checks each step. Follow them step by step with the program open. Each step says where to click and shows a screenshot of what you should see. Click any screenshot to see it full size.

The finished Lesson house is included to compare your work: File ▾ → Samples… → Lesson house.

Typing sizes. When a tool is waiting for a click, you can type a size instead and press Enter. Type feet and inches like 50', 14'6" or 174". What you type shows in the Size box at the bottom right.
Undo. Ctrl+Z undoes anything. Esc or a right-click stops the tool you're using.

2. The main screen

AreaWhat it does
Top barMenus in the order you'll usually want them, each in short groups with headings: File ▾ (plans, bringing in a drawing, settings), Export ▾ (showing it to people, drawings, 3D files) and a quick Save; Design ▾ (style, rooms designed for you, suggested layouts), Review ▾ (design checks, codes, the permit set, schedules, markups), Budget ▾ (what it costs, each trade, quotes for the client, the schedule), View ▾ (what the 3D view shows), Draw ▾ and Modify ▾; undo and redo; Snap & units ▾; Show ▾ (clean floor plan, dimensions, grid, floor patterns, wall fill); Sheets, Guide ▾, search, fit and 3D view. Can't find something? Press Ctrl+K and type a word.
Tools (left)The tools you use most are one click: Select, Measure, Wall, Room, Door, Window and Blocks. The rest sit under short headings (Finishes, Roof, Stairs & decks, Yard, Framing, Pipes & wires, Notes, Draw): click one to pick a tool from it (see The tool bar's headings). Then Move, Duplicate (copies right in this plan; Ctrl+C / Ctrl+V copies into another plan), Rotate, Mirror and Delete. The letter in each corner is its keyboard shortcut.
Level barAbove the plan: pick the floor you're drawing on, + Add level, level Settings, ▼ ▲ to go down or up a floor, and the This floor / + below / Whole house switch for how much of the house shows, on the plan and in 3D.
Panel (right)The settings for the tool you're using, or for whatever you've selected. With nothing selected it shows the Project settings and the Design check. Click a section's heading to fold it away (click again to open it); the panel remembers which ones you folded. The small tab on the panel's left edge hides the whole panel for a bigger drawing (click the tab at the window's right edge to bring it back), and dragging either tab makes the panel wider or narrower.
LayersBelow the panel. The eye hides a layer; the lock stops it being selected; the snowflake (when you point at a layer) freezes it. Quick sets opens Floors & layers. Click the Layers heading to fold the list down.
Status barAt the bottom. Tells you what the tool wants next. Read it whenever you're unsure.

The tool bar's headings

Click a heading and a picker opens beside it, with each tool's picture, its name, its key letter and what it's for. Pick one and the picker closes; the heading lights up and shows the tool you're using. The pin at the top of the picker keeps it open as a small window you can drag out of the way, handy when you use several of its tools in a row. Esc closes it and the arrow keys move through it. Keys still work without opening anything: S is Stairs, F is Roof, U is Ducts, pipes & wires. On an iPad or a phone the picker opens from the bottom of the screen with big rows for your finger, and with Bigger text on the tool bar's names grow too.

HeadingIts tools
FinishesFloors & baseboards (A), Paint (K), Decorate (Y)
RoofRoof (F), Dormer, Skylight, Chimney
Stairs & decksStairs (S), Porch or covered deck (G), Terrace or balcony, Floor opening, Trellis or pergola, Spiral ramp
YardOutdoor (O: patios, decks, fences, beds, the lot), Plants, Sprinklers, Rock, Stone facing
FramingFraming piece (J), Post, Beam, Stud, Joist, Column grid
Pipes & wiresDucts, pipes & wires (U), or start straight on Ducts, Pipes or Wires
NotesText (T), Dimension, Markup (I), Section cut, Detail callout
DrawLine (L), Polyline (P), Rectangle, Circle, Arc, Hatch (H), 3D shape

In ArcRes the drawing tools (Draw, and Pipes & wires) show a small lock until the first lesson is done, or until you say you know your way around.

Samples

The Lakeside House is a modern house on a lake made only with Arc's own tools: floor-to-ceiling glass that turns the corner, a stacked-stone ground floor and a wood upper floor cantilevered over the terrace, an infinity pool, furnished rooms and a guided tour. Its lot on a small bay is planted with the Landscape tools: granite at the water, a bank of native plants, a birch with hostas and iris at the door, a fire pit with Muskoka chairs by the water, steps down to a dock and path lights. Open it at the Empty lot stage to build it yourself with its hands-on lesson (Guide ▾ → Hands-on lessons).

The Lesson house is the small house the hands-on lessons build, on a 60′ × 100′ city lot. Its Elevated stage shows a small yard done well: curved front beds with edging, a flowering crabapple, a stone walk with path lights, a pergola patio, a fire pit with seat walls, a mixed border along the back fence and raised vegetable beds. Select a bed to see what's in it, or open Budget ▾ to see what it costs.

Welcome screen

When Arc starts, the welcome screen offers the quickest ways in: New house plan, New commercial plan, Start from a template, Open a plan, Explore a sample and Bring in a drawing or scan, with links into this guide. Untick Show this when Arc starts to skip it; open it again any time from File ▾ → Welcome screen…. It doesn't show when Arc brings back unsaved work after a crash.

Templates: house plans by size, duplexes, garages and commercial shells

File ▾ → New from a template… opens the template gallery: folders down the left, the folder's templates beside them. The house plans are laid out for city lots with the garage entered straight from the street (a front garage with a bonus room over it), or a laned lot with the garage at the back; the long acreage bungalows are in the larger sizes, one with a side-entry garage.

  • Size folders: Under 1,200, 1,200–1,500, 1,500–1,800, 1,800–2,000, 2,000–2,400, 2,400–2,800, 2,800–3,000, 3,000–3,300, 3,300–3,500 and 3,500–4,000 sq ft, measured outside above grade (the basement and garage aren't counted).
  • Style starter houses: the front-garage two-storey (about 2,100 sq ft) already dressed as a Modern farmhouse, Modern, Craftsman, Tudor or Mid-century, as 3 bed + office + bonus room or 4 bed + bonus room.
  • Duplexes & townhomes: a side-by-side duplex with front garages (paired in the middle), a laned duplex with garages at the lane, and a row of four townhomes with garages at the back. Party walls are drawn between the homes and the size shown is each home's.
  • Garages & garden suites: detached double, oversized double with a workbench, triple, a garage with a garden suite above, and a garden suite on its own.
  • Rooms, shells & 3D print: the blank plan, the small cottage and studio, single rooms and the 3D print starts.

Every house card says the building pocket it needs: the house's width across the front, measured outside, which has to fit between the side setbacks, with the lot width that gives with 4' side yards, and the house's depth. For example Fits a 29' building pocket (8.8 m): about a 37' lot with 4' side yards. House depth 49'. Check the actual lot's setbacks and zoning. The same note goes in the plan's Project notes.

Use asks for a style (or Plain, to choose finishes later) and builds the house: walls, doors and windows, stairs, roofs, the porch or covered entry, the kitchen, bathroom, laundry and mechanical fixtures fitted around the doors, furniture, smoke and CO alarms, and cars in the garage. Its basement comes in Design ▾ → Design options…: left open to develop, finished (a rec room, bedrooms with egress windows, a bath), and on the laned houses a legal basement suite. Change anything afterwards: it's an ordinary plan.

Bi-levels and narrow infill. Bi-level: 3 bed + ensuite, raised main floor (41' wide, for a 50' lot) has a split entry: a landing at the front door, a half flight up to the main floor and down to the lower level. The ground is set about 5' below the main floor (spot heights you can move with the Outdoor tool), so the lower level is half out of the ground with full-size windows. Its lower level comes three ways in Design options: to develop, finished (two more bedrooms, a family room and a bath) or a legal suite. Infill two-storey, 20' wide is for a 28'–30' lot with a back lane (Edmonton's 25' skinny lots need a narrower house): three bedrooms up, the garage at the lane, and the same three basement choices.

New lot: size, setbacks and the houses that fit

File ▾ → New lot (size and setbacks)… starts a plan from the lot. Type its width along the street and its depth, and the setbacks: how far the house has to stay from the front, the back and each side (in feet or metres). They start from typical numbers (front 15', back 25', sides 4'): check them against your city's zoning; Arc remembers yours for next time. As you type, it shows the building area (what the setbacks leave) and the house templates that fit it, roomiest first.

  • Start with the lot: the property line, the street along the front, the building area as a dashed blue outline, and their sizes.
  • Use this house: the template is built (in the style you pick) and the lot drawn round it, the house centred across the building area and at the front setback, in every one of its design options. A garage at the lane may sit in the back yard.

Either way, the plan gets a design check (in Review ▾ → Project specs & guidelines) that warns if a wall goes outside the building area, on any floor above the ground; a detached garage or shed is left out.

Lots that aren't rectangles. Pick the Lot shape; a picture beside the numbers shows the lot, its street side and the building area as you type:

  • Pie (wider at the back), as on a cul-de-sac or the outside of a curve, and Reverse pie (wider at the street): type the width at the street, the Width at the back and the depth.
  • Irregular: type its sides for any other shape. Go round the lot with the lot on your left: side 1 is along the street, from its left-hand corner as you stand in the street, then each side's length and the corner angle where it meets the next (90° is square). Add as many sides as it has; leave the last one out and Arc closes the outline with a straight line. It starts from the rectangle you had, so you only change what's different.
  • From a Real Property Report or survey plan: open Paste the bearings, type or paste one side a line (a bearing and a length, the street side first, either way round) and click Use these. Arc turns them into sides and sets true north from them, so the property lines show the report's own bearings and the sun study's shadows fall the right way.

Each side keeps its own setback (the street side the front setback, the sides facing the back the back setback, the others the side setbacks), and the dashed building area follows the lot's shape. The house templates that fit are tried against the building area's real shape, as near the front as they go (a reverse pie is widest at the street, so a wide house can fit there). A pie's front can follow the street's curve: type its radius in Front curve radius (0 for a straight front). In Edmonton, a pie lot keeps the city's lot area: change a width and the depth follows.

An Edmonton lot from its address. File ▾ → New lot from an Edmonton address… (or the address box at the top of New lot): type the house number and street, like 10736 122 Street NW, and click Find. Arc looks it up in the City of Edmonton's open data (it needs the internet) and fills in:

  • The lot's size and shape: its area from the city, its width from the spacing of the neighbouring lots on the same street, and the depth from the two. The city no longer publishes lot lines (they come from the province's licensed mapping, which is what SLIM Maps shows), so on a straight street the lot is a rectangle. Round a cul-de-sac's bulb or a curve in the street (from the city's road centre lines), Arc works out a pie (wider at the back) or a reverse pie (narrower at the back, on the inside of a curve), with its front following the curve, from the lot's area, where its middle is and the lots on either side; it says Shape guessed from address points: check the survey or Real Property Report. Either way, check it against the Real Property Report or the survey plan from Alberta Land Titles and type the right size. Change the width and the depth follows, keeping the area.
  • The zone's setbacks under Zoning Bylaw 20001 (RS, RSF, RSM, RM, RL and RR, and the special areas' GLDF, GRH, SLD, SRH, SRA, PLD, RVRH and BRH), and its height and site coverage as design checks, with the zone's link and its overlays. For other zones (a Direct Control zone, say) it gives the zone's name and link to look them up. A treed boulevard, a lane or a corner can change the numbers: check with the city.
  • A corner lot: when there's no lot beside it on one side (a street, then), Arc says so and sets Corner lot to that side, so that side gets the zone's flanking setback (and, in some zones, a smaller rear setback). A corner house addressed on the avenue still counts as the neighbour, and a lot round a cul-de-sac isn't a corner lot (the gap beside it is the bulb). Change it if Arc got it wrong.
  • A ravine or the river valley nearby: an environmental reserve lot (like Lot 27ER), land zoned A (River Valley) or NA (Natural Areas), or the River Valley and Ravine System Protection Overlay near the lot. Arc warns and points to the City's Top-of-Bank policy (C542A): always check it before you design near a bank.
  • Ground that has changed: when the lot's subdivision is newer than the city's contour lines (2021), or the house was built after them, or the contour lines and the ground the city measured beside the buildings differ by more than about half a metre, Arc says so: use the lot grading plan or a survey for the heights.
  • The ground: the city's contour lines every 0.5 m round the lot, and the ground beside each building, become the plan's contours and spot heights, so the 3D view and the grading tools have the real slope. The main floor is taken as 8" above the ground at the house; the plan notes how high that is above sea level. Untick Ground heights to leave them out.
  • The buildings already there, from the city's 2019 roof outlines with their heights: as pre-existing walls kept (for a renovation or a yard), as walls being removed (for a new house; a template house always does this), as outlines, or left out. The walls go 18" inside the roof outline. The neighbours' buildings come as outlines with their heights.
  • Where it is: the sun study's place (Edmonton) and true north, so the shadows fall the right way, and the address and legal description for the title block.

Arc works out which side the street is on from the lots across it (or the garage behind the house); if it's wrong, click The street is on the other side. The address is only sent to the city's open data site, data.edmonton.ca. Contains information licensed under the Open Government Licence – City of Edmonton.

No Real Property Report? The Edmonton lookup's Find your lot's survey shows the lot's legal description (plan, block and lot, with a Copy button) and the ways to get its shape: the report you may already have (the papers from buying the house, the lawyer, the survey firm on it, the lender); the lot's own survey plan from Alberta Land Titles (SPIN2 or a registry agent), whose side lengths and bearings go straight into Paste the bearings; a new report from an Alberta Land Surveyor; and meanwhile tracing a screenshot from SLIM Maps with File ▾ → Plot plan or survey…. If the lot you draw is more than 8% off the city's lot area, the window says so.

Your own templates go in folders. Save this plan as a template… (or File ▾ → Save as a template…) suggests the size folder from the plan's square footage; pick another, or choose New folder… and name it (a client's name, a builder's series). Each saved template has Move to… to file it somewhere else. They're kept on this computer or iPad.

Commercial shells (Arc). In Arc the gallery has Homes and Commercial at the top; it opens on the side the open plan is (a house plan shows the house templates). The commercial folders have a 1,200 sq ft retail bay, a restaurant, an office suite, a clinic, a warehouse with offices, a four-bay strip plaza with demising walls, and Empty commercial shell, which asks for the floor area and the width across the front. They start as commercial projects with occupant loads, exits and fixture counts checked. ArcRes shows only the homes.

About & licence

The first time the program starts, it shows its licence: it's licensed to one person for their own use, so please don't share the program, its installer or your sign-in. Your plans and drawings are yours. Click I agree to carry on; it doesn't ask again. File ▾ or Guide ▾ → About & licence… shows the version, who your copy is licensed to (with its licence number) and the full terms. That name also appears in small print on the drawing sheets and the PDFs you save.

Privacy. About also says what leaves this device. Your plans stay on it, and Arc has no ads, tracking or account of its own. Arc sends something only when you ask: the address you type into New lot’s Edmonton lookup goes to data.edmonton.ca, with nothing else from the plan; a share link you publish puts that page on Cloudflare Pages; and a video lesson that isn’t on this device plays from arc-137.github.io. On the web, the app itself comes from arc-137.pages.dev, and Cloudflare checks your sign-in.

Search for any command (Ctrl+K)

Press Ctrl+K, or click the magnifier on the top bar, and type a word or two: wall, pdf, sofa, grid off, basement. The list finds tools, every menu command, the Snap & units and Show switches, levels, the 3D toolbar's buttons (with the 3D view open), every block in the library and the topics in this guide. It also finds things in this plan: a window or door by its mark (W12), a room by its name or number (kitchen, 101), every block of one kind (sofa), everything marked to remove (walls to remove) and anything by the value of one of your own fields. Pick one and it's selected on the plan, on its floor. Use ↑ ↓ and Enter, or click. With nothing typed it shows what you used last. The letter beside a tool is its shortcut, so search is also a way to learn them.

The ? buttons

Most headings in the side panel have a small ? on the right. Click it and this guide opens at the part that explains that tool or setting. The guide opens in its own window, and every ? reuses it.

Learning while you draw: Guide ▾

The Guide ▾ menu on the top bar keeps the help in one place, without leaving your drawing:

  • Video lessons: each lesson is listed by name and length. Pick one and it plays in a small window over the drawing; drag it aside, make it bigger, or pop it out onto a second screen.
  • Hands-on lessons: a panel walks you through the steps and ticks each one off as you do it.
  • User guide, Tour of the screen and Tools, one by one: this guide, opened at the right place.
  • Quick reference card, Search commands and help (Ctrl+K) and the Welcome screen.

Everything for pricing a job is under Budget ▾: the takeoff, takeoff on a PDF, the quote, change orders, selections and allowances, and the shopping list and budget.

Preferences and Project settings

File ▾ → Preferences… (Ctrl+,) holds how Arc works on this computer, for every plan: units for new plans, the code rules (Canada NBC, US IRC, UK), the country for prices and money (Canada, the US or the UK), angle lock, snapping and rounding, AutoCAD keys, what the plan shows, the 3D background, how often autosave runs, the welcome screen, showing commercial tools in house projects, and a button that opens every folded panel section again.

Backup copies. Arc keeps earlier copies for you. On a computer: the last 10 copies of each plan from before it was saved over (one every 10 minutes at most), a copy of unsaved work every 15 minutes, and always one when a plan with unsaved work is replaced by a new one (New, Open, Start fresh). In a browser or on an iPad: the last 4 copies of your work. File ▾ → Backup copies… lists them; Open starts one as a new, unsaved plan, so the file it came from is never changed. Save it under a name to keep it.

On an iPad or in a browser, autosave and the backup copies are kept in the browser's own storage. When that room is nearly full, Arc says so once (before anything fails): save your plan to Files (File ▾ → Save) and remove old copies in Backup copies. Everything you tap on an iPad is at least finger size (44 px).

File ▾ → Project settings… holds what's saved with this plan: the title block (project name, address, client, designer, drawn by, date), the project type, its units, whether to check it against codes, and its levels. The Project heading in the panel (nothing selected) has a ? that leads here too. Changes in both apply straight away.

Houses and commercial projects

A house project keeps the screen simple: the commercial-only tools are tucked away. That's Design ▾ → Sprinklers and HVAC (supply air), the life safety plan, room numbers and door marks and steel framing in View ▾, the column grid in Draw ▾, and the commercial blocks (office, restaurant, commercial restroom, life safety, ceiling, HVAC, building core). Set Project type to Commercial or Mixed-use (Project settings, or the Project section of the panel) and they all appear. To keep them in house projects too, tick Show commercial tools in house projects in Preferences.

Building codes follow the project type too. A new house plan uses the house code set chosen in Preferences (House code: Canada NBC, US IRC or UK; new installs start with NBC). A new commercial plan uses the matching commercial set (Canada NBC Part 3 if your house code is NBC, otherwise US IBC), with exit-stair limits of about a 7" riser, an 11" tread and a 44" width. Changing a plan's type switches its code set with it. To use another set for one plan, pick it under Code limits in Project settings, or change the limits in the Stairs tool's Code limits section; the plan keeps them.

Project specs, guidelines and notes on warnings

Review ▾ → Project specs & guidelines… (also a button in Project settings and in the panel's Project section) holds rules for this plan: a client brief, a bylaw, neighbourhood or HOA guidelines, an accessibility goal, or your own standards. When the plan goes against one, the Design check shows it as a purple Spec warning, with a purple marker on the plan.

GroupRules
RoomsMinimum room area, minimum room width, minimum ceiling height, window glass area as a % of the floor. Rooms are picked by name: bedroom finds Bedroom 2 and Primary bedroom; leave it blank for every room.
Doors, halls & stairsMinimum door width (every door, outside doors or inside doors), minimum hallway width, stair limits (riser, tread, width)
SiteSetback from the property lines, maximum building height (to the ridge), maximum lot coverage. These need the property lines drawn with the Outdoor tool (closed round the lot for coverage); until then the rule reminds you. Driveway length: from each garage door straight out to the back of the sidewalk (a path or line named Sidewalk), or to the front property line when no sidewalk is drawn. Maximum building length: the outside of the walls, front to back or side to side, every floor from the ground floor up. New lot adds both for an Edmonton RSF lot (6.0 m and 30 m).
Developer guidelinesNo vinyl siding (what each outside wall is really clad in, its Exterior finish first: stone or stucco over a vinyl wall type isn't vinyl, and fibre-cement lap isn't either), approved roofing only (type the list, one per line), the garage's share of the house front, a triple garage's jog (the third stall steps back), minimum door height (garage doors, the front door, or every outside door), and the rear eave's height above the grade on a walkout (from the design grades).
ChecklistA reminder in your own words ("Client wants no carpet", "HOA: black windows only"). It stays at the top of the Design check until you click Done.

Each rule can say where it comes from (Source: "City bylaw 4.2", "HOA", "Client brief"), and that shows beside its warnings. Untick a rule to pause it, or click × to delete it. The ready-made sets add several at once: Accessible / aging in place, Neighbourhood / HOA guidelines and Comfortable family home; change their numbers to suit. The dialog shows how many warnings each rule has right now.

Developer rule sets. Arc, ArcRes and Arc Landscape (Ctrl+K → "developer rule sets"). A new subdivision's developer has architectural guidelines of its own. Melcor Jagare Ridge (first pass) adds them as rules: no vinyl siding, approved roofing, the garage no more than 71% of the front, a triple garage's jog, 8′ garage doors, and on a walkout the rear eave no more than 20′ above the grade. What a plan can't measure is a checklist reminder: decks built with the house, the limit on bare parging, 24″ returns of the front's stone or siding round the corners. It is a first pass: developers change their guidelines, and the developer's architectural approval is what counts, so check each rule against the current guidelines (the approved roofing list starts empty: type it in). For your own subdivision or client, set up the rules, type a name and click Save these rules as a set: the set is saved with the plan, and its button adds the rules again (in another design option, say).

Notes on warnings. Every warning in the Design check, codes, tips and specs alike, has a Note button. Type why it's fine, what to do, or who to ask; it's saved with the plan when you click away, shows under the warning, and stays even if you Ignore the warning. Ignore moves a warning to the "ignored" list at the bottom, where Check again brings it back.

Lot type & design grades

A new house on a new lot comes with an approved lot grading plan: the finished ground's elevation at the house's corners, front and back, and the main floor or top of the foundation. Review ▾ → Lot type & design grades… (Arc and ArcRes) takes them as the plan prints them, in metres (or feet), with a sketch of the house showing which corner is which, seen from the street. Pick the lot type too: Regular, Walkout (Edmonton's "Type W", the back a storey lower) or Split.

  • The design grades are kept apart from the existing ground (the city's contour lines and spot heights). If you typed a main floor and the city's ground was set with a different one, saving moves that ground to match, so its real elevations stay the same.
  • Height is measured from the average grade, the average of the four corners' grades, to the ridge, not from the main floor. That's Arc's reading of how Edmonton measures height; it isn't checked against the wording of Zoning Bylaw 20001 (section 5.70), so check it with the city. On a walkout or split lot Arc also warns when the house is over the limit measured from the lowest grade, so you can check how the city measures that lot.
  • The Design check warns where the top of the foundation (where the wood framing starts) is less than 150 mm above a design grade (NBC 9.15.4).
  • Drafted elevations and sections show the average grade with its elevation, the top of the foundation and how far it shows above grade, the overall height from grade, and the height limit from grade. The ground line near the house follows the design grades.
  • A slab-on-grade house's footings go the frost depth below the lowest design grade. The site plan shows each design grade in a blue box, with the existing ground's height under it.

Untick Use these to keep them in the plan but measure from the main floor as before. A plan without design grades works as it always has.

3. The menus

Each menu is in short groups with a heading. Where a menu has the same tools as the tool bar (Draw ▾), it uses the tool bar's headings. Each command is in one menu only.

Project notes

Review ▾ → Project notes… keeps everything about this job in one place: what the engineer changed, what the inspector asked for, the client's wishes, a supplier's lead time, your own reminders. Each note has a title, the note, its kind (engineer, inspector, client, supplier or general), who said it and the date, and, if you like, the things on the plan it's about.

  • About something on the plan: select a beam, post, door, window or wall, and pick Add a project note about this… at the bottom of its panel (or Add the selected items in the note). For a door or window the note is about its header; for a wall, its footing.
  • Engineer says: an engineer's note can set a size. Tick Engineer says and type what they specified (for example 3-ply 1-3/4" × 11-7/8" LVL). On its items Arc then shows their words plus (per engineer) in place of its own size: in the 3D labels, the plan label, the door and window schedules, the framing, the foundation notes and the parts list. Arc's own size stays beside it ("Arc: 2-2x10 header") so you can see the difference. Arc's structure warning on those items is quieted; their panel says Per engineer: name, date, and the Permit-set check lists them so the engineer's letter goes in with the set.
  • Show on drawings: each note has its own switch. A note that's shown prints in a General notes block in the title block of the sheets: on the cover sheet (the first sheet), or on the sheet of its item (the floor, framing or foundation plan of its level, or the door or window schedule). Notes you keep for yourself stay off the drawings.

Notes are saved with the plan and undo like any change. A plan with no notes draws, counts and warns exactly as before.

File ▾

New (a new plan, from a template, a new lot, a building on this site), Open & save (open, samples, save, print this floor plan, save as a template, My library, backup copies), Bring in a drawing (DXF, DWG, a room scan, a PDF plan, a plot plan, an aerial photo) and Settings (project settings, preferences, workspace).

Everything for checking the design and getting it built: Check the design (design check markers, project specs & guidelines, the building code summary, aging in place, lighting), Measuring an existing home, Site & yard (before you build, privacy and views, FireSmart, yard challenges), Permit drawings (permit-set check, door, window & room schedules, wall types, window and door products), Energy (NBC 9.36, heat loss, energy performance) and Notes for the builder (markups & RFIs).

Budget ▾

Everything about money and time: The budget (takeoff & cost estimate, build budget, cost room by room, options & upgrades, the takeoff as a spreadsheet), Buying (purchases & invoices, allowances, comparing a contractor's quote, shopping list), Yard (yard phases, do it or hire it), By trade (framing, electrical, plumbing, heating & cooling, assemblies and cut lists, the trim cut list), For the client (the client brief, quote, change orders, selections & allowances) and Plan the job (phase schedule, takeoff on someone else's PDF). A selected wall's panel shows its own budget too.

Export ▾

3D printing a house

Export ▾ → 3D print (STL files, plates, checks)… turns the plan into parts for a 3D printer. Pick your printer (the Bambu Lab H2D, X1/P1, A1, A1 mini, Prusa MK4 or Ender-3) and nozzle, the model and the scale. On the H2D, tick Two nozzles when a part prints in two colours with both nozzles: the bed both nozzles reach is 300 × 320 mm, smaller than the 325 × 320 mm of one nozzle, and the checks and plates follow it.

  • Floors that stack (plus the roof): each floor is its own part, and the roof lifts off. With Pegs and sockets ticked, small posts in the corners of each floor carry a peg up into a matching socket under the floor above, and under the roof. The roof gets a flat base to stand on (with its sockets), and its attic is filled in so it prints solid. Peg clearance sets how snug they are: 0.2 mm usually; 0.3 mm if your printer runs tight.
  • Dollhouse: the same, with the furniture in each floor. Tick Leave staging decor out to print the furniture without the staging and seasonal decor (vases, books, wreaths, pumpkins). One solid piece: everything as one model.

The Checks list each problem with a button to fix it:

  • Too big for the bed: the parts are measured against your printer; the fix prints at the biggest scale that fits.
  • Walls too thin: walls need to be at least about three nozzle widths (1.2 mm with a 0.4 mm nozzle). Thicken walls too thin for the nozzle fixes it for the print only; your plan keeps its walls.
  • Overhangs: eaves and other undersides steeper than 45° need supports; the check says how much and suggests tree supports. Window and door heads are short enough to bridge without them.
  • Window glass is left out (it prints as a thin skin that fails), and so are downspouts. At 1:96 the furniture in a dollhouse is very small: the check suggests 1:48.

The parts are laid out on plates that fit the bed (turned a quarter turn when that fits better), shown under the checks. Save files… saves one STL per plate, one per part (or just one kind, under Save), and a print guide PDF: the slicer settings for each part (orientation, supports, layer height, wall loops, brim), a picture of each plate, and how the parts go together. Open the plate files in Bambu Studio, PrusaSlicer or Cura. Tick Show the printer bed on the plan to see a dashed outline of the bed at the print scale while you draw. To start from something small, use File ▾ → New from a template… → 3D print: a house model.

Filament to buy, under the checks, estimates the filament for every part: the walls and top and bottom skins solid, the inside at the infill the print guide suggests, in PLA. It's totalled by colour (each shape's colour; a ground plate's base colour below its filament change and the ground cover above), with the 1 kg spools to buy, 10% extra for colour changes and reprints. Type your Price per kg for the cost, and click Add to the budget to put one line per colour in the cost estimate (Budget ▾ → Takeoff & cost estimate), under 3D printing filament; clicking it again replaces those lines. The print guide lists the grams for each part and the spools to buy. Your slicer shows the exact grams.

Plate infill (for ground plates) picks what goes inside them. Lightning, the default, only props up the top, so a plate takes about half the filament and much less time. Gyroid fills it evenly, for a sturdier plate. The estimate and the print guide follow your choice. They're matched to Bambu Studio's own slicing, within about 10%.

Organise as a kit (for 3D shapes) turns a model made of many small shapes into a kit of numbered parts. Shapes that touch, or that share a print group, print as one part: a lamp's post, globe and cap come out as one lamp. Identical parts print from one file, marked "print 6". Each part gets a number from its layer (TR-04, LT-12), the plates are sorted so each holds one main colour, and a parts list (CSV) comes with the files. A part in more than one colour also comes as a 3MF file with one piece per colour, so the slicer can give each colour its filament.

When the print files are saved, Arc seals each part's mesh: combining shapes can leave tiny gaps where pieces meet, and a slicer may read those as holes and skip layers. Every part is sealed: 3D shapes, ground plates, imported models, and a house's floors and roofs. A house part is many pieces that overlap (each wall, the floor slab, the posts and pegs, a roof's ceiling plate), so first Arc joins them into one solid in the background, then seals it. The print check says how many gaps it closed, which pieces it couldn't join (they're saved overlapping, which Bambu Studio merges), and which parts still have open edges. Should print well only shows when nothing is in the way: no warnings, no open edges and no thin spots.

3D printing an object in millimetres

For anything else (a bracket, a stand, a part that broke), use File ▾ → New from a template… → 3D print: an object in millimetres. The plan is in millimetres at full size, with the printer's bed outlined. Draw with Draw ▾ → 3D shape:

  • Rectangle: click a corner, then type the size, width by depth, like 40 x 25, and press Enter. Circle: click the centre and type the radius. Any outline: click the corners. Set the Height in the panel first, or push and pull the shape in 3D afterwards.
  • A shape drawn inside another cuts a hole straight through it.
  • Select a shape and its panel shows a 3D printing box: the printed size, and a warning if it's thinner than the nozzle can print, too big for the bed, floating above the bed, or wider at the top than the bottom (an overhang).

Then Export ▾ → 3D print… with Model set to 3D shapes I drew and model ground: each shape is its own part, checked and laid out on a plate, with the print guide. The hands-on lesson 3D printing: an object, then a house (Guide ▾ → Hands-on lessons) walks through both.

Model ground: dioramas, site models and printed landscapes

The ground tools (contour lines, spot heights, flat pads, hills and hollows, driveways and paths, water, rock and retaining walls) also shape the ground of a model: a diorama, a site model for a client, or a landscape to 3D-print. Pick Outdoor and set Draw to Ground: model base, then click the corners of the board the ground sits on. The ground then covers just the base and stops square at its edges, shaped as finely as you need. Draw two bases side by side for boards at different heights, like a table and a lower side table; the ground flows smoothly from one to the other.

  • Bottom at: the height of the board's top. Ground detail: the size of each square the ground is shaped on (empty for automatic, about 500 squares across). 1.5 to 2 mm suits a landscape for a 3D printer.
  • Base colour, and Ground cover colour starts higher up: below that height the ground shows the base colour, as a print does when it changes filament there. A path or plaza sunk below it shows the base colour. Ground cover (in any ground panel) makes the ground grass, snow, bare earth or sand.
  • Water has a Depth and can be Frozen (ice), and so can a waterfall. A driveway or path can have Heights at each bend, so it can level off and steepen like a ski run (the panel shows its steepest stretch), and a Groomed snow or Ice surface. Only lowers the ground makes a path a cut that never fills, like a lane kept clear under a chairlift.
  • Surface patterns on a flat pad or a path: cobblestones, flagstones, bricks, herringbone bricks, basketweave bricks, square slabs, hexagon pavers, wood planks, snow ripples, ice cracks, corduroy (groomed snow, running down a ski run) or moguls, with their size and groove depth. They show in 3D and are pressed into the printed plates on a finer mesh. A pad can also carry LEGO studs (4.9 mm, on an 8 mm grid), printed with the plate. Stays level over paths and walls keeps a pad flat even where a path or ski run passes close by.
  • My paving (on a model): under Surface pattern, a picture of the pad's or path's paving, and Pictures of every pattern to pick from. Besides the patterns above there are more paving looks (in the list under More paving): running bond and stack bond brick pavers, mixed-size ashlar (cut stone in three sizes), fish-scale fans of setts, cobblestones with a brick edge (on a path or a round pad), a boardwalk with gaps, log rounds and crazy paving. Change this paving… opens a window you can move, with a live picture and only the sliders that make sense for that pattern: pattern size, groove depth, joint width, flat-bottomed joints, soft edges, rounded corners, how far along each row of bricks or slabs starts (0% stack bond, 50% running bond), gaps between boards, and a brick edge. It says what you might have missed: a joint narrower than about 0.5 mm won't show with a 0.4 mm nozzle (make it 0.6 mm or more), and a groove shallower than one 0.2 mm layer may not show at all. Save as My paving… keeps it with a name, in the plan and in My library for your other plans and editions (your paving from another edition shows marked ⤵ and is copied here when picked); Reset puts the pattern back as Arc ships it. The paving keeps its pattern's name, so joined paths still count it as paved, and an older Arc shows the pattern it was made from.
  • Rock shows on slopes over (in the model base panel): where the ground is steeper than this angle, bare rock shows through the snow or grass, in the Rock colour. About 45° looks like real mountains. It colours the 3D view and renders only; to print rock, add rock ledges.
  • On a model, rock ledges are sized to the model and drawn in a plain colour you can set. Retaining walls in stacked stone or fieldstone, and walls drawn with the Rock tool, are real rock: see Rock.
  • Print plates: 3D printing cuts the ground into plates. Type the Column widths and Row depths (the last ones take what's left), a Plate names start and Row names (like F, R for front and rear), or leave them empty to split the base evenly to fit the printer. Puzzle tabs lock neighbouring plates together, with a little clearance in each pocket.
  • A 3D shape's panel has When 3D printing: print it on its own, join it to the ground plates (studs, ledges), cut it out of the plates (holes, sockets, wiring channels underneath; shown as a faint red ghost in 3D), or leave it out (for show only). Cuts count even when their layer is hidden.

Export ▾ → 3D print… with The ground, as plates ticked adds a part for each plate, named on the plan, checked against the printer like any other part (with Patios with their paving pressed in, each patio's herringbone, bond, hexagons, flagstones, cut stone, slabs or circle kit is pressed into the plate as 0.6 mm joints at any scale; pavers too small to print at the scale are drawn bigger; exposed aggregate, asphalt, grass pavers and stamped wood print flat), with the filament change height in the print guide. A plate taller than the printer prints as stacked layers (C1R layer 1, C1R layer 2…): each layer above sits on pegs on the one below, with sockets underneath, so the ground can be as tall as you like.

A 3D shape can Glow (a light): a lantern, a lit window or a string of fairy lights. It glows in the 3D view, and an Evening (lights on) render turns each one into a real light.

Shape the ground by hand in 3D, and draw a road on it

You don't have to draw contour lines to shape a model's ground. In the 3D view, click Sculpt ground (or, with the Outdoor tool set to Ground: model base, Sculpt the ground in 3D… in the panel). Then drag on the ground of the model base. A ring shows the brush.

  • Raise pulls the ground up where you drag. Lower pushes it down. Drag back and forth to build a hill or dig a valley.
  • Smooth softens bumps and sharp edges. Flatten levels the ground to the height where you first pressed: press on a spot that is the right height, then drag across what should match it. Or type a height in Flatten to and it levels to that.
  • Grab drags the ground under the brush sideways as one piece, like pulling a bank along. Hold Shift and drag up or down to lift or sink it instead.
  • Ridge raises a ridge along your stroke, as wide as the brush: a snow drift or a bank. Gully cuts one: a creek bed or an avalanche chute. Height sets how tall or deep (empty: a quarter of the brush size).
  • Hill and Hollow stamp a round hill or hollow the size of the brush with one click.
  • Freeze paints areas the other brushes may not touch, shown as blue dots: a road, a plaza, a turntable socket. Shift thaws. Freeze what's drawn freezes every pad, path, water and ground shape on the plan in one go; Thaw all clears it. Frozen areas are saved with the plan.
  • Roughen adds small random bumps and dips, like rough ground or drifted snow. Terrace steps the ground into level bands, using the Terrace step height.
  • Mirror copies every stroke across the middle of the base, left–right or front–back, so one side of a hill shapes the other. Stamps, ridges and grabs mirror too; a fainter ring shows where the copy lands.
  • Size and Strength set how wide the brush is and how much each drag does. Falloff is how the brush fades to its edge: soft, round (a dome) or sharp (full strength to near the edge). [ and ] make the brush smaller or bigger. Holding Shift does the opposite: Raise lowers, Smooth roughens.
  • Contours every draws lines of equal height over the ground while you work, so you can see the shape. Set it to 0 to hide them. Colour by height paints the ground blue (low) to red (high) while the brush is out, and the status line reads out the ground's height under the pointer.
  • Smooth all softens the whole base once. Clear sculpting takes all the hand shaping off (the contours, pads and paths drawn on the plan stay).
  • Ground from a picture… (in the model base's panel, beside Sculpt the ground in 3D…) shapes the ground from a greyscale picture (PNG or JPG) stretched over the base, its top edge along the back of the plan: white is the highest ground, black the lowest, and you type the height of white. It replaces the sculpting; Ctrl+Z takes it back.
  • On the plan, a sculpted base shows its ground as faint purple contour lines, at the Contours every step (or about 60 across the base). Show the sculpted ground as contour lines on the plan in the base's panel turns them off.

Each drag is one undo step: Ctrl+Z takes it back. Drag off the base to turn the view, or right-drag to slide it. Esc or Done puts the brush away. The shaping is saved with the plan, and the printed plates follow it like any other ground. Pads, roads and plazas drawn on the plan still win where they are: sculpt the hillside, and the road stays level. After the first drag Arc keeps the solved ground and only lays the sculpting and what's drawn on the plan again, so later drags on a big, fine base are quicker; if it still lags, set its Ground detail coarser while you rough out the shape, then finer at the end. The ground Arc solves from contour lines keeps faint steps between the lines, which print like a topographic map. To soften them, set Smooth the ground in the model base's panel (next to Ground detail) to a number of passes: 0 leaves the ground as drawn, 20 or so takes the steps out, more makes it smoother still. Pads and paths are levelled afterwards, so they stay flat.

To put a road on the hill, click Road on ground in the 3D view (or Draw a road on the 3D ground… in the Outdoor panel). Set its Width, Surface and, on a model, the Pattern (cobblestones, planks, groomed snow…), then click points along the middle of the road on the ground. Each point takes the ground's height where you clicked, so the road follows the slope; an orange ribbon shows it. Backspace takes the last point off, and the panel reads out the steepest stretch between your points as you go. Enter, a double-click or Finish makes the road. It is an ordinary driveway or path: select it to change its width, surface, pattern or the height at each bend, make it a printed insert, or tick Smooth curve.

Fine snow and stone on printed ground

A printer lays plastic in layers, usually 0.2 mm tall, so a slope prints as little steps 0.2 mm ÷ its steepness apart: about 5.7 mm apart at 2°, 2.3 mm at 5°, 0.9 mm at 12°. On gentle snow those steps look like the contour lines on a map. Snow prints best dead flat, or steeper than about 12°, with its texture made of small crisp forms one to four layers tall. These tools do that, on a model base, and line it all up across the plates' seams (everything is worked out from its place on the whole board). Each one is off until you turn it on: a plan without them prints exactly as before.

  • Print detail (the model base's panel, under Fine snow and stone): Automatic uses a fine mesh, about 0.27 mm, only where the ground has detail (a pattern, a snow form, a bench edge), as soon as the base uses any of these tools; plain snow stays on the ground's own grid, so the files stay about the size of a textured plate before, or smaller. Finer is about 0.23 mm (bigger files); Standard is as before.
  • Flat ground on whole layers sets nearly flat ground (under 1°) to an exact layer, so its top is one clean layer. Benches on gentle slopes turns 1° to 12° slopes into flat steps 0.8 mm tall joined by short 30–45° faces, their edges wandering across the slope, so the layer steps read as wind-packed slab edges. Paths keep their own slope.
  • Wind blows toward sets the way the wind crosses the board, in degrees on the plan (0 to the right, 90 up the page). Drifts, scour hollows, drift tails, tree wells and wind ripples face it, and the Bambu Studio project runs each plate's top lines along it, so the sheen reads as wind grain and matches from plate to plate.
  • Deep snow (round mounds all over): a thick blanket of soft round scoops over the whole base, like the storybook village: mounds about Mound size across (15 mm if empty) and up to Mound height tall (3.2 mm), with a layer of smaller scoops on top, sizes as printed. Covers less than 100% leaves it in soft patches about 80 mm across. Paths, pads, ponds, rock ledges and the shapes on the base stay clear, the snow rising from each edge over 1.5 to 9 mm, so sets, sockets and roads still sit level; sockets, channels, tabs and inserts are still cut from the plates. The mounds carry on across the plates' seams. The plates print it on a mesh of about 0.5 mm (about twice the triangles of a plain plate); with fine patterns on the same base, the whole base goes to the fine 0.27 mm mesh, which makes much bigger files.
  • Patterns for pads and paths (the Surface pattern list): Wind ripples (a sawtooth one layer tall, 3 to 5 mm apart, crests wandering, in patches; Ripples cover sets how much: about 15–35% looks natural), Sastrugi (wind-carved ridges 0.6 to 1 mm tall with a blunt nose into the wind), Flagstones in rectangles and Herringbone bricks (2:1 brick pavers at 45° to the way; Pattern size is a brick's length, so 6.8 mm makes 6.8 × 3.4 mm bricks; flat-bottomed joints Joint width wide, 0.7 mm if empty; on a path it follows the path's direction, so the bricks stay at 45° to the curb round a bend), Herringbone bricks (square to the edge) (the same bricks running along and across the path), Basketweave bricks (two bricks side by side in each square, turning from square to square), Square slabs (a sidewalk's even grid; Pattern size is a slab's side), Hexagon pavers (Pattern size is across the flats), Pillow flagstones (big sidewalk stones in rows; Pattern size is a row's depth, each stone 9/8 to twice that long, corners rounded 1.2 mm, joints 0.8 mm if empty, a fifth of the stones a layer low and a tenth split by a crack) and, on a pad, Bricks in rings (rings round the pad's middle a half brick and a joint apart, bricks a brick long round each ring, every other ring half a brick round; on a round pad the rings are fitted between its edges so no thin ring is left over). These pavers all have flat-bottomed joints Joint width wide. On a pad, Middle across and Middle up move the middle the rings go round (it starts at the pad's own), and Inner edge radius stops the pattern round an island or a hole in the middle. Bricks and slabs can have Round the corners (0.5 mm), as worn pavers look. On a path of stones or pavers, Soldier border along the edges adds a row of bricks laid across the path along each edge, one Pattern size deep, with a joint between it and the rest, and Border across the ends lays one across its start, its end or both: a threshold where the path meets a square or a round pad (past that end the path adds no pattern, so the pad's own shows). A round pad of stones or pavers can have Soldier border round the edge: a ring of bricks pointing to its middle round its edge, and round its inner edge too. Border one layer proud stands any of these borders one 0.2 mm layer above the rest, like a low curb. Stone patterns (cobbles, setts, flagstones, bricks and the pavers) can have Flat-bottomed joints (at least 0.8 mm wide, two nozzle widths, so the slicer keeps them open where a V joint closes up) and Some stones one layer lower, which looks hand-laid.
  • Paving that joins and edges smoothly (on a model base). A paved path or pad (a stone or paver pattern, or a paved surface) whose sides slope over less than two ground squares, such as a sidewalk raised 1.4 mm with Sides slope over 0.5 mm, is a curb: Arc works its edge out exactly instead of on the ground's grid, so the 3D view and the printed plates show a clean curb that follows the outline, in the paving's colour, with no saw-teeth and no snow on the paving. Edges sets it (Automatic, Crisp or Soft, as before). Paved paths and pads at the same height that touch are one surface: One surface with paving it meets at its height (on unless you untick it) rounds the inside corners where they meet, about a fifth of the narrower one's width, or the Corner radius you give. Where they meet, one pattern shows, the one drawn last. On any path, Width at the end tapers it from start to end, and Widen to meet the path it starts on (or ends on) makes it as wide as that path where they meet, narrowing to its own width over the length you give: a narrow road leaving a wide street. Ease into and out of its climbs rounds the path's height where its slope changes, over that length, so a climb leaves a level street without a kink; its ends keep their heights (the middle gets a little steeper to make up for it).
  • Sculpted surface (the model base's panel, at the end of Fine snow and stone): Bring in a sculpted surface… takes a surface shaped somewhere else, such as Blender, and prints the plates from it. A mesh (STL, GLB or OBJ) gives its top as seen from above; its heights are the ground's, in mm above the base's bottom, placed by its own x and y from the base's front left corner. A height picture (PNG or JPG) is added to the ground: white is 10 mm up, black nothing, spread over the base. Arc keeps it inside the plan as heights every 0.5 mm (about 1 MB for a 400 × 300 mm board). Where the surface has a hole, Arc's own ground shows, so leave holes where Arc's paving, pads or sets should show. Its heights switches between the two ways; Keep paths, paved pads and shapes clear (added heights only) lets it rise from their edges as deep snow does; From the base's left edge, From the base's front edge and Raise it by move it; Heights times makes it taller or flatter; Remove the surface takes it off. The plates follow it on a mesh as fine as its heights, then cut every socket, channel, tab, peg and insert from it as always, so they all stay. The print check warns where the ground is less than 1 mm thick over a channel cut inside a plate. The 3D view shows it a little coarser than it prints; Relief preview… shows a plate as it will print.
  • Snow drifts and tree wells: the Outdoor tool's Ground: snow drift or tree well, one click on the base. A drift is a crescent across the wind: a long back at about 14°, a steep 45° lee and a crisp crest, its horns trailing downwind, in whole layers. Cornice lip on the crest adds a lip up to 1.5 mm past the crest with a 45° underside and a tip at least 0.8 mm thick, so it prints with no supports. A tree well is a dish 6 mm wider than the trunk, about 1 mm deep, with a low rim on the lee side. Select one to change its size; move or delete it like any area.
  • Snow round it (a 3D shape's or a rock ledge's panel): Scour hollow and drift tail digs a moat 0.6 to 1 mm deep on the windward side and lays a tail behind, 3 to 5 times as long as the shape is wide; Keep clear within stops anything rising near its middle, like a turntable tray's sweep. Tree well puts a well round a printed tree.
  • Plow bank (a path's panel, on a model): a chunky bank beside the road, its road face about 50° with clods and notches, soft behind, its top wandering 2.5 to 4.5 mm, with a gap where a walk crosses. Packed snow on it lays snow 0.6 mm thick along the edges and in a broken strip down the middle, leaving the wheel ruts bare. Above the colour change it all prints white with no extra swap.
  • Matte white on the drifts: a second filament change above each plate's flat snow, so every drift, bank top and drift tail prints matte with one swap. Matte starts at empty works out each plate's flat snow by itself. In the 3D Print workspace, No matte on plates leaves it off steep plates, where a height split would draw a hard line across the slope (the print check names them).
  • Ledges for snow (the Rock tool's panel, on a model): about 5 to 8 real ledges every 300 mm, at different heights, each with a snow pillow printed in white on the second nozzle, at least 1.2 mm thick with its front leaning back, so no supports.

The print check names each wide patch of gentle slope ("L2F: 120 × 300 mm at 2.4° (lines about 4.8 mm apart)") and says how big the fine files are. The Bambu Studio project comes ready to print: each ground plate has its filament changes set (the ground cover colour, then matte white), with Bambu's own PLA Basic and PLA Matte profiles for the H2D, and the top lines along the wind. Relief preview… in the 3D Print workspace shows one plate as it will print, from above under a low sun: its real print mesh cut into layers, in its filament colours (matte shown a little warmer). Lower the sun to see one-layer detail; click the picture to look closer.

The 3D Print workspace

The 3D Print button in the top bar (or Export ▾ → 3D Print workspace…) gathers every printing tool in the side panel, in the order you use them. The plan and the 3D view stay as they are, and the sections follow what you select on the plan. ← Back to the plan closes it. Nothing here is a new setting: each control is the same one the panels and the 3D print window use.

  1. Printer and bed: the printer, nozzle, layer height (automatic by scale, or the one you print at: it goes into the print guide and the filament estimate), scale and model, and the bed outline on the plan. Save a fit test… saves a small plate to print before a big model: a puzzle tab and its pocket, a peg with three sockets (0.1, 0.2 and 0.3 mm clearance, marked with 1, 2 and 3 dots), and a step at your colour-change height. Its read me says what to check and which setting to change.
  2. Model ground and plates: Start a model base if there isn't one, then the base's settings (bottom, detail, colours, the plate columns, rows, names and puzzle tabs). With Ground cover colour starts higher up ticked, it says exactly which layer to change filament at in the slicer. Under it, the plate infill and a plate map with every plate's name (inserts hatched, holes cut through the plates in red), which you can save as a picture.
  3. Surfaces: the surface pattern of the selected pad or path, with its size and depth, and for cobblestones and flagstones the joint width and (cobblestones) soft edges, for pillowy stones. Select a model base to see its side relief, if a script gave it some.
  4. Inserts: see below.
  5. Parts and numbers: for the selected shapes, the part name, Print together as and the layer's part number letters. Make the parts list builds every part as the print files would and lists its number, name, how many to print, colours, size, grams, what the filament costs (the $ column: grams × how many × the Filament price a kilogram, set under the list) and which bed plate it's on; click a line to select its shapes; Save as CSV keeps it. Numbers are given automatically each time, in order, from each layer's letters.
  6. Check and export: Check and save files… opens the 3D print window (the checks with their fixes, the filament to buy and the print time with Add to the budget, the plates, Save files…). To make one thing again, tick plates under Just some plates and Save just these plates…, or select the shapes of a part and Save the selected parts…: only those are built, with their usual numbers and a print guide.
  7. What to watch out for: quick notes, without building anything, on what's selected (or the whole plan): a shape that crosses a plate seam, a puzzle tab under it, a shape standing in an insert's pocket, something that starts or ends right at the colour-change height, and anything too big for the bed. The print check looks closer.
  8. Mesh doctor and collisions: warning colours on the model, open edges in red, and parts that overlap. See below.

Above the sections, Print it for me does it all in one go: see below.

Learn it hands-on: Guide ▾ → Hands-on lessons has three short lessons on a practice landscape (also File ▾ → New from a template… → 3D print: a small landscape): plates and the colour change, surfaces and printed inserts (including changing the snow), and parts and part numbers. Each one ticks off its steps as you do them.

Print it for me

The Print it for me button, at the top of the 3D Print workspace (also Export ▾ → Print it for me…, the 3D print window, and Ctrl+K), does the whole job in one go, for someone who just wants it printed:

  • Best side down. Each 3D shape and imported model is turned so it needs the least support, keeps a good footing on the bed and isn't needlessly tall: a flowerpot that flares out at the top prints upside down, a part with a big flat side lies on it. A part you've turned yourself (Print orientation), one that stands up to print, or one that pauses for magnets is left as it is.
  • Too big for the bed? It's split into pieces that fit, joined with printed pegs (or filament pins where a cut is too small for pegs), as Print in pieces does. A house too big for the bed is printed at the scale that fits.
  • The plates are filled, and the filaments are matched to the ones loaded in your H2D (see below).

It ends with a plain checklist: Load red PLA on the right nozzle. White PLA is loaded on the left nozzle already. 2 plates, about 3 h 10 min., then what Arc turned or split, any pauses for magnets, the hardware to have ready and any warning still left. Save the Bambu Studio project… saves it, and on the desktop Open in Bambu Studio opens it there, so there's no hunting for the file. In Studio, check each filament matches its spool and press Slice all.

The filaments in your H2D, and Open in Bambu Studio

File ▾ → Preferences → 3D printing lists the filaments loaded in your Bambu H2D: for each, the nozzle it feeds (left or right), the kind (PLA Basic, PLA Matte or TPU 95A) and its colour. Add a filament adds one; Remove takes one off. Print it for me asks the first time if the list is empty, then stops asking. Each colour a print needs is matched to the nearest loaded one of the same kind; one that isn't loaded goes on the nozzle with fewer, and the checklist says to load it. TPU always goes on the right nozzle, from the spool at the back. The Bambu Studio project (section 6) follows the list too: with filaments on both nozzles, the parts are laid out where both nozzles reach (300 × 320 mm). With an empty list, everything goes on the right nozzle as before.

On the desktop, after Save a Bambu Studio project… in section 6, an Open in Bambu Studio button opens the project you just saved in Bambu Studio (or whatever opens .3mf files on this computer, if Studio isn't in the usual place). It only ever opens a project Arc has just saved.

Printing imported models

A model imported with File ▾ → Import (STL, 3MF, GLB, OBJ and the rest) prints as a part of its own when the model is 3D shapes I drew and model ground: as it sits on the plan, its lowest point on the bed, sealed and checked like any other part. A very fine mesh (over 200,000 triangles, a 3D scan say) is made coarser when it's imported, which keeps the plan quick and prints the same; the import says so. To print it at full detail anyway (a sculpt made in Blender, say), tick Keep full detail for printing when you import it: the plan and 3D view show the lighter copy, and the print files use the full mesh (the plan file grows by its size). Anything on the plan that doesn't print this way gets a note in the print check, so nothing is left out without a word: blocks from the library (they print in a dollhouse), shapes set not to print, and bought machine parts.

Warning colours, the mesh doctor and collisions

Section 8. Mesh doctor and collisions of the 3D Print workspace looks at the selected shapes and models, or all of them when nothing is selected:

  • Show print warnings on the model paints the 3D view: red where a part hangs out steeper than 45° (it needs supports, or turning over), amber where it's thinner than two lines of the nozzle (0.8 mm with a 0.4 mm nozzle). The thickness is measured for real, by a ray from each face straight through the part, so a thin fin on a fat part is caught; the print check warns about it too, with the thinnest spot. The colours follow your changes while it's on.
  • Mesh doctor: Check for open edges draws each part's open edges as red lines in 3D and lists how many each has. A part is watertight when every edge is shared by two faces, like a balloon; an open edge is the rim of a hole, where a slicer can't tell inside from outside. Imported models are checked as they are, and Patch the holes fills them (Undo takes it back) and checks again. Arc's own shapes are closed when they print, so for them it only shows what Arc couldn't close. The lesson 3D Print workspace: the mesh doctor goes through it with a broken cup.
  • Do these parts collide?: Check for collisions finds parts that overlap (two gears that cut into each other, a shaft too fat for its hole) and says by how much: Gear and Housing overlap by 4 cm³ here, with the overlap shown in red in 3D. Show selects the two parts and shows just that overlap. Parts with the same Print together as are one part, so they don't count. Parts that slide or push together need a gap, not an overlap: 0.1, 0.2 and 0.3 mm are the fit test's three, and 0.2 mm suits most printers.

Vase mode and drain holes

A 3D shape's panel, under its print settings, has Vase mode: one wall going round and up in one go, with a solid bottom and no top, for a vase, a pot or a lampshade. It's quick, light and smooth. The part goes on a plate of its own, and the Bambu Studio project turns on Studio's spiral vase mode for that plate (one wall, no top, no infill). Leave the shape solid: vase mode prints only its outside wall anyway.

A hollowed shape (Hollow it out) has Drain hole: a round hole 5, 8, 12 or 20 mm across through the middle of its floor, so a planter drains. It shows in 3D and prints with the shape, and moves with it.

Plates changed since their files were saved

When you save plate files (from the 3D print window, Save just these plates… or the Bambu Studio project), Arc remembers what each plate looked like. Afterwards the plate map and the Just some plates list mark any plate that has changed (orange) since, and any plate that is new (blue); section 2 says when the files were last saved. Tick the changed ticks them all, ready for Save just these plates…, so you only print again what you have to. A plate changes when its base, the ground on it (pads, paths, inserts, shapes that join or cut), the terrain, the ground cover or the print settings change.

Draw on the sides of a model base

With a model base selected, section 3. Surfaces has Draw on the sides…: the base's upright sides unrolled flat, left to right the way round the base from its first corner, with the ground line at the bottom and the colour change shown. Place snow drifts along the bottom, snowflakes and dots higher up (the Size slider sets how big), or Draw a shape by clicking or tapping its corners, then tapping twice in the same spot (or Close the shape) to finish. A stamp always stays on the side: one bigger than the side there shrinks to fit. Take one off removes a shape you click or tap; Undo last and Clear do what they say, and the plan's Undo takes each step back too. Choose Raised off the side (printed in its own colour by a second nozzle) or Engraved into the side, and how far. The shapes show in 3D at once and print on the plates.

A Bambu Studio project

Section 6. Check and export → Save a Bambu Studio project… writes one .3mf project: every plate as a build plate with its parts laid out where Arc put them, each part on the extruder that matches its filament colour, and the layer height, wall loops and infill in the project settings. For the H2D with a 0.4 nozzle it carries Bambu's complete 0.20 mm Standard settings, with PLA Matte for matte filaments, PLA Basic for the rest, and every filament on the right nozzle. Open it by itself, in a newly started Bambu Studio or after File → New Project, because opened on top of another project it can warn about an invalid config and keep only the shapes. Check that the filaments match the spools you have (it matches them by colour), and slice. With the filaments in your H2D listed in Preferences, each colour goes on the nozzle that has it loaded, and on the desktop Open in Bambu Studio opens the project you just saved. With other printers and nozzles, set the print settings in Bambu Studio yourself. The plate and part files save as before; the project is an extra.

Lettering on a 3D shape

Select a 3D shape and type a word or number under Lettering in its panel: a sign, a house number, a name on a base. Choose Raised off the shape or Cut into the shape, The top or The front (the side facing the street), one of the fonts (plain, rounded, serif, classic serif, the scripts Grand Hotel, Brusher and Pacifico, and the playful Mouse Memoirs) or a font file of your own (Add a font file…: a .ttf or .otf, kept on this device only, never in Arc's installer, web copy or backup), the letter height and how far the letters stand proud or are cut in, a turn for letters on the top, Italic to lean them over, and a colour: for raised letters their own colour, for cut letters an inlay colour (the letters are cut in and filled with letters in that colour, flush with the surface, like a name plate). The letters show in 3D straight away and print with the shape: raised letters and inlays in their colour as their own piece for a second nozzle, plain cut letters taken out of the shape. Keep cut letters at least two nozzle widths wide so they print cleanly.

How each part prints

A 3D shape's panel also keeps Print orientation (as drawn, or upside down with its top on the bed) and Supports (let Arc suggest, none, tree supports on the build plate only, or normal supports everywhere). The print check, the print guide PDF and the parts list follow what you set, so a part you have printed once prints the same way next time.

Below those, a part can have its own slicer settings: Filament (PLA, or TPU 95A, the flexible one, for grips, pads and tyres), Walls (2 to 8; 6 for strong parts like bases and hubs), Infill (10% to 100%; 100% is solid, so light glows through evenly), Brim (none, or 5 mm outside only for tall or thin parts) and Fuzzy skin (on the outside walls, a rough finish like stone or bark). Left blank, a part uses the project's settings. The Bambu Studio project writes them on each part, so Studio opens it with them already set; the print guide PDF lists them and the filament estimate counts them. TPU parts go on plates of their own. On the H2D, TPU uses Bambu's TPU 95A profile on the right nozzle (Studio won't slice TPU on the left), and a project with only TPU in it prints at 25 mm/s. Feed TPU from the external spool, not the AMS.

Turned shapes, ropes and rails, and gears

The Shape tool's Draw list has three more ways to make a 3D shape, each still an ordinary shape on the plan (it moves, prints and takes lettering like any other):

  • Turned shape (centre, then size) — a half profile spun round the middle, like work on a lathe: a vase, a bowl, a finial for a post, a roller with a ball groove, a ball. Pick a preset in the panel before you click, or afterwards choose Edit the profile… and click the corners of your own half profile, from the bottom up (the other half is mirrored as you go). The profile is kept as a proportion, so making the shape wider or taller keeps its form.
  • Rope or rail along a path — a round or square section run along the points you click: a rope, a cable, a pipe, a rail, a moulding, a curb. Enter or a double-click finishes the path; clicking the first point again closes it into a loop (a ring, a track). Section and Thickness (printed millimetres) are in the panel, before and after.
  • Gear (click the centre) — an involute spur gear with a hole for its shaft: Teeth, Tooth size (the module, in printed millimetres: 2 mm is a sturdy printed gear, 1 mm a fine one), Bore and Thickness. Two gears mesh when their modules match: with a gear selected, Add a matching pinion… makes a second one to its right, meshing, at the right centre distance (the panel says how far apart their centres are).

Sizes for gears, ropes and rails are in printed millimetres at the print scale (the 3D print settings), so what you type is what prints.

My printed parts

In the 3D Print workspace, section 5, select the shapes of a part you will want again (a tree, a lamp post, a turntable, your inlaid name plate) and Save selected shapes to my parts…. They are kept on this device, with their colours, lettering and print settings, and listed under My printed parts in every plan: Add to the plan puts a copy in the middle of the view on the current level, selected, so you press M to move it where it goes. Remove takes one out of the list. Like My fonts, the library lives on this device only, never in a plan file, the installer or the backup.

A print kit for someone else

Section 6 has Save a client print kit…: one folder with everything a print shop or a friend needs. The plate and part files are numbered in the order to print them; READ ME FIRST.txt says, in plain words, what is in the folder, the printer, scale, nozzle and layer height, how each part goes (orientation, supports, walls, colours, size, grams and time) and the filament to buy; the parts list, the plate map and the print guide come too. Only the kit name, who it is for and the note you type go in from your side, nothing else from the plan.

Plates built in the background

The ground plates (the slow part of a print with cobbles and patterns) are now built in the background: Arc stays usable while they build, the status shows Building the plates: 3 of 20 (L2F)…, and a Cancel button beside it stops the build. This applies in the 3D print window and to every save in the workspace (plates, the Bambu Studio project, the client kit). The plates come out exactly as before.

One file per insert, and its own guide page

With a printed insert (section 4), the files now include Pad name insert (all pieces).3mf: every piece of the insert laid out as it sits in the pocket, one object per colour, so the whole insert slices together. The print guide gets a page for each insert: its pieces with their sizes, colours, grams and time, and how to fit it. In the Inserts section each piece's line shows its size and about how many grams and how long it takes, with a total for the insert.

Printed inserts

A printed insert is a flat pad that becomes its own part: a pocket in the ground plates with a separate piece that drops in, with stones or tiles on top. Select a flat pad on a model base (draw one with Outdoor → Ground: flat pad), open the workspace's Inserts section (the pad's own panel has a Make it a printed insert… button that takes you there), pick the stones or tiles and click Make it a printed insert. The pad drops by the pocket depth (3 mm) to become the pocket's floor, so the finished surface stays where it was, and its pressed-in pattern comes off (the insert has its own stones).

  • Stones or tiles: soft cobblestones (rounded stones like the cobblestone pattern, each with a smaller rounded top), cobble setts, square tiles on the diagonal, basketweave, herringbone or long tiles in rows, with their size, height and joint width, and soft edges for cobblestones. The base under them has its own colour.
  • Snow on the stones: drag the slider (or type a percentage) for how many stones get a thin white cap of snow, in drifting patches, more toward the edges and fewer where people walk round a socket. The insert is made again as soon as you let go. Snow cap height and Snow colour go with it.
  • A hole is left wherever a shape cut from the plates (a turntable socket) passes through the insert, with a little clearance, and the stones are cut back round it.
  • Split into: as few pieces as fit the bed, or one, two or four. When there is one hole, the pieces are split through its middle, so each has a bite out of its corner rather than a hole in its middle. Each piece has a 0.3 mm gap to its neighbour and 0.25 mm clearance to the pocket.
  • Only where the ground is the pocket's floor: where something else (a street) lowers the ground inside the pad, the insert stops short of it.
  • What was made says how many pieces, stones and snowy stones there are, and each piece's size and whether it fits the bed. Make the insert again rebuilds it; Remove the insert takes it away and puts the pad back up.

The pieces are 3D shapes in the plan (on the pad's layer), one print group per piece, so they print as kit parts: each is one numbered part, in two colours when it has snow (its 3MF file has a piece per colour: give the snow its own filament in the slicer). Move, resize or lower the pad, or change any setting, and the insert is made again by itself. Undo works as usual.

Joining printed pieces

Arc has several ways to join printed pieces. Ground plates lock with puzzle tabs or dovetails (select the model base and pick Shape of the joins under Print plates), stacked floors sit on pegs, and path inserts push onto pegs. For any 3D shape there are two more:

  • Print in pieces (in a shape's panel, under 3D printing): As many as fit the bed, or two to six pieces, cut the long way, left to right, front to back or across its height. Pick what each cut is Joined with: printed pegs (diamond-shaped on a side cut, so they print without supports), magnets (6 × 3 or 10 × 3 mm, glued into open pockets or sealed inside while the print pauses), steel dowel pins (3 × 12 mm, a 3.1 mm hole to press into and a 3.2 mm hole to slide into), filament pins (2.0 mm holes for a piece of 1.75 mm filament), keyed pins (printed D-shaped pins, so the pieces only go together one way) or dovetail slides (side cuts only). Arc finds room on each cut face, keeping 1.2 mm of plastic round each hole, and the print check says how the pieces go together and if a cut face is too small. The plan isn't changed: the shape is only cut for the print. Good for a mini golf hole longer than the bed, a big model base or a long frame.
