  • Hole for hardware (Shape tool, Draw): click on a shape to cut a magnet pocket, an M3 or M2 heat-set insert hole, an M3 nut trap (with its screw hole through), an M3 screw hole, or a hole for a steel or filament pin into its top or bottom. Use it for a roof that lifts off on magnets, or a motor mount held by screws.

The sizes are printed millimetres, the same at any scale, with fits for a 0.4 mm nozzle on the Bambu H2D. The fit test in the 3D Print workspace now has a second plate that tries each one first. The print check and the read me list the hardware to buy, with starting prices from Canadian suppliers first (Lee Valley, Princess Auto, Spool3D, Fastenal Canada), then US and UK ones. Magnets: stack them all in one column and mark the top face; on each cut, the piece before it gets the marked face toward the cut and the piece after it the marked face away, so every pair pulls together.

Paths as inserts (a ski run in another colour)

A driveway or path on a model base, a ski run say, can print as pieces in another colour that push into a recess in the ground plates. Select the path and tick Print it as an insert in another colour in its panel, then pick the Insert colour. In the 3D view the path shows in that colour.

  • The plates get a recess the shape of the path, 4 to 11 mm deep (deeper where a flat piece sits on sloping ground), with pegs 4 mm wide standing up from its floor.
  • The pieces are cut at the plates' edges and about every 120 mm along the path. Each has the path's own surface on top (its pattern, corduroy say, comes with it) and a ribbed hole over each peg underneath: three thin ribs squeeze the peg a little, so it holds without glue and still lifts out. Where a shape is cut from the plate (a light pipe), the piece has a hole too.
  • How each piece prints: where the ground under it is near enough flat, its underside is one flat face and the file is already turned to lie on it, so it needs no supports and its top prints level and smooth. Where the ground changes too much (a bend in a steep run), its underside follows the ground instead, about 4.5 mm thick all over, and it prints as it sits with tree supports underneath (hidden once it's in). The print guide says which is which.
  • Slivers: where a path only just clips a plate's edge, the thin strip is left in the plate's colour (too thin to print) and the print check says so. So does a piece left out because its recess would cut too deep into the plate.
  • Not yet: a path that runs off the model base, or two inserted paths that cross. The print check warns, and leaves those pieces out.
  • Print one piece and its plate first to check the fit on your printer.

Taking a drawing to AutoCAD

Export ▾ → DXF saves the plan as a 2D drawing that every version of AutoCAD opens, and so do DraftSight, LibreCAD and SketchUp Pro. Choose this level or all levels (each level gets its own layers), and whether to include wall dimensions and floor patterns. Export ▾ → DWG saves AutoCAD's own format for the version you pick; Arc makes it with the free ODA File Converter, which you install once (Arc offers the download page the first time).

When it opens in AutoCAD:

  • It's in model space at full size: 1 unit is 1 inch (1 millimetre for a metric plan). If the screen looks empty, type ZOOM, Enter, E (Extents).
  • Type UNITS and check that Insertion scale says Inches (or Millimeters), so blocks and xrefs you add land at the right size.
  • Lines are on standard layers: A-WALL, A-DOOR, A-FURN, A-FLOR-STRS, A-ROOF, A-ANNO-DIMS, A-ANNO-TEXT and so on, ready for your own layer standards.
  • Dimensions and labels arrive as lines and text, not live dimensions: re-dimension in AutoCAD where you need them to update. Walls are outlines you can hatch or offset. Floor patterns add many lines, so they're off unless you tick them.

To print the drawings, Arc's own Sheets already have title blocks and scales; save them as a PDF.

Taking a model to SketchUp

Export ▾ → Collada .dae (SketchUp) saves the whole 3D model: walls, floors, roof, doors, windows, stairs and furniture, in inches with Z up, so it arrives at the right size and the right way up. In SketchUp:

  • File → Import, set the file type to COLLADA (*.dae) and pick the file. Under Options, tick Merge coplanar faces for a cleaner model.
  • It arrives as one component at the origin. Double-click into it, or right-click → Explode, to edit. Inside there's a group for each floor, and in it a group for each trade (walls, siding & trim, doors & windows, roofing, cabinets…); each piece is named by its floor, room and wall, so you can move them or swap them for your own. Framing, footings and other repeated pieces come across as every copy, each in its place.
  • Colours come across as SketchUp materials named by their finish ("Brick_red", "Siding_lap_gray"); photo textures come across as their average colour.

For a flat plan to trace or push and pull, export DXF and use File → Import in SketchUp Pro (AutoCAD files). For Blender, Unreal or Twinmotion use Export ▾ → glTF .glb, which keeps the photo textures.

Sending the model to Blender

Export ▾ → glTF .glb (and Collada) is made for finishing pictures in Blender:

  • Groups per floor and trade. Each floor is a group, and in it a group per trade: Foundation, Walls, Drywall & paint, Siding & trim, Doors & windows, Roofing, Floors, Ceilings, Finish carpentry, Stairs, Cabinets & built-ins, Plumbing fixtures, Fireplaces, Furniture & decor, Porches & decks, Site & landscape.
  • Names that stay the same. Pieces are named by floor, room and wall ("Main_floor_Wall_S_3", "Main_floor_Kitchen_base-cab_2"), and materials by their finish ("Siding_lap_gray", "Brick_red", "Drywall_painted", "Roof_asphalt_charcoal", "Metal_flashing"). The same plan gives the same names every time, so a material you set up in Blender (a real brick, a scanned oak) is still on the right pieces when you export again: in Blender, File → Link or Append your materials, or use the same names.
  • Its data. Each piece carries its wall type, thickness and height, outside finish, paint, room, trade and Arc id. Blender shows them under Object Properties → Custom Properties.
  • The small real pieces: sloped sills and drip caps at the windows, flashing where roofs meet walls (see Finishes where a builder puts them).
  • With framing (ticked in the export window): the studs, plates, headers, joists, rafters and footings, built just for the export, as each floor's own Framing group, so you can show the frame or hide it.
  • High detail: rounder round parts (posts, lamps, faucets) and trees with leaves, as in renders. A bigger file that takes longer to make.

Render in Blender (the desktop app). In the Render window, Render in Blender sends the plan to Blender, which renders it with Cycles in the background: the same Camera, Size and Lighting (its sun and a real sky; with Lights on or the evening lighting, the room's light fittings), with the finishes as they are in Arc. Pick the Blender quality (Quick 64, Good 256 or Best 1024 samples) and Render on (the graphics card when Blender can use one, or the processor only, for older or built-in graphics; if the graphics card runs out of memory part way, it finishes on the processor). Progress shows under the picture, with Cancel. The picture shows in the Render window, Save PNG… saves it again, and it's saved by itself beside the plan ("plan Blender render date.png"; a plan not saved yet: Pictures\Arc renders). Arc finds Blender in its usual places; if it doesn't, it asks you to point to blender.exe (or use Where is Blender…) and remembers. Your Blender setup isn't changed: Blender runs with its own factory settings and nothing is saved there. Blender is free from blender.org; Arc was tried with Blender 4.2 to 5.2.

Handing the model to an engineer or energy advisor (IFC)

Export ▾ → IFC building model saves the building as IFC4, which Revit, ArchiCAD, Vectorworks, FreeCAD (Bonsai), Solibri and the free BIM viewers open. Levels become storeys, and walls, doors, windows, rooms, roofs, stairs, 3D shapes and furniture come across as what they are. For the engineer, beams and posts drawn with the Framing tool come across as beams and columns at their size, and the footings (strip footings, stepped where they step, and the pads under posts, as on the foundation plan) as footings. Each item also carries its data:

  • Walls: the wall type's layers outside to inside, with their materials and thicknesses; the U-value worked out from the layers; the fire rating and STC; whether it's an outside wall and whether it carries a load; its length, height, and side area with and without the doors and windows.
  • Windows and doors: the mark from the schedule, the U-value and SHGC from the product, the maker, a door's fire rating, and the size and area.
  • Rooms: the room number, floor area, height, perimeter and volume.
  • Existing or new: walls, windows and doors marked existing to stay come across as EXISTING, ones to remove as DEMOLISH, and the rest as NEW, so the other program can show the phases.
  • Arc_Common: every field the panel shows for the item, your own fields (Supplier, Ordered…) and the item's Arc id. Copies of one component share one furnishing type.

Each item keeps the same IFC id every time you export, so the person you send it to can compare this week's model with last week's. The first export gives the plan its own IFC id and saves it with the plan. Porches, decks and site work aren't in the IFC yet.

4. Tools, one by one

A short note on each tool and what its panel settings do. Click a tool on the left (open its heading first if it's under one, or press its letter), then read the status bar at the bottom: it always says what to click next. You can type a size instead of clicking, and Esc goes back to the Select arrow.

Walls (W)

Click where a wall starts, then click each corner; right-click, double-click or press Esc to finish. F flips which side of the line the wall sits. Type a length and press Enter to make a wall exactly that long. The panel sets the preset (exterior 2x6, interior 2x4 and so on), thickness, height, material and which way the wall sits on the line you draw (centred, left or right). Walls join at corners by themselves. Select a wall to change it; drag it to push or pull it (attached walls stretch), or drag a blue grip to move an end. Shape makes curved or round walls.

Adding onto a house. Start a wall on the house's outside wall (its line, either face or a corner), or end one there, and the new walls take that wall's build: its thickness, height and siding, so an addition joins the house instead of standing in plain drywall. Roof it with Roof ▸ Auto and cut a door through the old wall with the Door tool.

Can't be moved. In a renovation some walls have to stay: a condo or townhouse's shared wall, a structural wall holding up the floor or roof, or a wall with plumbing or ducts inside. Set Can't be moved in the wall's panel. The design check then warns if the plan moves it, takes it out, or cuts a new door or window in it, and says what that would need.

A selected wall glows in the 3D view too. Its panel has:

  • Framing (all walls in this plan): wood or steel studs and their spacing for every wall. They're saved with the plan, so it shows the same studs on any computer. A plan from before 0.90 uses the setting this computer had, until you change it. The tall-wall stud check uses each wall's own spacing.
  • This wall's framing: its own stud spacing (12", 16", 19.2" or 24"), + Extra studs (doubled, tripled or a 4-stud post at a distance from the start grip: under a beam, beside a heavy cabinet) and + Cut-out (a framed hole for a duct chase, a recessed medicine cabinet or a pass-through: set where it starts, its width, bottom and height). Cut-outs wider than a stud bay get king and jack studs and a header; narrower ones get blocking and a trimmer each side. They show in the 3D framing view (your extra studs a shade darker), the takeoff and the cut lists.
  • Budget for this wall: what it costs, line by line, from the takeoff: studs, plates, headers, drywall sheets, paint by colour, wallpaper or panelling, sheathing, insulation and siding, at the takeoff's prices (typical prices to start with; type your own in Budget ▾ → Takeoff & cost estimate). Select several walls to see them together.
  • Interior finish: each side named by the room it faces. Pick Paint (any colour, with flat, matte, eggshell, satin or semi-gloss), a Feature wall colour, Wallpaper, Wood or slat panelling, Tile, Stone or brick, Plaster, limewash or Venetian (see Plaster walls), Drywall only or Bare framing; Same on both sides copies it across, and Apply to all walls… puts that side's finish on the inside walls of this room, every room on this floor or the whole house in one step. It shows in 3D and is priced in the takeoff. The outside of the house is set under Exterior.

Siding styles, and your own (My siding). An outside wall's Exterior finish has today's 13 finishes and, under More siding, ten more: narrow lap (4″ showing) and wide lap (8″), vertical vinyl, log and half-log siding, cedar tongue and groove, horizontal metal, corrugated metal, acrylic stucco with joint lines, and two-tone (stacked stone up to about 3′, lap siding above), and fibre-cement lap siding (7″ showing) and fibre-cement board & batten. Under the colour, a picture shows the siding as it is. For a fibre-cement wall all the way through, pick the wall type 2x6 R-22, fibre-cement siding on a rainscreen: lap siding on it is counted and noted as fibre-cement, with fibre-cement trim at the corners and round the windows and doors.

  • Change this siding… opens a window beside the panel with a live picture and only the settings that finish can take: Board showing, Batten or rib spacing, Batten width, Shingle row height and How ragged, brick length, course height, Mortar joint, Bond (running, stack or third) and Mortar colour, stucco Joint lines every, panel width and height, a two-tone wall's Stone up to and Stone below, and Shadow depth. Each change is the wall's own when you let go (one undo step). The window says what you might have missed: boards wider than lap siding is made, battens too far apart, shingle rows showing too much for a wall, a stack bond with no steel, stucco joints too far apart.
  • Save as My siding…: give it a name, and it's one of My siding, in the same Exterior finish list, kept in the plan and in My library for your other plans. Your siding from your other editions shows there too: pick one and it's copied here. My stones are in the list too: pick one and the wall is stone veneer in your stone. Reset puts the finish back as Arc ships it.
  • The wall keeps its finish type and colour as before, so the fire checks near the property line and older versions of Arc still read it (an older Arc draws a new style as the finish it sits on: vertical vinyl as lap siding, say).
  • The estimate (Budget ▾) adds a Siding materials section, worked out from the same numbers the 3D view draws with: 12′ boards from how much of each board shows, board-and-batten boards and battens by the foot from their spacing, cedar shingle bundles from the row height, bricks from their size and mortar bags from the joint, metal and fibre-cement panels, and stucco control joints, each with the siding waste (10% unless you set your own). They're materials only: leave them out if the siding's square-foot line already has an installed price. A two-tone wall's stone has its own square-foot line.

Make this a feature wall. In Interior finish, each inside face has a Feature wall list: the plan's style pack's own look first (shiplap for a modern farmhouse, a slat wall for contemporary or Scandinavian, brick for industrial, limewash for Mediterranean, reclaimed wood for rustic, picture-frame moulding for traditional or French…), then every ready-made design. Pick one and it goes on, fitted round the wall's doors and windows, in one undo step; Take the feature wall off clears it. For your own design, work on the face in 3D:

Work on a wall in 3D (a drawing kit of real materials). In Interior finish, click ✎ Work on this face in 3D on a side. The 3D view turns to look straight at it (use 🔒 Lock view to keep it still), and a drawing board of that face opens beside it with its tabs. Pick a piece and drag on the wall (or click, for the ones that go at a point); the + pieces fill the whole face or go round every window. Click a piece with Select to change its size, how far it sticks out, its material or colour. With nothing picked, Wall colour paints the wall behind the pieces, and Apply to all walls… puts that paint on this room's walls, every room on the floor or the whole house.

  • Feature wall: shiplap, tongue & groove, board & batten, a slat wall, wainscot panels, picture-frame moulding, a chair rail, stone and brick veneer, reclaimed wood, acoustic panels, a floating shelf, a picture ledge and medallions, a mirror and limewash, each at its real board or trim size; or start from a ready-made design (it steps round doors and windows): board & batten (to 60" or floor to ceiling), wainscot, picture-frame or grid moulding, shiplap, tongue & groove, a slat wall, stacked stone, brick, reclaimed wood, limewash, a mirror wall for a home gym, or acoustic panels.
  • Exterior: board & batten, shutters beside every window, window trim round every window and door, corner boards, a band board, a stone wainscot along the bottom, a shake panel for a gable, a single shutter, a gable vent and corbels.
  • Framing: the 3D view switches to the framing and the studs show behind the board. Add a stud, doubled studs or a post (click), a header (sized from its width), blocking or a plate (drag across), or a cut-out (drag a box). They go into the wall's own framing: darker in the 3D framing view, and in the takeoff and cut lists.
  • Electrical: outlets, GFCI and USB outlets, switches, dimmers, 3-way switches, data, a thermostat and wall sconces, clicked onto the wall at their usual height (16" for outlets, 48" for switches) unless you click well above or below. They're real blocks on the Electrical layer, in the takeoff and the wiring.
  • Draw & fill: draw on the wall itself and fill what you draw. The plan's drawing tools work on the face (Line, Polyline, Rectangle, Polygon, Circle, Ellipse, Arc, Spline) plus a freehand Pen. A line, level, sloped or curved, splits the wall into two zones: it carries on (dashed) to the wall's edges. Two lines make three bands; a closed shape is a zone of its own, so a line and a circle make three. Pick a finish under Fill with (paint, or any siding, stone or brick in the siding list's own colours, charcoal shale too) and click a zone with Fill. Each zone is laid flat in the wall's own siding, its stone or boards at their real size, with no lines between the pieces, and steps round the doors and windows; the wall stays one wall, with the same framing, openings and corners. With Select, drag a line to move it (the stone stays on its side), type a sloped line's end heights or click Make it level, or press Delete to take it off (its zones join up again, in the fill painted last). On a gable, the board shows the whole gable up to its peak.
    • Half-timbering (Tudor): Half-timber: posts, cross or curved, then click a zone. Dark 1×8 timber boards go on over stucco, kept inside that zone and clear of the windows: a rail along its foot, posts up it, and cross or curved braces between them, lower near a gable's ends. Click a board with Select to change its width or colour.
    • In the estimate: each zone is counted by its area as the finish it is (the same line as that siding on another wall, with its siding materials), and that area comes off the wall's own siding or paint on that face. A zone in a gable, above the wall's height, is counted too. Timber boards are counted by the foot (Half-timber trim boards). Stone veneer and stacked stone on outside walls start at about $14 a sq ft supplied plus the mason's time in the build budget, about $29 a sq ft installed (an estimate: check it against a quote).
    • On the drawings: the elevations hatch each zone as its finish and label it (Stacked shale / ledgestone (Charcoal shale), Half-timbering: 1×8 trim boards over stucco).

    Straight from the 3D view. Click a wall in 3D: a small ✎ Draw on this wall button comes up where you clicked. It opens that side of the wall (the side you clicked, inside or outside) flat and big, as its elevation, with the same tools. Nothing changes until you click Save, which puts it on the wall in one undo step; Cancel leaves the wall as it was, and Undo on the board takes back one change at a time. On an outside wall the button also offers Whole … side: every outside wall facing that way, laid out as the elevation of that side of the house, so one line or one stone band runs across all of them. Lines and fills already on those walls show and stay (change them on one wall); walls at an angle to the elevation are drawn as they're seen. Ctrl+K → Draw on a side of the house opens it too, with the side to pick at the top.

    Find it with Ctrl+K: Draw on a wall (with a wall picked), or the lesson Draw on a wall.

Feature and exterior pieces stand out from the wall in 3D, show on rendered elevations, are priced in the takeoff by what they are (shiplap by the square foot, trim by the foot, shutters each), and sit on their own Feature walls & trim layer: hide it to see the plain walls.

Rooms (R)

The Room tool draws four walls at once: set the width and depth (inside or outside sizes) and click to place it. Rooms are also found by themselves when walls you draw close a space, and an inside wall drawn across a room splits it in two (the bigger part keeps the name, floor and ceiling; the new one copies them, ready to rename). Select a room to name it, see its area and set its floor, ceiling height and ceiling type; in commercial projects, its occupancy. On an upper floor, Joists under it puts the floor joists under that room at 12" or 8" on center, or doubles them, for a heavy load (a tiled bath, a tub, a piano, a kitchen island): the extra joists run the full span and show in the 3D framing view, the floor framing plan, the takeoff and the framing package.

Floor framing as it's built. The floor over a stair, a floor opening or a sunken room is framed round it: the joists through it are cut back to doubled headers (tail joists, each on a hanger), a doubled trimmer runs the full span each side, and the headers hang on the trimmers. The floor framing plan draws them heavier with the opening crossed through, the 3D framing view shows the same pieces, and the takeoff lists the headers and the joist hangers. The joists are sized for the clear span between the walls and beams on the floor below, and where a piece would be longer than 16' (lumber) they break over a wall or beam and lap there: the plan marks it JOISTS LAP OVER BEARING. On the floor framing sheet's panel, Joists run turns them to run the long way. Openings wider than 3.2 m, and trimmers beside headers over 2 m, are sized by calculation (NBC 9.23.9): Arc says so.

Where a room stops. Finding a room (Floors, or clicking inside one) stops at doorways and archways up to 8' wide, so a bedroom doesn't run out into the hall. Where two rooms run together with no opening to stop at (a kitchen and its dining area, a hall and the landing), set the Room tool's Draw to A dividing line and click its two ends: a dashed line on the plan that finding a room stops at (select any line and tick Dividing line to make it one, or untick it to make it a plain line again). Room names that would print over each other (a closet inside a bedroom, two small rooms side by side) move apart on the plan.

Vaulted ceilings and sunken rooms. Select a room and set Ceiling to Vaulted (rises to a ridge): the ceiling rises from the tops of the room's two long walls to a ridge down the middle, as it does under scissor trusses (a bonus room over a garage, a great room), with the ends filled in above the walls. Vault rises sets how far it rises at the ridge (empty is a 4/12 pitch). The 3D view, renders and walks show it. A room set a step down (or up) from its floor, a sunken bonus room or living room, shows its own flooring, its walls painted down to its floor, and its baseboards on that floor; furniture in it sits on the lower floor. A feature wall in a sunken room can start below the main floor so it reaches down to the room's floor.

Doors (D) and windows (N)

Hover over a wall: the distances to each end show. Click to place, or type the distance from the blue end. Pick the style in the panel first (hinged, double, pocket, sliding, bifold, barn, garage or a cased opening; hung, casement, sliding, fixed, awning, ribbon or curtain-wall windows) with its width, height, sill and header. Select one to flip the swing, change its design, colour, grilles and trim, or make a glass corner.

Big glass (Arc and ArcRes). More in Type: for doors, a Multi-slide / lift-and-slide door (2 to 6 panels, sliding to one end or half to each, into a pocket in the wall if you tick it), a Folding glass wall (3 to 8 panels), a Pivot entry door (set how far the pin is in from the edge) and a Dutch door; Door height has 8', 9' and 10' as well as the standard 6'-8". For windows, a Bay window or Bow window that stands out from the outside of the wall (how far, the side angle or the number of units, a window seat, a little roof over it and a skirt or brackets under it; Flip to other side if it faces the wrong way), a Mulled unit (several windows joined: how many across, the end windows' width and kind, a row of transoms above; it is one tag in the window schedule), and shaped windows: Trapezoid, Triangle, Round and Half-round. Each has its own plan symbol, shows in 3D, and is counted in the schedules, the energy worksheet (a bay by all its faces), the heat loss, the estimate and IFC. For egress, the part that opens counts: a bay's side units or a mulled unit's casements; shaped windows are fixed glass. A wide one (multi-slide, folding wall, mulled unit, bay or bow) can have a Portal frame ticked under Header over it: an engineered frame that braces the wall there, so the braced-wall check (NBC 9.23.13) counts that stretch as braced, and the permit list asks for the engineer's letter.

Arched tops. A door, a window or a cased opening (between rooms too) can have an arched top: select it (or pick the Door or Window tool before you place one) and set Top to a Round top (a half circle), a Segment arch (a shallow curve), an Elliptical (basket) arch, an Eyebrow (a gentle wave), a Gothic (pointed) arch, or a Peaked top that follows the roof. Rise is how far the top rises above where the straight sides stop. A door has In the arch: glass in the arch over the door (a transom), or the door arch-topped too (a storybook plank door). The height is to the top of the arch. The wall is framed square, with a header the full width and an arch form (plywood cut to the curve) under it; the frame, glass, casing and siding follow the curve in 3D, the elevations and sections. The plan shows a dashed curve across the wall, the schedules add a Top column, the parts list prices it higher (about 1.8 times a window, 1.6 a door, 1.5 a cased opening) and counts the arch forms, and NBC 9.36 and the heat loss use the true glass area. Only the part below the curve opens, so that is what egress measures. Product has arch-top units ready to pick: a round-top fixed window over a picture window, a segment-top casement, a peaked gable window, an arch-top entry door with an arched transom and an arch-top plank door. A GLB export (for Blender) has the arch as drawn; IFC has the opening as its square size.

A window up in a gable end. A wall under a gable roof rises to the roof, so a window can go up into the gable: put it in the gable end wall and set its Sill height above the wall's height (Ctrl+K: Window up in a gable end). Give it a Peaked top and press Match the roof slope to make its top follow the roof. A window that runs past the slope is trimmed to fit and Design check says so; one above a wall with no gable or vault over it can't show, and Design check says that too. A gable with a window in it is framed as a rake wall (its studs up to the roof, the window framed with a header, sill, king and jack studs) in the framing view and the parts list.

Several at once, evenly spaced. In the panel's Several at once section, set How many (say 4) and pick the spacing:

  • Equal sections: the wall is split into that many equal parts (dashed tag lines) with one centred in each.
  • Equal gaps: the space at both ends and between each one is the same.
  • Centred on the wall: the group sits on the wall's middle with the gap you set between them.
  • From the end: the first one starts a set distance from the end nearer your cursor and the rest follow with your gap. Type a distance and press Enter to change where the first one goes.
  • From where I click: the first one goes where you point and the rest follow along the wall with your gap.

Hover over a wall to see them all with tag points at their centres and the space between each. One click places the whole set, and one Undo takes it off. It turns red if they won't fit or would overlap another door or window. Set How many back to 1 to place one at a time.

Door designs include 4 panel (2 over 2), and for bifold and sliding closet doors, Mirror: a slim frame round mirror glass on both faces. Bifold and sliding doors now show the design you pick (2 leaves on a narrow closet, 4 on a wide one).

Real wood doors. A door or garage door (not a glass one) can be Real wood: western red cedar, Douglas fir, white or red oak, walnut, cherry, mahogany, hard maple or pine, under a Wood finish (a clear coat, natural oil or a stain). The grain shows in 3D and runs the way the boards do: up a door and its stiles, across its rails; across a flush, raised-panel or ribbed garage door; up a carriage door's boards. Picking a colour paints it again.

Outside trim and inside casing. A door or window can have an Outside trim colour and an Inside casing colour of its own, so black trim can go round black sashes outside while the casing inside stays cream. Left at As it is, both faces show the trim as before (a palette's trim colour, else white).

Walls whose outside changes along them. Arc works out the outside face for each stretch of a wall from what is on each side: an open porch or outdoors is outside, a room or a garage is inside (even where the garage door is drawn as a gap). So a wall that runs from an open front porch on into the house gets siding on the porch stretch and drywall past it. The Design check warns when an outside face shows drywall.

Windows set in the wall, sills, pull bars and pivot doors. A window's Set in the wall moves it back from the outside face (flush, 2", 4" or 6" back), so the wall's thickness shows round it as a reveal, as in stone or brick. Sill outside adds a stone (or precast) or wood sill under it, out past the wall. A door's Handle can be a Long pull bar, and a hinged door can be a Pivot door: wider and taller than a hinged one, turning on a pin set in the floor and the head.

Window opening control device. A window that opens, with a low sill on a floor more than 1.8 m (6') above the ground outside, is flagged by the design check: a child could fall out. Tick Window opening control device in its panel for a stop (ASTM F2090) that lets it open only 100 mm (4") until it's released; the release still lets the window open fully, so a bedroom's escape window still works. The check is then met, and the window schedule lists the device. In Alberta it's accepted on a bedroom escape window too (STANDATA 23-BCV-001), but not in an adaptable or barrier-free home.

Stairs (S)

Set the floor-to-floor height, width and tread, choose straight, L, U or spiral, then click where the stair starts and the direction it climbs. The number of steps and the riser height are worked out. Select a stair to change each flight and landing, or click one step to give it its own depth, height or shape (a bullnose or a winder). Warnings compare it with the code limits in the Code limits section (choose the rules in Preferences).

Curved stairs. Set Shape to Curved (follows a curved wall), click the centre of the curve, then where the middle of the bottom step goes. Select it to set the Inside radius, Width and how much Each step turns; it's checked against the ordinary stair limits, with the treads measured 12" in from the inside like winders. Beside a curved wall, Fit to the curved wall gives it the wall's centre and runs its outside edge along the wall.

Stair parts: treads, stringers, railings and finishes. Select a stair and open Stair parts in the panel. Quick style sets a whole look in one go (open risers with glass on steel beams, turned balusters with box newels, metal balusters with a wood rail, a steel beam with cables, floating treads, a half wall with a cap), or set each part: How it's built (solid steps as before, closed stringers, open stringers that show each step, one or two steel beams, or floating treads), open or closed Risers, Tread thickness and Nosing; the Railing on the open sides (handrail only, balusters: square, turned, round metal or flat bar, glass on standoffs or in a channel, steel cables, or a solid half wall), Newel posts (box, turned or steel), the Handrail shape, and which side is Against a wall (that side gets a handrail on brackets instead). The railing goes on the sides chosen in Handrails, and round the open edges of each landing. Each part can have its own finish: wood, paint, stone or metal. The 3D view and Photo renders draw every piece, the estimate counts the treads, risers, stringers, balusters, newels, rail, glass and cable, and the stair's warnings check the guard height (900 mm on a stair in a home, NBC 9.8.8.3), the gap between balusters (a 100 mm ball mustn't pass, 9.8.8.5) and, under US or UK rules, open risers. Flared bottom (left, right or both sides) gives the bottom steps rounded ends that stick out past the side, the bottom one furthest (Flared steps, Sticks out), shown on the plan and in 3D, on straight, L, U, curved and spiral stairs (a spiral flares on its outside). The railing sweeps out over them, and At the bottom ends it in a curl (a volute, the scroll a stair builder carves) or on a newel post of the stair's own newel style. The side board (stringer) on a flared side starts above the rounded steps. The plan draws the balusters, newel posts, glass and handrails to scale from above. The estimate gives the stair and railing parts starting prices (treads, risers, stringers, starting steps, the curl and the railing: estimates from Canadian stores, to check with yours), and an IFC export writes the stair as its flights, landings, stringers, railings, balusters, glass panels and newel posts, so a building-model program sees each part. Back to a plain stair puts it back as it was. The Stairs tool's Style gives new stairs a look before you draw them. Printing the house on a 3D printer, thin balusters, rails, cable and glass are made about 0.8 mm thick on the print (two lines of the nozzle), so the railings show on the model; the 3D view, the plan and the IFC keep them as drawn.

The opening upstairs. A stair that goes up to the next floor cuts that floor where you need headroom over it: the slab, the room's flooring (in 3D, the flooring count in the estimate and the IFC file) and a drawn ceiling under the top of the stair below. A guard runs round the opening's open sides upstairs, not where you step off and not along a wall: the stair's own railing (balusters, glass, cable or a half wall, from Stair parts), or a plain rail for a stair without parts. Its parts are in the estimate. Untick Guard round the opening upstairs in the stair's panel if a wall or your own railing is there instead. (A Floor opening with a rail drawn round the stair opening replaces it.) The floor framing round the opening is shown in the framing view.

Floors and baseboards (A)

Pick a floor (hardwood, laminate, vinyl plank, tile, stone, carpet, concrete, cork, rubber gym tiles…) with its colour, plank or tile size and direction, and a baseboard, then click inside a room. Open areas can be outlined by clicking their corners. Show ▾ → Floor patterns shows them on the plan; the 3D view shows real planks and tile.

How much grain shows. A hardwood, laminate or vinyl plank floor has a How much grain shows slider: None for a plain, smooth look, 100% as it comes, or up to 300% for a strong, rustic grain. It's kept on that room's floor, and shows in 3D and in Photo renders.

Kinds of carpet. A carpet floor shows a picture of each kind, in the room's colour: Plain (the carpet Arc has always drawn), Plush (cut pile), soft with a velvet sheen; Berber (loop), rows of tight loops with darker flecks; Frieze (twisted), long curly yarns that hide footprints; and Cut-and-loop pattern, with lower loops making diamonds. Carpet texture sets how much the pile stands out in 3D. Change this carpet… opens a window with a live picture and that kind's sliders (texture, tuft size, flecks of another shade, and for cut-and-loop the pattern size), notes on what you might have missed (big loops snag on pets' claws, carpet in a bathroom), Reset, and Save as My carpet…, which keeps it in the plan and in My library for your other plans and editions. The kind changes only the look: the estimate and the transition strips are the same for every kind.

Where two floors meet. Wherever a doorway or an open edge joins two different floors (carpet against plank, plank against tile), the 3D view shows a transition strip, and the estimate counts Transition strip by the foot under Floors.

Ceiling texture and steps. A room's panel has Texture for a drywall ceiling: smooth, popcorn, knockdown, California knockdown, orange peel, skip trowel, stipple, swirl or Santa Fe, in the ceiling's colour, on plain, tray, coffered and vaulted ceilings; the estimate has a line for it. Old popcorn to scrape off adds the scraping to the estimate (popcorn sprayed before 1990 can hold asbestos: have it tested first). Where a high ceiling meets a lower one with no wall between (a 9' living room open to an 8' hall), the 3D view drops the drywall down between them as a bulkhead, by itself.

Tray, coffered, beamed and vaulted ceilings. In Arc and ArcRes, select a room and pick its Ceiling. A tray steps up in the middle: one step or two, how far each step comes in from the walls and how high it is, a crown moulding in the step, and LED cove lighting (a hidden strip that washes the ceiling; pick its brightness and colour, and its lumens are counted in the lighting study). The middle stays at the ceiling height and the edge drops, or the middle rises if there's room above. A coffered ceiling is sized by the size of each coffer or by how many there are, with the beam width and depth, a finish for the beams and the panels (paint, beadboard, oak, walnut, cedar), a crown in each coffer, and Line them up with: the room (equal coffers), the middle of the room, or a table or island so a coffer sits right over it. Exposed beams (box beams, solid timber or reclaimed timber) go on a plain ceiling or follow a vault, at the spacing, size and finish you set. A vaulted ceiling has a Shape: a ridge down the middle, a ridge off-centre, follows the roof above (a cathedral ceiling at the roof's pitch: change the roof and it follows), one slope (shed), barrel (curved) or groin (two curves crossing; a long room gets a row of bays). Vaults with a ridge take timber trusses: king post, scissor or hammer beam. Ceiling lights sit on whatever ceiling is above them, and a fan, pendant or chandelier gets a rod up to a high ceiling. The ceiling plan draws the steps, beams and ridge lines, sections cut through them, and the estimate counts the drywall along the slopes, the beams by the foot, the trusses, the crown and the LED strip. The trusses and beams here are for the look and the budget: an engineer sizes the real ones.

Framing a curved wall. A curved or round wall (Wall tool, Shape) is framed the way a builder frames it, as one curve, not a row of short straight walls. Its top and bottom plates are cut to the curve from 3/4" plywood, two layers glued together for each plate, and the parts list counts them as plywood sheets (how many strips fit on a sheet depends on how tight the curve is). Its studs are laid out along the whole curve: 12" apart on a curve tighter than 20 ft, 9" under 6 ft, and 6" to 8" on the tightest. The drywall on each face is picked for how tightly that face bends: ordinary board on a gentle curve, 1/4" flexible board in two layers on a tighter one (wetted on the tightest), and plaster on metal lath past that. The sheathing on an outside face is 1/2" plywood stood on end, then 3/8", then two layers of 1/4", then bending plywood as the curve gets tighter. These follow CertainTeed Canada's flexible drywall data sheet and APA's plywood bending table. The curved wall's panel says what Arc assumed under How it's framed, and the 3D framing view draws the plates curved. A framer may build it another way, so check with yours.

Walls under a vaulted ceiling. In a vaulted room the walls run up to the slope: a gable end wall up to the ceiling (and outside, up to the roof), a partition up to the ceiling over it, and the wall between a vaulted room and a flat one up to the vault on the vaulted side, with its paint, wallpaper, tile or feature wall going all the way up on that side. Windows and doors can go up past the wall's height into the slope (a triangle or a trapezoid of glass under a gable, say), kept just under the ceiling; their headers are sized for the room under the slope, and they're in the schedules as usual. In the framing view each of these walls is a rake wall: its studs run up to the slope under a doubled sloped top plate, and the roof's gable studs are left out over it. A post drawn with the Framing tool and left Up to the wall tops runs up to the vaulted ceiling over it. An interior elevation of a vaulted room shows the slope along the wall, with its lowest and highest heights. Where a vaulted room is open to another room with no wall between, the 3D view still fills the end in above the lower ceiling. Not yet: the estimate and cut lists still count a rake wall's studs and the gable at the wall's own height, and the IFC file has flat-topped walls.

Trim with real profiles (Arc and ArcRes). Select a room. Under its floor, Base profile gives the baseboard a real shape (colonial, craftsman, modern square, stepped…) in place of the simple one, at the height and colour you set. Under Trim, pick a Crown, a Casing for this room's side of its doors and windows, and a Chair rail: twenty profiles in all, each shown as a small drawing of its shape. Each has a size and a colour (it follows the baseboard's colour unless you choose one), and the chair rail has its height above the floor. In 3D each one runs round the room in its true shape, mitred at the corners; the baseboard and chair rail stop at each door, and, as a builder fits them, at cabinets, a tub, a shower, a vanity or a fireplace set against the wall. Crown stops where something reaches the ceiling (a tall cabinet or built-in, a fireplace surround to the ceiling). The estimate, the shopping list and the trim cut list count only the trim that's there, and the cut list says where each piece stops ("square (at the tub)"). Use this trim in every room copies it to the whole plan. The cost estimate counts crown, casing and chair rail by the foot under Trim & mouldings; a baseboard with a profile is its own line under Floors, named for the profile.

A profile of your own. Draw the moulding's shape at its real size with the Line or Shape tool, as you'd see its end: the wall on its left, the floor along the bottom (for crown, the ceiling along the top; a casing lies flat with the door or window on its left). Select the shape: its panel shows Trim profile from this shape. Say what it is (crown, baseboard, casing or chair rail), name it, and Save as a trim profile. It is now in each room's lists as My: its name, and it is kept in the plan. A shape bigger than 16" either way isn't offered.

Panelling on every wall of a room. In the same place, Wall panelling puts one look on every wall of the room at once, fitted round the doors and windows: wainscot with a chair rail, picture-frame moulding, full-height panelling, beadboard, board and batten, a tall wainscot with a plate ledge, tongue and groove, shiplap, fluted wood panels, wood veneer panels or limewash. Tile already on those walls stays. Boards and battens carry on under a window and over a door; a look's own chair rail is left out where the room already has a chair rail at about that height. Take the panelling off every wall removes it, and Undo takes either back. Each wall can still be changed on its own in the wall designer, where a picked frame (a wainscot panel, picture-frame moulding) has Corners: top and bottom running the full width, sides running the full height, or mitred at 45°, and Same corners on every frame here.

Rock: walls, ledges and cliffs in real rock

Open Yard on the tool bar and pick Rock (or Draw ▾ → Rock wall, ledge or cliff). The panel shows a picture of each rock: laid stone (stacked shale, ledgestone, rough shale, fieldstone, carved blocks, and the newer stones below) for walls, and natural rock (granite boulders, Rockies layers, layered rock, limestone cliff, mountain cliff) for ledges and cliffs. Click one, set Top of wall and Thickness, then click along the wall and press Enter. Every stone stands out in 3D, in renders and in the print files. On a house the rock is sized in inches (a shale course is 2 to 4 in. tall); on a model base, in millimetres. Level ground behind makes it a retaining wall.

  • Colour: a real stone colour from the squares, or any colour from the box beside them. Each stone its own shade makes every stone a little lighter or darker; Darker joints shades the gaps.
  • Some stones in a second colour, and How many of them (up to 60%).
  • Stone size, Depth (how far the stones stand out), Roughness (lumps, chips and grain) and Wobble (how much the lines wave). Reset the shape puts them back as the rock was designed.
  • Carved blocks: square-cut stone blocks in level rows of mixed lengths, each with rounded edges, chisel marks and a deep joint all round (a castle, a bridge, a storybook wall). Two more sliders: Block length and Joint depth. Printed standing up, the underside of each block becomes a 45° slope, so it still needs no supports.
  • The newer stones, in every edition with the Rock tool (house stone first in Arc and ArcRes, garden walls first in Landscape, models first in Hobby):
    • Rundle stone (ArcRes: Dark mountain stone): dark grey Bow Valley stone in rows of mixed height, about one stone in seven rusty, for fireplaces and house fronts in the Banff and Canmore style.
    • Tyndall stone (ArcRes: Smooth cream limestone): big smooth cream blocks with thin joints and a soft mottled pattern, the Manitoba stone on many Canadian public buildings. On a model its joints open to 0.6 mm, so the nozzle can draw them.
    • Squared stone in three heights (ArcRes: Cut stone, mixed sizes): squared stones with split faces, some two rows tall, the cultured stone pattern on many newer Alberta houses.
    • River rock (ArcRes: Round river stones): smooth round stones in a flat bed of mortar, for chimneys, pillars and cabin foundations.
    • Split-face wall block (ArcRes: Retaining wall blocks): same-size blocks with a rough face, each row half a block over, like the stacking blocks at a landscape yard. (Each row's step back comes later.)
    • Armour stone (ArcRes: Big quarry blocks): very big squared quarry blocks with rough faces, for retaining walls, shorelines and seating edges.
    • Storybook boulders: fat rounded stones of mixed sizes, some joined into big ones: the bubbly cartoon stone of a Christmas village or a fairy house.
    • Castle wall: worn blocks, a few sunk deep, corners knocked off, for castles and ruins.
    • Railway stone: big blocks with a flat border round each one and a rough bulging middle, as on old railway bridges and tunnel mouths.
    Each starts in its own colours (Rundle with its rusty stones, river rock in two greys); change them as for any rock. Their own parts are sliders in Change this stone…: the Bond (stacked, half or a third over), Stones two rows tall, Mortar bed depth, Stones joined into big ones, Flat margin, Stones set back, Corners knocked off, Mottled colour and Chisel strength. The block-shaped ones have Block length and Joint depth too.
  • Change this stone…: a window you can move, beside the panel, to change the stones themselves, with a live picture: the height of the rows and the length of the stones, the joints, how far stones stand out, chipped or rounded edges (each rock shows only the sliders that make sense for it: fieldstone has stone width and height, not rows). Each change goes on the wall as you let go of a slider (one undo step). Reset to puts the rock back as it comes. On a model it says what you might have missed, like joints too thin for the 0.4 mm nozzle to show.
  • Save as My stone…: give it a name, and it's one of My stones: in a row of its own under the rock pictures, kept in the plan (it opens the same anywhere, on the web too) and in My library (File ▾ → My library…) for your other plans. Your stones from your other editions show in the row too, marked ⤵: pick one and it's copied here. A plan from someone else with stones you don't have shows them too, with Keep in My stones. An older Arc shows the rock your stone started from.
  • Select a rock wall to change any of these later. Retaining walls in Stacked stone or Fieldstone are rock walls too.
  • Printing (on a model): each rock wall prints as a part of its own, standing up. Every stone keeps to the 45° rule, so it needs no supports. Stones in a second colour are a second piece of the part, for a second filament (the Bambu Studio project gives each its own). A second colour makes a print much slower, because the printer changes filament on every layer.
  • Save a test tile… (on a model): a piece 50 mm long and 30 mm tall of the rock, as a Bambu Studio project, to try on the printer before the whole wall.
  • A rock wall along the edge of a model base is a skirting piece: it stands against the base and lifts off.
  • House finishes: walls finished in Stacked shale / ledgestone or Stone veneer outside (Stone veneer's Cut stone blocks is the carved blocks), or in a stacked stone or fieldstone material inside, show real stones in 3D and renders, with light and shadow on each one. One of My stones can be a house finish too: on a wall finished in Stone veneer or Stacked shale, pick it under My stone (it takes the finish's colour, and the budget names it), or pick it in the Stone or brick list of a wall's inside face. Only laid stone that repeats with no seam can be a finish (not granite or the cliffs).

The lesson Do it with me: rock walls and cliffs (Guide ▾ → Lessons) goes through all of it on a small landscape.

Stone facing: a carved stone face on a model

On a model base the Rock tool also draws stone facings: a sculpted stone face that is a piece of its own and lifts off the model, like the carved walls of a storybook village. Pick Rock and set Draw to A stone facing (or Draw ▾ → Stone facing (on a model)), then click along where it goes and press Enter:

  • Along a base's edge (start and end on the edge): it covers the base's side, round corners too, from the base's bottom up past the ground by Height above the ground. Hangs below the base by takes it lower, down over a skirting.
  • Anywhere else: a facing on its own line, its face on your right as you click. Select it to turn on Smooth curve through its points, or to Face the other way.
  • Stone: Carved blocks on a plinth (square blocks in level courses, pillowed edges, chisel marks, deep joints, and a course of longer, flatter stones along the foot), or a rock from the Rock tool (pick it from the pictures). Shuffle the stones lays the same stone differently. Solid behind the joints is how thick the piece is where the joints are deepest.
  • Arches, windows and columns (select the facing): Storybook wall sets the village's wall at once: carved blocks on a plinth, a stone column at each end and arches between, each in a ring of nine wedge stones with a proud keystone, a deep recess and a lantern hanging inside. Add arch, Add arched window, Add lantern niche, Add column and Add shale panel put one in the widest stretch of plain wall; Space the arches evenly spreads them 50 mm apart, clear of the ends and the columns. Each has a row with where it is along the facing and how wide; × takes it out. Above them the face is laid out flat: drag a numbered marker to move that one along.
  • Snow (select the facing): A snow cap rolling over the top lays soft round scoops along its top, with lobes hanging a little lower and a pillow on each column; How far it rolls over and Lumpy shape it. Drifts banked at the foot pile snow against it, kept off the arches' sills (How high, How many). Snow always meets the stone at a slant with a small rounded fillet, never as a sheer step, and is never thinner than 0.6 mm. On its own line, Snow round it on the ground lets the board's snow scour a hollow on its windward side and trail a drift behind. Storybook wall turns the cap and drifts on.
  • Lit arches (select the facing, Lights): a groove 4 mm deep runs along the facing's back, 11.5 to 22.5 mm above its foot, for a 12 V WS2811 LED strip (60 LEDs a metre: one light you can set every 50 mm, the arches' spacing). The strip sticks to the side the facing covers and the facing lifts off over it. The face moves out 4.4 mm, so behind each arch is a 1.2 mm wall of white that glows, and the lantern glass prints white too. The panel counts the lights and the strip's length and says about how much power it draws. In 3D the arches glow.
  • Sizes that print: nothing is thinner than 0.6 mm, and a lantern's glazing bars are 0.64 mm. An arch narrower than 12 mm gets no lantern, and the panel says so.
  • In 3D a facing shows a little coarser than it prints (about 60,000 points each), so a big model stays quick to turn.
  • Printing: each facing is a part of its own, printed at full detail (a point every 0.3 mm). A straight one prints face up, lying on its back: no supports, so every joint and undercut prints. A curved one prints standing on its foot, its stone and the snow cap's underside kept to the 45° rule (round arch tops bridge). Prints in its panel can choose. A facing longer than the bed is cut into pieces that butt together, each cut halfway across plain wall, never through an arch or a column ("piece 2 of 3"). Its colours (stone, snow, the dark recesses and frames, the lanterns' gold) are each a closed piece of their own in one Bambu Studio project, each with its filament; Colours: Stone and snow only keeps it to two filaments, one in each of the H2D's nozzles. A project with facings is saved compressed (a facing is often a million triangles).

The lesson Do it with me: a stone wall with arches (Guide ▾ → Lessons) goes through it on a small landscape.

Outdoor (O)

Patios, decks (with height, posts and railings), landscape areas (mulch, rock, gravel, grass, garden beds), fences and property lines. Property lines can be typed as surveyor bearings like N 45°30' E 120'. Sloped ground, rock and water are here too.

Paver patterns. A patio's Design list has herringbone, basketweave, running bond, stack bond (a straight grid, every joint lined up), cobblestone, ashlar, large slabs, hexagons (8" across), a circle kit (rings round the middle, nice under a fire pit) and grass pavers (a concrete grid with grass in the holes, so rain soaks in). Brick has stack bond too. Direction turns the pattern: for herringbone, Left to right lays it at 90° to the edges and Diagonal at 45°. Tick Soldier border for a row of pavers laid crosswise round the edge. The plan, the 3D view and the amounts to order all show it.

The ground's look on a sloped site. Under the ground tools' settings (any contour, spot height, water or rock panel, or the Outdoor tool with a ground mode):

Graded ground round the house. Where the ground outside a ground-floor wall is below the floor, the concrete foundation shows down to the ground, parged grey. Patios, walks and fences on the ground floor lie on sloped or graded ground (a walk down a bank, a terrace that falls away from the house). The Design check's drainage note looks only outside the house, and beside paving asks a 2% fall (about 2½" in 10′) instead of the 6" it asks of planted ground.

Glass and cable railings. In the Outdoor tool's Fence, the Metal kind has Glass panel railing and Cable railing at guard height (42"): along the edge of a terrace or a retaining wall where the drop is more than 600 mm, so a guard is needed, without hiding the view.

Walls, fences and hedges that join. Where two meet, Arc joins them the way they're built. A retaining wall's corners are mitred (no notch at the outside, no gap at a wide bend), in 3D, on the plan and in a printed rock wall. Two walls drawn to the same corner make one corner; where one is lower, the taller runs through the corner and the lower stops against it; a wall drawn into the side of another, or a little short of it, stops at its face. Fences that meet share one post (the taller fence's); an end drawn a few inches short of another fence meets it; a fence that tees into another gets a post there, with the posts on each side spaced again. Hedges placed end to end or round a corner are one hedge: one outline on the plan, and in 3D each hedge is a row of shrubs its whole length. To keep one as it was, untick Joins the walls it meets, Joins the fences it meets or One hedge with the hedges it touches in its panel.

Fence styles, and your own (My fences). The Outdoor tool's Fence has 27 styles in six kinds: wood, vinyl, metal, chain link, composite, and Farm & rural (page wire, post-and-rail, a hog panel in a wood frame, and a snow fence of wood slats on wire). Wood also has a good-neighbour fence (each panel's boards on the other side from the last, so both yards see a good side), a lattice screen and boards across on steel posts; chain link comes with privacy slats too. Under the style, a side view shows the fence as it is.

  • Change this fence… opens a window beside the panel with a live side view and only the settings that style can take: Height, Board width, Gap between boards, Top of the boards (flat, dog-ear, pointed or scalloped), Rails, Post spacing and Post cap (none, flat, pyramid or ball). Each change is the fence's own, one undo step at a time. Notes say what you might have missed: a gap over 100 mm a small child fits through, posts too far apart for 8′ rails, a fence over 5′ with only two rails, a fence taller than the bylaw allows, or a snow fence that isn't about half open.
  • Save as My fence…: give it a name, and it's one of My fences: a row of pictures under the side view, kept in the plan and in My library for your other plans. Your fences from your other editions show there too, marked ⤵: pick one and it's copied here. Reset puts the style back as Arc ships it.
  • The estimate (Budget ▾) adds a Fence materials section: posts (gate posts come with each gate), boards counted from their width and gap, rails, wire, panels and caps, at typical prices for your country. They're the materials to build it yourself: leave them out if the fence's own line has an installed price.
  • Round a pool, the Design check reads the real rails and gaps: rails closer than 45″ (1.14 m), boards or cables across with gaps a foot fits in, or wire mesh, are easy to climb; a gap over 100 mm lets a child through.

An infinity (vanishing) edge. Select an in-ground pool and set Edge to a vanishing edge on one side: the water runs to that edge with no coping and spills over it into a catch basin below. Its price is on the pool's lines in the takeoff.

  • Ground cover: grass, snow, bare earth, sand or Forest floor (leaves and moss), for a house in the woods. It shows on a house's site as well as on a model.
  • Ground detail: Fine (18") or Very fine (12") shapes the ground more closely, for steep banks, rock and streams. Normal is quicker for big, gentle sites.
  • Bare rock: where the ground is steeper than the angle you pick, it shows as sandstone. Banks made by retaining walls, paths, pads and water stay ground.

Water now shelves down from its edge to its full depth, so banks run down into a stream instead of stopping at a wall. Trees, shrubs and boulders on the ground floor stand on sloped ground by themselves (unless you give one a height of its own). Boulders come in 6' and 10' sizes and in Sandstone or Mossy stone, and sandstone rock ledges look like natural layered rock. Samples → Fallingwater uses all of it: Bear Run running under the house, its falls, and the stream stepping down the glen through the woods.

A deck's height is above the ground under it, whatever floor you draw it on: a deck 24" up drawn on the basement level is 24" above the yard, not 24" over the basement floor. Where the ground slopes (contours or spot heights), the height is from the ground under the middle of the deck, each post and pile reaches the ground under it, and the guard is worked out from the lowest edge. The panel shows where the deck's top is against the floor it's drawn on (a deck off a door is usually a step below the floor inside). A deck from an older plan keeps its height above its floor, so the plan opens as it was; its panel says so and has a button to measure it from the ground.

Deck framing and the deck permit drawing. Select a deck and its panel shows Deck framing: what holds it up, sized for the plan's city (File ▾ → Plan info). A deck carries 1.9 kPa or the snow load, whichever is more (NBC 9.4.2.3), so in Edmonton the 1.9 kPa governs and in St. John's the snow does. Arc picks:

  • Joists (treated S-P-F, CSA O86 with the wet-service and treated-lumber factors, no more than span/360 of sag) at the spacing you choose (12", 16" or 24"), or the size you choose.
  • A beam set back from the outer edge, with the fewest posts no more than 10' apart; a freestanding deck gets a second beam at the back.
  • 6×6 posts on round concrete piles sized for the heaviest post on firm clay, each reaching the ground under it and taken below your city's frost line; the takeoff counts them by length, and the foundation plan sheet shows them.
  • A ledger when the deck runs along an outside wall (Arc finds it, or set Built), bolted to the rim joist and flashed. Not on brick or stone veneer: Arc tells you to frame it freestanding.
  • The guard the deck needs (over 600 mm up: 900 mm high, 1 070 mm above 1.8 m) and the stairs down to the yard, from the plan's stair limits.

Hover any size for why it's that size. Show the framing on the plan draws the joists, ledger, beam, posts and piles over the deck, and Make the deck permit drawing adds a one-page sheet: the framing plan with its dimensions, a section through the deck, and every size with its reason. The framing lumber, hangers, connectors and piles are in the takeoff. The 3D view builds a raised deck from the same sizes: the ledger on the house, rim joists, joists at their spacing, the beam under them, 6×6 posts and round concrete piles down below the frost line, so an export to Blender has the real frame. These are estimates for a homeowner's permit: your city's deck guide sets the pile depth and the ledger bolts, and an engineer has the last word.

An aerial or drone photo under the plan

The quickest way to get an existing yard in: trace it off a photo from above. File ▾ → Aerial or drone photo… offers two:

  • Your own photo: a drone shot taken straight down, or a screenshot of a satellite map. It comes in see-through; click two points on it a known distance apart (the two front corners of the lot, or both ends of a fence you can measure) and type that distance. It's then to scale: move and turn it onto the lot.
  • The City of Edmonton's aerial photo (2019, about 7.5 cm a pixel), for a lot made with File ▾ → New lot from an Edmonton address…: a 90 m square round the lot, already to scale and turned to the plan's north. It's free to use with credit to the City. It works in the desktop app; the City's map server turns away web pages on other sites, so on the web Arc says so and you bring in your own photo instead.

Select the photo for its panel: Fade to see the plan over it, Set scale… again, and Lock it in place so it doesn't move while you trace. Roofs lean a little in an aerial photo: trace what's on the ground (bed edges, paths, fence posts, tree trunks), not roof edges. The lot lines stay the ones New lot worked out (from the City's open data, never from the photo or SLIM Maps): check them against your Real Property Report.

Buried lines from the locate marks

When the free locate is done (Click Before You Dig in Alberta, 811 in the US), the yard has paint and flags: yellow for gas, red for power, blue for water, green for sewer, orange for phone, internet and cable. Draw them with Outdoor → Buried line: pick the line, type its depth if the locator said, and click along the marks. Each is drawn dashed in its colour with its name along it; select one to see the 1 m hand-dig band either side.

The Design check warns about anything dug within 1 m of a buried line: fence and deck posts, footings, a retaining wall, a patio or path base, a court's base, a tree or shrub's hole, a pond, a sprinkler trench, a shed or a gazebo. In Alberta, within 1 m of a mark you dig by hand or with a hydrovac until the line is showing: no machines, augers or post-hole diggers. Better, move it. Before you build lists the lines drawn and how many things are too close. The locate doesn't mark lines you own (power to a garage, gas to a BBQ, sprinkler mains): draw them as Your own line after a private locator finds them.

Privacy and views

Review ▾ → Privacy and views… answers "what do I plant so they can't see the hot tub". Add a sight line, click the neighbour's window or deck, then the seat in your yard (clicking a hot tub or bench takes its middle). Pick where they look from (an upstairs window, a main-floor window, a raised deck or their yard) and how you sit (a chair, the hot tub, a lounger, standing): each sets the eye's height.

Arc follows the line of sight across the seat, from 1 m to its left to 1 m to its right, past solid fences, buildings, sheds and plants, and says how much of the seat is hidden now, after 5 years and after 15 years, with each plant at its size then and each thing there once its year comes (Yard phases). The Winter column counts only evergreens, fences and buildings: leafy plants are bare from October to May. When it isn't hidden, Arc says how tall a screen 1, 2 or 3 m from the seat would need to be (near the seat a short screen hides the most) and names narrow evergreens and columnar plants hardy where you are that would get there in 5 years. Look from their side in 3D puts the camera at their eye; Now, 5 and 15 years in 3D shows three pictures from there. The sight lines show on the plan, coloured by how hidden the seat is now.

Yard phases: year by year

Most yards are built over two or three summers. Select anything in the yard (a patio, a bed, plants, a fence, a court) and pick Built in: Year 1, 2 or 3 in its panel. Budget ▾ → Yard phases: year by year… then shows each year's estimate (the takeoff's own prices, with its contingency and tax) against a budget you type, and what's left over. Show it shows the plan and the 3D view as the yard stands at the end of that year; Add a sheet for each year adds a site plan sheet per year. The takeoff and Yard: do it or hire it show the split by year too. Things with no year count as this year's.

A sensible order: year 1 the ground, drainage, hard surfaces, sprinkler mains and trees (they take longest to grow); year 2 the beds, shrubs, a deck or pergola; year 3 perennials, lights and a fire pit. Run the sprinkler and light trenches before the sod goes down.

Ducts, pipes and wires (U)

Choose duct, pipe or wire and its kind (supply air, cold water, drain, 120V branch and so on) and size, then click along the route; it snaps to fixtures, devices and walls. Runs are colour-coded and tagged. To draw them in 3D (risers, drops, stacks) use Draw pipes & wires on the 3D toolbar: see Drawing ducts, pipes and wires in 3D. Design ▾ → Pipes & wires: outlets, wiring, plumbing, ducts… drafts outlets, plumbing and ducts for a whole floor.

An exhaust duct out through a basement wall. Select a duct (or pipe) and set End rises to: the run turns up at its end to that height (from this floor), so an exhaust duct from a 3D printer's enclosure, a dryer or a bath fan in a basement rises to its hood above the ground outside; the plan marks the rise UP with its heights and the 3D view shows it. Empty takes the rise away. The Design check says when an exhaust duct meets an outside wall below the ground (a basement wall: the ground outside is taken as 8" below the ground floor), when it reaches the wall with no exhaust hood on the outside face, and when an inline duct fan or printer enclosure has no exhaust duct to an outside wall yet.

Roofs (F)

Watch the house break. Select a roof framed with rafters and click Watch it break: pile on snow under Roof framing. Snow piles up on a section through the roof while a meter shows how much of the rafters' strength it uses (CSA O86, the same check that sized them). It passes your city's design snow, sags past span/240, and at last the rafters' check trips: they turn red, snap, and Why it broke says at how much snow, whether bending or shear gave out, and how many times the design snow that was. Drag the slider to try any depth. Real rafters usually hold more than Arc's check: the code keeps a margin.

Click inside the building and the roof follows the outside walls, or draw its outline. Choose hip, gable, shed, flat, barrel, gambrel or mansard, the pitch, overhangs and ridge direction, the roofing, fascia, soffit and gutters. Roof framing adds rafters or trusses with a lumber list and span checks.

Framed the way it's built. Ceiling joists stop at a vaulted room: they don't run across it. A room whose vault follows the roof above has nothing tying its walls together, so the ridge over it becomes a ridge beam sized for the roof and the snow (CSA O86), on a post at each end down to the foundation: the framing plan, the 3D framing view and the lumber list show the beam and the posts. A vault of its own shape keeps the ridge board, with a note that its rafters need ties higher up. On a truss roof, a dormer or a skylight wider than the truss spacing takes out the trusses it cuts: the truss each side is doubled (a girder truss), doubled 2x6 headers run across at the top and bottom of the opening, and 2x6 rafters and ceiling joists are framed in between. The truss maker designs the girders; the notes call a dormer a dormer.

Select a roof and orange handles show at each end, each eave side and, on an L or T shaped roof, each wing. Click an end to make it a hip, a gable, a dutch gable (a hip with a small gable wall at the top: Small gable starts at says how high) or a clipped gable (a gable with its peak hipped back, a jerkinhead: Clip starts at), and give a hip end its own pitch. Click a side to give just that side its own pitch (a steep front and a long low back, say: the ridge moves to where the slopes meet), or a wing to give that wing its own pitch. Same pitch as the roof puts it back.

A gable over one wing, and a finish for each gable. On an L or T roof, click a wing and set Ridge to Turned the other way to turn just that wing: a garage's gable to the street, with the rest of the roof as it was and a valley where they meet. A wing against a taller wall leans on it as a shed; set its Shape to Gable for two slopes and a whole gable at each end (a garage beside a two-storey house), or to Shed to tuck it against the wall. Where one wing runs on through another and pokes out the far wall, click that end and pick Runs into the roof beside it: the wing stops under the other's ridge and they meet in valleys. Where a garage steps out at the front, wider than the part behind it, turn the front wing so one gable spans the whole front, and make that wing's back end a gable: it stands on the roof behind. Then click each of its ends to make it a gable, hip, dutch or clipped gable. An inside end made a gable (where the wing stands over a lower roof) gets its whole gable wall, standing on the lower roof. Click a gable end (on the plan, or in 3D with the roof selected) and pick This gable's finish to give just that gable its own siding or stone and colour: one front gable in charcoal stacked shale with the rest in cream stone, say. The others keep the roof's Gable ends finish; a lower wing's gable inside a bigger one on the same wall takes the bigger one's unless you give it its own. Each gable's finish is in the estimate as that siding's square feet, and noted on the elevations.

Gambrel and mansard roofs. A Gambrel is a barn roof: a steep slope from the walls, then a gentler one to the ridge, with gable ends. A Mansard has steep sides on every wall and a low top, so a whole storey fits in the roof. Set the Lower pitch (the steep part), the Upper pitch and how high above the walls the Slope changes at. The framing puts a plate at the break for the upper rafters and lists it in the lumber, and the eaves, gutters and eave protection follow the steep lower slope.

Trim and flared eaves. Tick Frieze board under the eaves for a 1×8 board along the top of the walls, Rake boards up the gable ends for a 1×6 under the roof's edge, and Eave returns at the gables to carry the eave's soffit and fascia about 2' round each corner onto the gable with a small flashed cap. Flared (bellcast) eaves (hip and gable roofs) kick the roof out flatter over the overhang, like the rim of a bell: best with an overhang of 18" or more. Each adds its lines to the estimate (the flare as a sprocket on each rafter tail).

Gable ends, deep edges and gravel roofs. A gable or shed roof's Gable ends can have their own finish (cedar shingles or board and batten over lap siding, say). Fascia goes up to a 2×12 and to 16" and 24" slab edges for flat roofs, and Roofing has Gravel (ballasted flat roof). On siding, an outside wall's Corner boards put a trim board up each corner in the colour you pick.

Finishes where a builder puts them. Arc puts a few things where a builder would, by itself:

  • Inside an outside wall is drywall, not the siding, brick or stone colour carried through the wall. Paint the wall, or give that face a finish, and that shows instead.
  • Corner boards go only at real corners. An outside wall drawn in two pieces in a straight line gets none where the pieces meet: the siding runs straight through.
  • Stone and siding: a two-tone wall (stone below, siding above) gets a cut-stone cap along the top of the stone, which sheds water, stopping at doors.
  • Sills, drip caps and flashing: each outside window gets a sloped sill (cut stone in brick, stone or stucco) and a metal drip cap over its head trim; a porch roof or a lower roof gets flashing where it meets the wall, and a dormer gets flashing where its walls meet the roof. They show in the Real and Balanced looks when the view is still, in renders and in exports, not in the Simple look or while you turn the view, so it stays quick.
  • Trim stops at cabinets, tubs, showers and fireplaces (see Trim with real profiles).
  • A design check warns when a cabinet, a tall built-in or a fireplace runs into the ceiling: under a sloped ceiling's low side, a beam, a tray's edge or a coffer, or a ceiling set lower than it is tall. A piece made to reach the ceiling that just meets it is fine.

Gable walls and a roof that keeps up with the walls. In Arc and ArcRes the wall under each gable end is built up to the roof the way a framer builds it: a real wall, as thick as the wall below, with the wall's siding running up into the gable (or the roof's own Gable ends finish above the top plate), and a window can go up into it past the wall's height. A roof keeps the outline of the walls it was made from, so when you move a wall or add a part after roofing, a card comes up at once: Re-fit the roof gives the roof the walls' new outline and keeps its style, pitch and trim (one undo step), and the roof's panel shows the same warning until it fits. A wall on the same floor taller or shorter than where the roof sits (Sits on walls at, else the height most of the floor's walls are) gets a card too, with the fix: make it that height, or give that part a roof of its own with its own Sits on walls at.

Smooth curves and bends. A driveway or path is one strip along its whole line, its edges meeting at every bend with no gaps. Tick Smooth curve (in the Outdoor tool before you draw, or in a selected path's panel) and it bends gently through the points you click instead. Landscape areas and patios have Smooth curve too: a gravel or garden bed's edge rounds through the corners you click.

Clicks far apart make a long gentle sweep, and clicks close together make a tight bend, so one path can do both and swing one way and back.

Closed loops and figure 8s. Click the first point again (or press Close the loop when drawing a road in 3D, or tick Closed loop in a selected path's panel) and the end joins the start with no kink. A figure 8 is a loop that crosses itself: click round one ring, through the middle, round the other, and back to the first point. A loop stays level unless you set heights at its bends. A path printed as an insert can't cross itself yet: the two pieces would overlap there, and the print check says so.

Pattern follows the bends. On a model, cobblestones and other path patterns turn with the path, so the joints run along the road instead of one way across the board. New paths do this; an older path keeps its look until you tick Pattern follows the bends in its panel. Stones squeeze a little on the inside of a tight bend and spread on the outside, as they do on a real road.

Porches and covered decks (G)

Two clicks along the house and one for the depth. Start on the house wall (its line, a face or a corner) and the porch sits against the wall's outside face.

Set the floor (concrete, pavers or deck boards) and its height (a minus height, like -7", sets the porch a step or more below the house's floor, with the steps down from the door counted), the columns, the roof (shed, hip, gable, flat or open rafters), ceiling, railings and steps.

The beam along the front columns is sized for the widest gap between columns and the roof and snow on it (lumber first, then LVL or glulam), and the 3D porch draws it that size. The design check looks at where the porch roof meets the house: on a one-storey wall it has to stay under the main roof's eave, on a taller wall it has to clear the window above by 4" for the flashing, and a window or door behind the porch has to stay under the porch roof's ledger. Each warning says what to change.

Where the columns go. Column layout in the porch panel: One every so far puts columns along the front and down the sides no farther apart than Columns up to; Front corners only puts one at each front corner and none between.
