Either way the panel and the parts list show the beam the columns carry, sized for its span, the roof on it and your town's snow, and warn when no wood beam holds that span.

One piece at a time. Select the porch and orange handles show on each column and on the steps. Click one: the panel shows Selected piece. Drag a column to move it, press Delete to take it out, or use + Add a column; from then on the porch keeps your columns (Back to automatic spaces them evenly again). Drag the steps to slide them along the front, and untick any side under Railing on. A deck (Outdoor tool) works the same way: click the handle in the middle of a side to turn its railing or posts off.

Framing: posts, beams, studs and joists (J)

The Framing button on the left (or J, or Draw ▾ → Post, Beam, Stud, Joist) draws one structural piece where you click, at its real size. Pick the kind at the top of the panel: a Post or Stud goes where you click; a Beam or Joist runs from where you click to where you click next (or type its length). Choose the material (lumber, LVL, glulam, I-joist, steel or concrete) and the size (2x4 to 2x12, 4x4 to 8x8, LVLs, glulams, W-beams, HSS and lally columns…); a lumber or LVL beam can be 2, 3 or 4 plies together. Posts and studs stand from the floor to the wall tops unless you give them a height; beams and joists sit with their tops level with the walls unless you set how far their bottom is off the floor. Joists can be a row: How many and their Spacing (they repeat to the left of the line you draw).

Select one to change it in the panel like a wall: material, size, plies, length, height, turn. A beam or joist has an orange handle at each end to stretch it. They show in the 3D view (and the framing views) at their size and colour, on the Structural layer, and in the takeoff as Structural members, by the foot, with typical prices. Have an engineer size beams and posts that carry loads.

Span check: a joist or beam drawn this way is checked against Arc's rough span table (the same one roof and floor framing use: #2 SPF, house loads). Too long a span shows in the Design check with a one-click Make it a bigger size, or another ply on a beam. Outside every room a joist counts as a deck joist (a little less span for wet wood). It's a rough estimate: have the lumber yard or an engineer size beams before you build.

Paint and materials (K)

Pick a material by its picture (paints, woods, marbles, stone, tile, brick, metals, fabrics, wallpapers, glass), then click walls, room floors or furniture on the plan or in 3D. Custom paint color… mixes any colour; Import paint colours… brings in your own fan deck (see Your paint colours); Add a photo material… turns your own photo of a tile or fabric into a material (see Your own photos as materials). The eraser takes a material off again. A click on a wall paints only the side you clicked, and only the stretch facing that room, so the room next door keeps its colour. One click paints in the panel does more at once: the whole wall (both sides), every wall in that room, every room on this floor or the whole house (the inside faces only: outside faces keep their siding). With those, clicking a room's floor does the floors of those rooms too. A selected wall's Interior finish shows what each side has.

Decorate (Y)

Pick a colour palette and click a room: its walls, an accent wall, the trim and baseboards, the ceiling and its curtains, pillows, art and rugs take the palette's colours. See Decorating.

Blocks library (B)

Shelves. The library opens with a row of shelves over its categories: Kitchen & bath, Living & bedroom, Decor & styling, Seasonal, Outdoor, Building services, Commercial (commercial projects) and Fun & people, with Mine for the blocks and models you saved. Click a shelf to see just its categories; the Category list under it then lists only those. All shows everything, and the search box always looks through every shelf.

Furniture, cabinets, appliances, bathroom fixtures, closets and built-ins, lights, people, cars and plants. Pick a category and an item, choose its size and finish, then click to place it; most items turn to sit against the nearest wall. Cabinets join side by side and get a countertop. Select a placed item to change its size, finish and style, or save a copy as your own. Sofas, beds, tables and vanities have more choices: legs (wood, metal or skirted) and seat cushions; a footboard and the bedding colour; a trestle, pedestal or hairpin base; a vanity with doors, drawers, floating on the wall or on furniture legs, and the faucet and pull finish. Search (Ctrl+K) finds any block by name.

Home gym and 3D printing. The Fitness group has a treadmill, an exercise bike (spin, upright or recumbent), a rowing machine, an elliptical, a power rack with its barbell and plates, an adjustable weight bench, a dumbbell rack with its weights, a yoga or exercise mat (flat or rolled), a gym wall mirror (36" to 96" wide) and a hanging punching bag, each at its real size. Hobby & 3D printing has an enclosed 3D printer (Bambu and Prusa sizes), an open-frame printer, a resin printer with its wash station, a printer enclosure with a 4" exhaust port on the back, and a filament dry box with its spools. The printers and the enclosure start 30" up, to sit on a desk or bench; set the height in the panel. To take the printer's fumes outside, Mechanical has an inline duct fan, a round duct (stretch it to the length of the run) and a louvred exhaust hood for the outside wall: see Finish a basement. Also new: a standing desk, a dehumidifier, a floor-mounted tub filler for a freestanding tub, a basement egress window well, and three styles of counter stool (a pedestal base, four wood legs, or a low back; the base follows the finish).

Range hoods and columns made your way (My range hoods, My columns). Arc and ArcRes (columns in Arc Landscape too). Select a Range hood or an Island range hood (Kitchen), a Porch column (Exterior), a Column (inside) or a Pilaster (column on a wall) (Wall panels & trim), or pick one in the Blocks tool, and the panel shows a picture of each style to start from:

  • Range hoods (20): a modern box, a slim floating box, the tapered chimney hood, a wide pro hood, a curved bell in stainless or in plaster, the chimney hood Arc always drew (stainless or matte black), a sloped plaster canopy to the ceiling (for an 8′ or a 10′ ceiling), wood-clad with iron straps, plaster with brass straps, a wood mantel hood with corbels, plaster with a crown and shelf, a riveted zinc hood, a French swoop whose outline you draw yourself, a hood hidden between the cabinets, a round island hood, a glass canopy and a flush ceiling hood. Picking one sizes the hood and sets its finish.
  • Columns and posts (24): round, slim round, Tuscan, Doric (fluted), Ionic (scrolls) and Corinthian (leaves), a square 6×6 post, a boxed column, a panelled box column, an octagonal column, a tapered craftsman column, craftsman on a stone base, paired columns on a stone pier, craftsman on a brick pier, a tall stone pier with a short column, a turned porch post whose outline you draw, timber posts with straight or curved knee braces or arched brackets, a rough-sawn timber post, a timber post on a stone base, round and square steel posts, and a Tuscan pilaster.
  • Change this range hood… or Change this column… opens the sliders window: the shape, width, depth and height, how much it narrows to the top, the chimney's height and width, the band along the bottom, a mantel shelf and corbels under it, crown moulding along the top, metal straps and their metal, rivets, the liner under the hood and the finish; for a column, its base, its top (flat, moulded, a flared capital, Ionic or Corinthian), flutes up a round shaft, raised panels on a square one, a pair side by side, flat against a wall (a pilaster), a stone or brick pier's height, width and colour, and knee braces (straight, curved or solid arched brackets). A photo or material instead puts one of the plan's photo materials, or one of My materials, on the hood or column. The window says what you might have missed: a hood too low or too high over the cooktop, narrower than the cooktop, or a plaster or wood hood that needs its fan insert picked first; a column too spindly, a pier taller than half the column, flutes too shallow to show, a squat Corinthian capital, or a pilaster mistaken for a support.
  • Draw your own: a round or classical column's Shaft shape, a turned post's outline and a hood's Side outline (pick the shape Your own outline) are drawings of dots: drag a dot out or in, + Add a dot for more detail (it goes half way along the longest stretch) and − Take a dot away (the one you last moved). Each change is the hood's or column's own as you let go.
  • My stone (base): a column on a stone base can use one of My stones, as a wall's siding can. So can the Porch post on a stone base block, and a Custom pergola with its posts on stone bases.
  • Save as My range hood… or Save as My column… keeps it in the plan and in My library; your other editions see it too. A porch's Or one of My columns (under its column style) puts a preset or one of yours on every column of the porch.
  • The block's own Width, Depth, Height and Finish change that one hood or column. A hood's Height runs from the bottom of the hood to the top of its chimney. Plain range hood or Plain column puts back the one Arc always drew; hoods and columns in older plans are unchanged until you pick a style.

Your own blocks. Select furniture, fixtures, lines and 3D shapes together and use Save as my block…: a custom piece (a built-in bench, a table from a top and legs) goes into My blocks, in 3D as well as on the plan. Edit the drawing takes one apart to change it, on its own: everything else fades and can't be moved, and Save as block on the bar puts it back together (Cancel puts the original back). When the same block is used elsewhere in the plan (or came from My blocks), saving asks whether to Update it everywhere (every copy takes the new shape, keeping its own place, turn and size, and My blocks is updated) or Save as a new block (only this one changes).

Manufacturers' products. While placing a block, Import a 3D model… brings in a maker's model as GLB, glTF, OBJ, FBX, COLLADA (DAE), 3DS, STL or 3MF (SketchUp files: export them from SketchUp as COLLADA, OBJ or FBX first). Import a product list (CSV)… reads a spreadsheet from a supplier, or one you make: each row (Name, Item, Width, Depth, Height, Manufacturer, Model, Price, Link, Finish) becomes one of My products at its exact size, drawn like the closest item (a sofa looks like a sofa), with its price and link carried into the shopping list and quote. Save an example list… gives a sheet to fill in.

Plaster walls: lime plaster, limewash and Venetian plaster. Arc, ArcRes and Arc Landscape. In a wall's Interior finish, pick Plaster, limewash or Venetian, then one of the ready-made ones: lime plaster, limewash in five colours, Venetian plaster in four, Roman clay and tadelakt (polished, waterproof lime for showers). Movement, right there, sets how much the trowel or brush marks and clouds show. Change this plaster… also sets its colour, the size of the marks and its sheen (matte to burnished), and Save as My plaster… keeps it in the plan and in My library. Changing one gives that wall its own copy; walls you then give the same finish with Apply to all walls… share it. The estimate prices it by the sq ft, supplied and applied by a plasterer (estimates: limewash about $5, lime plaster $12, Venetian $18, tadelakt $30 a sq ft). The older Lime plaster and Limewash colours under Stone or brick are still there, as they were. Wallpaper has twelve more: grasscloth in navy and sage, champagne silk, woven linen, a brass-on-navy geometric, a trellis, chinoiserie, a charcoal and gold damask, a wide stripe, a fine herringbone, cork, and a Rocky Mountains mural.

Your paint colours (import a fan deck). Arc comes with no paint maker's colours: you bring the ones you use. In a painted wall's Interior finish, or the Paint tool, click Import paint colours…, then paste a list or open a CSV or text file: one colour a line, with its name, its code and its colour as a hex code (#F5F3E9) or as red, green and blue (245, 243, 233), with or without a header row. Arc shows what it found; type the Maker and a name for the list, and Keep in My materials. Then a wall's My paint colours finds one as you type its name or code, and the Paint tool's From My library… lists them too. Where to get your list: each colour's page on the maker's site (Benjamin Moore, Sherwin-Williams, Cloverdale Paint, General Paint) shows its name, code and colour, and their stores and apps can share the ones you chose. Screen colours are close, not exact: always try a real sample on your wall, in your light.

My library. File ▾ → My library… lists everything you keep for other plans, from every edition on this computer: blocks, products, 3D models you imported, printed parts, fonts, palettes, templates, golf obstacles, your own plants, materials (your paint colours and photo materials), wall types, and windows and doors. Each edition keeps its own; Use in … copies one from another edition. To keep one of your colours or photos, pick it in the Paint tool (My colors & photos) and click Keep in My library (a photo is kept as a small tile, not the whole photo); a wall type or a window or door, in Wall types or Windows and doors. From My library… in the same three places copies one into the plan you have open: the plan gets its own copy, so it opens the same anywhere, and a change there changes only that plan. If the device is full, Arc says so and nothing is lost.

My models. A 3D model you imported can be kept for your other plans. Select it and click Keep in My models… (under Parts and colours): Arc keeps its name, a small picture, its real size, how many triangles it has, how big its file is, and a category. To use it, open File ▾ → My library…, pick My models, and click Use in this plan; or click it under My models in the Blocks library. Models you kept in another edition show there too. The models are kept on this computer, not in the installer. A model already in a plan gets the same size check as an import: when it looks the wrong size for its name (a sofa 70' across, brought in with the wrong units), its panel says so, with Make it … to put it right or Keep this size. Lesson: Your own 3D models.

  • One file, many copies. A model is stored once in a plan however many copies stand in it, so ten dining chairs make the file no bigger than one. Importing the same file again uses the copy already there.
  • The size check. When you import a model, Arc compares its size with the thing its name says it is. If it is far off, it asks: "This sofa would be 14' across. A sofa is usually about 7'. Was it drawn in centimetres?" One click fixes the units; Keep leaves it as it is.
  • Parts and colours. The panel lists the model's parts (its materials), each with a dot of its colour. Give a part an Arc finish (a wood, a fabric, a leather, a metal) or a colour of your own; As imported puts it back. Only that copy changes. A picture on the model (a fabric pattern, say) comes in with it; very large pictures are kept at 2,048 pixels across.
  • Use it as a product. Type its Manufacturer, Model, Price and Link in the Product box (or in My models with Details…). A model with those goes on the shopping list and gets its own line in the cost estimate at your price. The price is in the money your Arc shows (Canadian dollars unless you changed it); a model priced in other money says so when you use it.
  • My blocks with a model inside. A block you save from a selection that includes an imported model now keeps the model with it, so the block works in your other plans too.

The library folds into groups. Each group is one line: its name, how many pieces it has, and three quick pieces to click: the one you used last, then the ones you use most (until you've placed some, the usual ones: a range, fridge and sink in Kitchen, a toilet, vanity and shower in Bathroom). Click the group's name (▸) to see everything in it; Arc remembers which groups you leave open. Open all and Fold all are at the top, and typing in the search box or picking a category opens just the groups that match.

How a cabinet is built (Arc and ArcRes). A cabinet's Cabinet style has Construction: Frameless (the doors cover the box, as Arc has always drawn them), Framed (a face frame shows round each door) or Inset (the doors sit flush inside the frame). Wall and tall cabinets take a Crown shape (square, cove, classic or a stacked crown) once Crown molding is ticked, a Light rail under wall cabinets, and a Stacked upper: a shorter row of doors on top, in glass if you like. The crown and light rail run along the whole row of cabinets as one piece and turn back to the wall at each end. Base and tall cabinets have a Toe kick height and recess, or a Furniture base with feet. Door styles now include Slim shaker, Fluted and Cane insert, and fronts include glass with mullions, reeded glass, a plate rack, wine cubbies and wicker baskets. A door with a frame (shaker, beaded inset, slim shaker, cane, any glass door) is drawn as it is made, the sides running the full height with the top and bottom rails between them; Frame corners changes that to the top and bottom running the full width, or to mitred corners (a cane door starts mitred).

Fillers, panels and corners. The Cabinets group has a Filler or scribe strip (to close the gap at a wall or an appliance), a Finished end panel for the end of a run (plain, or framed like the doors), an Appliance panel to put on a panel-ready dishwasher or fridge, a lazy Susan corner and diagonal corner cabinets. Crown and light rail carry across a filler or an end panel. A lazy Susan's countertop joins the countertops along both of its walls, and the automatic backsplash covers both walls behind a corner cabinet.

Specialty insides. Open Specialty insides on a cabinet and tick what goes in it. Only the ones that fit that cabinet are listed: a cutlery divider, a spice drawer insert, a knife block, a charging drawer, a peg drawer for plates, a pot and pan organizer, pull-out garbage and recycling, a spice and oil pull-out, a utensil pull-out, tray dividers, roll-out shelves, a mixer lift, a tip-out sink tray, an under-sink caddy, a blind corner pull-out, a towel bar, a toe-kick drawer, pantry pull-outs, lift-up door stays, lights behind glass doors and a pull-down shelf. Show it open in 3D draws the drawer pulled out with the organizer in it, for a picture. Each one is on the cabinet schedule and is counted in the cost estimate with a typical Canadian price for the hardware (checked against Lee Valley, Rona and other Canadian stores in November 2026; type your own price in the estimate when you have a quote).

Built-ins. A closet or built-in (bookcase, media wall, lockers, window seat, desk) has the same choices under How it is built: construction, a door style, frame corners, a furniture base and a crown. Set a section to Your own to decide what is in it: how many drawers and how tall (above the lower doors, like a sideboard, or stacked from the bottom when there are no lower doors), doors (on the lower part, the whole section, glass, or solid below with glass above) and how high the lower part reaches, a ledge on top of it, shelves by number or at heights you type (42, 56, 70), and a hanging rod.

Staging and seasonal decor

Pieces that make a model feel like a home, for showing it to people. They're never priced: the takeoff, the shopping list, room costs and quotes leave them out. They sit on their own Staging & seasonal decor layer, so one click on the layer's eye hides them all (for the drawings, say) and shows them again.

CelebrationsCanada Day (a porch flag, bunting), summer (café string lights, tiki torches, lemonade, beach towels, a hose reel, a kiddie pool, sand toys, a window box, a hammock), back to school (backpacks, a homework station, a first-day sign), Hanukkah (a lit menorah, dreidels and gelt, a blue and silver garland), Diwali (diya lamps, a rangoli, a marigold toran, paper lanterns), Lunar New Year (red lanterns, door couplets, a kumquat tree, a fu hanging), birthdays (a balloon arch and cluster, a banner, a party table, a piñata), graduation, New Year's and Pride

Countertop pieces start at counter height and table pieces at table height; change Off the floor to set one on a shelf, a mantel or a coffee table. Wall pieces (wreaths, garlands, stockings, bats) turn to sit against the nearest wall. Each takes a colour from its swatches, and many have a second colour (the ornaments, the roses, the throws). The Christmas tree is drawn at its real size on the plan, so you can see whether it fits beside the sofa or blocks a walkway, on the plan and walking through in 3D. Staging pieces never raise design-check warnings. Search the library by everyday words too: xmas, spooky, bday, wfh.

Stage rooms for showing. Design ▾ → Stage rooms for showing… (or Stage this room… in a room's panel) sets out staging that suits each room by its name: the coffee maker, knife block, utensils and herbs on the kitchen counters and a fruit bowl on the island, a table set for dinner with a centrepiece, a tray, frames and a bowl in the living room and a blanket ladder by the wall, books, glasses and a carafe on the nightstands, the desk setup in the office, soap and towels in the bathrooms, and a doormat, coats on hooks, shoes and an umbrella stand in the entry. Choose this room, every room on the floor or the whole house, and a season or holiday for its decor in the rooms it suits: the tree (stepping down to a smaller size if it must) with the gifts under it, the wreath in the entry, the centrepiece on the dining table. Pieces go on the furniture that's there, against a free stretch of wall or on clear floor away from the doors, so furnish the rooms first; anything with no place is listed. Remove staging takes out what it set out, and it all undoes in one step.

Showing it without the decor. Photo mode has Show staging & seasonal decor, and the share page has a tick for it, so a client can see the house dressed or plain.

Work on just this part

Select what you want to work on (click it, or drag a box round it) and click ◎ Work on this part at the top of the panel (or Modify ▾ → Work on just this part). Only those pieces can be picked and changed: the rest of the floor fades on the plan and leaves the 3D view, so nothing else moves by accident. A wall's doors and windows come along with it, and anything you draw joins the part. You're changing the real plan, so nothing needs copying back: Done keeps it all, Cancel puts back everything changed since you started. Edit the drawing on a block opens it this way too, with Save as block on the bar.

Pieces and handles. With one thing selected, its pieces show orange handles: a porch's columns and steps, each side of a deck, the corners of a duct, pipe or wire run, and each end and wing of a roof. Click a handle to edit that piece in the panel, drag it to move it, Delete to remove it. The panel says so too when a thing has pieces.

The quick bar. Whatever you select gets a small bar just above it: Lock, Move, Turn 90° (a quarter turn each click, round its middle), Edit (work on just this part) and Delete. For furniture, cabinets or fixtures near a wall it also has Face the room: the back goes against the nearest wall and the front faces into the room, which fixes one that came in backwards from a converted drawing or another plan. Turn it off in Preferences → Drawing if you'd rather not see it.

Lock part of the drawing. Select the part to keep still (click it, or drag a box round it) and click Lock on the quick bar (or Ctrl+L, or Modify ▾ → Lock the selection together). Everything picked is locked together as one group, with a dashed outline and a padlock: it can't be picked, dragged or box-selected, and walls joined to it leave it where it is, so you can move everything else around it freely. To change it again, click any part of it and choose Unlock: the whole group is freed at once and comes back selected. Modify ▾ → Unlock everything (Ctrl+Shift+L) frees every group. Locks are saved with the plan and undo like any change.

Groups. Select two or more things and press Ctrl+G (or Modify ▾ → Group, or right-click → Group). From then on a click on any piece picks up the whole group, so it moves, turns, copies and deletes as one. Alt+click picks just one piece of it. With the group selected, the panel shows its Name, Ungroup (Ctrl+Shift+G) and Lock the group. Group a group with other things and it goes inside the new one; Ungroup takes apart only the outer group. A copy of a whole group is a group of its own, and one piece copied on its own comes out loose. Groups are saved with the plan.

Components (linked blocks). Select one of your blocks (or some lines and 3D shapes) and click Make component in its panel (or Modify ▾ → Make component). Every copy of it on the plan, and every copy you make after, stays linked. Edit component opens its drawing on its own, as Edit the drawing does; Save component changes every copy at once. Each copy keeps its own place, turn, size and floor, and can have its own name (This copy's name). Select all copies picks them all, and Unlink this copy makes one a plain block that no longer changes. The fixtures schedule counts a component's copies in one row. Components are kept in the plan (not in My blocks), so they go with it, also when you copy and paste into another plan. A plan with groups or components needs Arc 0.86.0 or newer to open; older plans open as before.

Vanities, showers and wood cabinets. A vanity's panel has Sinks (Auto puts two on a vanity 60" or longer; or choose one or two) and, with one sink, where it sits: in the middle or a third of the way in from either end, for a long top with the sink to one side. A shower's Framed door style is a hinged door in a metal frame, with Door glass clear, frosted or in frosted stripes and a Door frame in chrome, brass, oil-rubbed bronze, almond or black. Cabinets and vanities can be Honey oak or Cherry with the wood's grain showing in 3D, and in natural oak, maple, walnut or espresso they show their grain too. How much grain shows, under the finish, goes from None through 100% (as it comes) to strong, for that cabinet; Apply style to all cabinets takes it to the rest of the kitchen.

Metal sheens, lacquer and grain on furniture. Cabinet hardware, faucets and light fittings that come in brass can also be brass, bronze, nickel or blackened steel, each polished (a mirror shine), satin (a soft shine), brushed (fine lines one way) or aged (worn, darker; bronze is oil-rubbed). Cabinets can also be high-gloss lacquer in white, black or navy, with a clear coat that reflects the room. Wood furniture (tables, beds, dressers, desks, shelves) in natural oak, maple, walnut or espresso shows its wood grain in plans started since this version, with a How much grain shows slider under its finish; plans made before keep their furniture plain until you move the slider. All of it shows in 3D and in Photo renders.

Photographed finishes. The Paint tool's Stone, Wood, Brick & concrete, Fabric and Metal lists end with real photographed materials, marked (photo): Carrara, Calacatta and black marbles, quartzite, travertine, limestone and granite; herringbone and wide-plank oak and walnut floors, and oak and walnut for furniture; smooth plaster, limewash in three colours, polished and board-formed concrete; cognac, white and black leather, linen, bouclé and wool; aged brass, bronze and brushed nickel, stainless or brass. Paint them on walls, floors and furniture like any material; the stones and concretes are in a counter's Countertop list too, and polished stone has a clear shine. Each loads the first time it's used (a moment later in 3D, which shows the drawn look until then), and they're free (CC0) photos from ambientCG and Poly Haven. Preferences → Photographed ground and finishes turns them off with the ground photos.

Countertops: patterns and edges. A base cabinet's Countertop list has quartz (white or grey), marbles (soft veins, or bold Calacatta), granites (black, grey, brown, speckled white, gold and green-black), laminates (beige, grey, white and almond, with a fine fleck), butcher block and concrete, each drawn with its pattern in 3D: granite's crystals, marble's veins, butcher block's strips running the length of the counter. Any of your photo materials is in the list too (Photo: …), for a slab you photographed at the stone yard. Edge shapes the front: Square (as before), Bevel, Half bullnose, Full bullnose, Ogee, or Rolled (a post-formed laminate top that rolls over the front and turns up into a coved splash at the wall). Shaped edges add a Countertop edge line to the estimate by the foot of front edge (in Canada, bevel $5, half bullnose $6, bullnose $14, ogee $24.50 a foot to start; type your fabricator's price over it).

Countertop slabs (My countertop slabs). Arc and ArcRes. Under a counter's Countertop row, Slab draws it as real slabs of stone, quartz or concrete: Calacatta, Calacatta gold, Carrara, Statuario and Nero Marquina marble, Taj Mahal quartzite, soapstone, black granite, white quartz, Calacatta-look quartz and cast concrete. Setting it on one cabinet sets the whole counter it's part of; an island's top is one slab. In 3D and Photo renders the slab is laid along the counter at its real size, with a fine seam where one slab meets the next; polished stone shines under a clear coat. Waterfall turns the slab down the left end, the right end or both, to the floor, with its veins carrying on over the edge. Change this slab… sets the base colour, the vein colour and strength, the size of the pattern, its movement (straight veins to swirling ones), honed or polished, where the first seam falls, and book-matched (each second slab the mirror of the one before, so the veins meet at the seam), with notes on what you might have missed (marble etches; a waterfall needs a mitred corner); Save as My countertop slab… keeps it in the plan and in My library. The estimate counts the slabs at their standard size (about 120" × 65" for marble, 130" × 65" for quartz), from the counter's area and its waterfalls plus a quarter for cuts and matching, in pairs when book-matched, and adds the fabrication and install by the sq ft. Concrete is cast, so it's by the sq ft. The prices are estimates in Canadian dollars: slab prices vary a lot, so pick yours at the stone yard and type its price over. A counter with no slab draws and prices just as before.

Move (M), Duplicate (C), Rotate (Q), Mirror (X) and Delete

Select something first. Move and Duplicate: click a base point, then where it goes (or type a distance); Duplicate keeps making copies until Esc. Rotate: click the centre, then the angle (or type it); tick Rotate a copy to keep the original. Mirror: click two points on the mirror line; tick Keep the original for a mirrored copy. To copy into another plan use Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V (more).

Dormers

Draw ▾ → Dormer (on a sloped roof), then click the roof slope where the dormer's front wall meets it (on the level the roof is on). It stands up out of the roof with a front wall, side walls down to the roof and its own roof, and a window in the front. In the panel: Gable (a small peaked roof) or Shed (one shallow slope, for a wide dormer), its width, front wall height, roof pitch, overhang and window. Its walls take the siding of the wall below and its roof the main roof's finish, and it follows the roof when you change the roof. Move slides it along the slope.

A room in the dormer. A new dormer opens into the room: the main roof is cut under it, and inside it has drywall on its walls, a ceiling under its own roof and jambs round its window, so a bedroom or bonus room in the roof gets its headroom and light. The roof framing is headed off round it (doubled headers at its front and back, doubled rafters each side), its own rafters, ridge, valleys and wall studs go on the framing plan and in the lumber list, and the estimate counts it: its roofing in place of the roof it stands on, its walls, window, insulation, drywall and step flashing. A dormer from before keeps standing on the roof until you tick Opens into the room (cuts the roof under it) in its panel. It has to sit on one slope (not across a hip, valley or ridge) and be at least 2' wide; the panel says if it can't open. A bedroom's dormer window still needs to meet egress: check its size.

The ceiling under a dormer. A dormer that opens into the room opens it in every view: the 3D view, the Render window, exported models for Blender or SketchUp, and sections on the sheets. The room's ceiling under it is cut and lined up to the roof. Over a vaulted ceiling the room gets the dormer's headroom and light. Over a flat ceiling it opens as a light well: a shaft from the ceiling up to the dormer, like a skylight's. That can be built, but it brings light, not headroom, and its window is out of reach, so it can't be a window to climb out of in a fire; the shaft's walls face the cold attic, so they're insulated and air-sealed like outside walls (the estimate counts them). The dormer's panel says which it is. For headroom, give the room a vaulted ceiling that follows the roof. A dormer that opens into the room also has a fascia and soffit under its overhangs and metal flashing across its front and up its sides, in 3D and in the estimate. A 3D print of the house keeps the roof whole.

Skylights

Draw ▾ → Skylight (on a roof), then click the roof where it goes (on the level the roof is on). It cuts the roof and, under a flat ceiling, opens a light shaft down to the ceiling. In the panel: Fixed, Venting (it opens for air) or a Sun tunnel (a small dome and a tube of light for a hall or a bathroom); its width and length up the roof; on a curb or low on the roof deck; a shaft straight down, flared to spread the light, or none under a vaulted ceiling; and its U-value. It follows the roof when the roof changes, and Move slides it. Skylights have their own tags (S1, S2…) in the window schedule, a row each on the NBC 9.36 worksheet against the skylight table, a share of the heat loss, a line in the estimate and a place in the IFC file. They don't count for bedroom egress. Fit one between the rafters where you can (22 1/2" fits 24" spacing): a wider one needs the roof framing headed off, and on a truss roof that is the truss supplier's design. In a stick-framed roof Arc sizes the headers round it, and checks the rafter each side (the trimmers) for its own roof and the headers' ends: doubled when that holds, tripled when it needs it (one more rafter in the takeoff), and a warning to have an engineer size them when even three don't.

Terraces, floor openings, trellises and chimneys

In Draw ▾. Click the corners of the outline (click the first corner again, or press Enter, to finish). A terrace is a cantilevered slab with a parapet or railing; a floor opening cuts the floor, open to the floor below; a trellis is an open pergola; a chimney rises up past the roof.

Open to below. A floor opening takes the floor out: the slab, the joists (framed round it with headers and trimmers) and the subfloor in the estimate stop there, and the room under it opens up to the ceiling above, in 3D, sections and Blender renders. A plan template's "open to below" (over a foyer or a great room) is a real floor opening. On an upper floor, select a room and press Make it open to below to turn it into one. Any edge open to the floor below needs a guard: the Design check flags one without a guard rail or a wall along it, and its Fix adds the rail.

One tall wall. A two-storey room's wall is drawn on each floor. Select the lower one and tick One tall wall up through the floor above: the two are framed and counted as one wall, with studs from this floor to the top of the wall above and that wall's windows framed in it. Past 3 m Arc checks the studs for wind and load; long or engineered studs may be needed. The floor framing notes that the joists hang off the tall wall on a ledger.

3D shapes (push/pull)

Draw ▾ → 3D shape: draw a rectangle, circle or any outline and it becomes a solid. In the 3D view drag its top to change the height, its bottom to lift it, or a side to push it in or out. Corners and edges of the top can be raised for slopes; the panel sets taper, a dome, glass, holes and merging or cutting several shapes.

Engrave or raise a pattern: in the Shape tool's panel set Drawn inside a shape to Engraves it into the top or Raises it from the top, set the depth (0.6 mm is three 0.2 mm print layers: crisp, and it prints in one colour), then draw a rectangle, circle or any outline inside a 3D shape's top: a snowflake, a star, initials, a border. It's cut into the shape (or stands on it) and moves, copies and prints with it; Undo takes it back out. Keep lines at least 0.8 mm wide so a 0.4 mm nozzle can print them. It works on flat tops; on a sloped top it cuts from the lowest corner.

Bevel edges (in the shape's panel) eases the edges where the top meets the sides, like Blender's Bevel tool: type how wide (say 2 mm), then pick Chamfer (a flat 45° cut) or Round (a quarter-round), and tick The bottom edges too if you want them eased as well. It shows in 3D and prints in the STL; holes get the same eased rim, and a tapered or sloped top keeps its bevel. Turned, swept and domed shapes keep their own form, and a bevel too big for its shape is left off (the panel says so). 0 or blank means sharp edges, as before.

Round the corners (in the shape's panel) rounds off the upright corners of the outline, and of its holes, by the radius you type: a tile, a plinth, a phone stand. The plan keeps the corners as drawn for stretching; the 3D view, the plan's outline and the print show them rounded. A corner between short sides takes what radius fits. It goes with a bevel: round the corners, then ease the top edges.

Hollow it out (in the shape's panel) makes a shape a shell with the wall thickness you type, open at the top (a planter, a box, a pot) or closed (hollow inside, to save filament). The floor is as thick as the wall. A tapered or sloped shape keeps its wall even; holes keep their rims. Turned, swept and domed shapes keep their own form. For printing, keep the wall at least two nozzle widths (0.8 mm); a closed hollow wider than about 30 mm needs supports inside or an open top.

Copies in a row or ring (at the bottom of the panel when anything is selected): type how many and the spacing to make a row of copies (fence posts, battlements, a run of cabinets), or how many in all and a radius for a ring (chairs round a table, posts round a gazebo), with the copies turned to face the middle or kept as they are. They are real copies, like Duplicate makes, so each can be changed after; Undo takes them all back.

Type a distance while pushing or pulling: start dragging a face, then type the distance (450, 450mm, 1'6" or 18") and press Enter. The face moves exactly that far, the way you were dragging; a minus sign (-100) sends it the other way. Pulling the middle of a top, = first sets the height itself: =450 makes it 450 tall. The number shows beside the pointer as you type, Backspace takes a character back and Esc stops typing. You can also let go first and type straight after, the way SketchUp does: the push you just made is set to the number.

Section cut in 3D: the Section button in the 3D bar slides a plane through the model to look inside a house, a box or a machine. Pick Across, Along or Level (a plan cut, looking down), drag the slider or type how far in, and Flip to keep the other side. Fill cut faces fills where the plane cuts walls, floors and shapes, so they read as solid. Save the view (Saved views → + Save this view) and the cut is kept with it: a 3D view on a sheet made from that saved view shows the cut, and so does a tour stop added while the cut is on. Off shows the whole model again.

Turn in 3D (in a 3D shape's panel) turns the finished shape any way: ↻ 90° about east–west, north–south or upright, or Turn by any angle about any axis (pick about my own axis and type it as east, north, up, such as 1, 1, 0 for a diagonal). Lay it on a face… asks you to click a face of the shape in 3D, and turns it so that face is down, the way a part is laid on the print bed. A turned shape rests on its base height and stays where it was on the plan, which shows what it covers now; merged and cut parts turn with it, rotating or mirroring it on the plan keeps its turn, and Push/Pull still works on its faces. A turned (lathe) shape can lie on its side. Stand upright again undoes the turn. (Stand it up, below, stands a flat outline on edge; Turn in 3D turns any finished shape.)

Round or chamfer chosen edges: Pick edges in 3D… (under Bevel edges in a 3D shape's panel) lets you click just the edges to ease: a top or bottom edge (click on its rim), or an upright edge (click up a corner). Each click rounds that edge, or chamfers it when the bevel style is Chamfer; click it again to put it back to sharp, and Esc when you're done. The bevel size and style in the panel are used (3 mm round when there isn't one yet). All edges again goes back to the whole top (and bottom). It shows in 3D and prints like any bevel.

Draw on a face, then push it: the Draw on face button in the 3D bar lets you drag a rectangle on a flat face of a 3D shape: its top, its bottom or an upright side (hold Shift for a circle). Let go, then move the mouse to pull it out (green: a boss, a tab, a peg) or push it in (red: a pocket, a slot, a hole) and click, or type a distance and press Enter. It becomes part of the shape, so it moves, turns, prints and separates with it, and Undo takes it back. Draw as many as you like; Esc stops. It works on shapes standing upright as drawn.

Snapping in 3D: Measure, Draw on face and Guides snap like SketchUp's inference: to a corner (Endpoint, green), the middle of an edge (Midpoint), anywhere along an edge (On edge), a guide, and from the last point along the red (east–west), green (north–south) and blue (up) axes, with a dashed line and a label by the pointer. Hold Shift while on an axis to lock it, or press → (red), ← (green) or ↑ (blue); the same arrow again unlocks.

Keys in 3D (with the pointer over the 3D view): P Push/Pull, F Draw on face, S Section cut. While pushing, Tab turns the push round and Esc puts the face back. On a tablet a number pad comes up after a push, to type the distance; Draw on face has a Rectangle / Circle switch. The compass takes N, S, E, W and T from the keyboard.

More on Draw on face: the rectangle starts and ends on snapped corners and edges, and on a top it lines up with the shape's nearest side, so a shape turned on the plan gets a square-on rectangle. While pushing it, type a size instead of a distance to change it: 40x20 for a rectangle, r15 or d30 for a circle. Hole for hardware… in its bar puts a magnet pocket, a heat-set insert, a nut trap, a pin or a screw hole where you click, sized for the hardware at the print scale. It works on turned and stood-up shapes too.

Guides and the tape: Guides in the 3D bar: click two points for a guide line, or click a point, point along an edge or an axis, and type a distance (600, 2') for a guide point that far away, like SketchUp's Tape Measure. Guides are kept in the plan, show as dashed orange lines and small crosses, and everything that snaps in 3D snaps to them. Delete last, Clear all and Hide are in its bar; they don't print or show on sheets.

Prints · Holds · Cost: with 3D shapes selected, small chips under the 3D view say whether they fit the printer's bed and need supports, whether they hold (Engineering's structure check, Hobby's wood checks), and what the filament and print time come to, updating as you change them. Click a chip for the details. Chips in the 3D bar hides or shows them.

Linked copies: Make a linked copy in a 3D shape's panel makes a copy that changes whenever the original does, and the other way round, like a SketchUp component: chair legs, drawer sides, brackets. Each copy keeps its own place, turn, mirror and base height; the shape itself (its size, pushes, pockets, bevels, colour) is shared. Unlink this copy lets one go its own way.

Follow Me: select a drawn path or a room and its panel has Follow Me: pick a profile (crown moulding at the ceiling, baseboard, chair rail, quarter round, a round handrail, a wall cap), set its height, and Run it round the room or along the path. Corners are mitred; round a room it sits on the inside. On a path, Rises to makes it climb, for a stair rail. Select a closed shape with the path and that shape is the profile. The result is a 3D shape: move, copy, print or price it.

Dimensions that move walls: a dimension stuck to walls has a Measures box in its panel. Type a new number and the wall (or door or window) at its far end moves to it, with the walls joined to it stretching and what stands against it coming along; a dimension along a wall from its end makes that wall longer or shorter. Locked walls stay put. Undo puts it back.

History: History in the 3D bar shows your steps by name (Pull the top 450 mm, Pocket 10 mm on the top) on a slider. Drag it back to watch the model go back step by step, press ▶ Play to run it through from the start, or click a step to go there. It works by undo and redo, so going forward again loses nothing; a change made at an earlier step starts a new history from there.

3D tips: the first time you pull a top, push a side, draw a boss or a pocket, ease an edge, turn a shape and so on, a small card says what just happened and what it's called; Show me plays the move again on the shape. Each shows once. Stop these tips ends them; Tips in the 3D bar starts them again.

Make a kit: in the 3D bar or a shape's panel. From the selected shapes (or all of them) Arc numbers the parts in the order they go together, sets parts too big for the bed to print in pieces that peg together, adds up the filament, print time, hardware and cost, and offers Save the build guide (a web page with the parts list, what to buy and the steps, to print or hand out) and Print it.

The solid engine: merging, cutting and overlapping shapes (and the print parts made from them) use Manifold, a free solid engine (Apache 2.0) that always gives closed, watertight results, so printed parts need no patching. It runs inside Arc, offline and on the web, and in the background worker that lays out print plates. A shape it can't take, such as an imported model that isn't closed, is worked out the old way instead.

Stand it up (in a selected shape's panel) stands its outline up on edge, like a sign, a wreath, a star or a cut-out letter: draw it flat on the plan, then stand it up. It reads from the front the way you drew it, its height becomes its Thickness, and any holes go through it. Turn and move it like any shape; stretching it or changing its height stretches the outline. Lay it flat puts it back. When 3D printing, a standing shape prints lying on its back, so it needs no supports.

Lines, text, hatch and dimensions

Text is under Notes on the left tool bar (or press T): click where it goes and type. The drafting tools in Draw ▾: line (L), polyline (P), rectangle, polygon, circle, arc, ellipse, spline, hatch (H), dimension and text (T). Modify ▾ has offset, trim, extend, fillet, scale, array and the rest. The panel sets the line type, weight, colour, lettering and hatch pattern.

Typing points, snaps and guide lines

While a wall, line, polyline or other drawing tool is going, type where the next point goes and press Enter. The text shows in the Command box as you type.

TypeThe next point
12'6"That far from the last point, toward the pointer
10'<45 or @10'<45That far at that angle (degrees, counter-clockwise from straight right)
@12',8'12' across and 8' up from the last point (a minus goes left or down: @-3',0)
30',20' or #30',20'That spot on the plan, measured from its origin. A wall can start there: pick Wall and type it before clicking.

Any unit works (@3600mm,0, 2.4m<90); a plain number is in the plan's units.

Which snaps are on. Snap & units ▾ has a switch for each kind of point: ends and corners (F4), midpoints (F5), centres (F6), intersections (F7), the perpendicular, tangent and parallel guides (F10) and line up with ends (F11). F3 turns all object snaps off and on, F8 swaps the angle lock between 90° and free, and F9 turns rounding to the step off and on. Each press says what it did. These keys sit where AutoCAD puts its own (F3 object snap, F8 ortho, F9 snap, F11 tracking).

Guide lines while drawing. From the point you're drawing from, Arc offers:

  • Perpendicular (blue): the spot on a nearby wall or line where yours meets it square. The line it squares off to shows dashed.
  • Tangent (green): where a line from your point would just touch a circle or arc.
  • Parallel (purple): with the angle free (hold Shift, or F8), a line within 3° of an angled wall or line runs exactly parallel to it. Straight across or up the plan, the angle lock already does that.
  • Line up with ends (orange): stop level with another wall's or line's end, or with where the shape you're drawing started, so the last side of a polyline closes square.

Levels (floors)

The level bar over the plan picks the floor you're drawing on. + Add level adds a floor above or a basement below, starting with the outside walls, every wall or nothing; Settings renames a level or changes its floor-to-floor height and its wall height. Change the floor-to-floor and the level's walls follow (the floor framing keeps its depth); change the wall height and the floor-to-floor follows. Walls you set to their own height (a guard, a knee wall) only change if you say so. If the floor above is deeper than the room left for it (deeper joists picked on the floor framing sheet), the Design check says so, and its Fix, the floor framing panel or Settings make the level fit. Select items and use the Level section of the panel to copy or move them to another floor. The list also has your detail drawings, under Details.

One floor at a time, on the plan and in 3D. The level picker is the one floor picker: the plan draws that floor and the 3D view follows it. ▼ ▲ beside it (or Page Down / Page Up) go down or up a floor. The switch next to it says how much of the house shows round that floor, and it's saved with the plan:

  • This floor: just this floor. The plan shows nothing else, and 3D shows it alone with its roof and ceilings off, so you look down into its rooms.
  • + below: this floor with the floors under it in 3D, and the floor below in light gray on the plan to trace over (Arc snaps to its walls).
  • Whole house: every floor in 3D; the plan as before, with the floor below in light gray. New plans start here.

The faint floor below can be turned off for good in Preferences (Show the level below in light gray). With several buildings, the arrows go through the floors of the building you're drawing in, and the switch hides when that building has one floor. The same floor and switch are in the 3D view's Layers panel and Ctrl+K ("this floor only", "up a floor").

Buildings: several on one site (an acreage)

One plan can hold several buildings on one site: an acreage's house, shop and guest house, or two houses on one lot. Each building has its own floors, floor heights and ground floor, so a two-storey house can sit beside a one-storey shop with a 14' wall. A plan starts with one building and works exactly as before until you add another.

  • Add one: File ▾ → Add a building to this site… (or + Add a building to this site… at the bottom of the level list). Start it from a template or house plan (the garages and shops are in the list), a saved plan file, a copy of the building you're in, or one empty floor. Give it a name, turn it a quarter turn if you like, and set its ground floor height against the first building's (a shop down the slope might be -2'). The first time, Arc also asks for a name for the building already in the plan. The new building's outline then follows the mouse: R turns it a quarter turn and a click puts it there (Esc leaves it 30' east of the others). A plan or template brought in leaves its own lot behind (its property lines and ground shaping) unless you tick Bring its lot too.
  • Switch buildings with the Building picker that appears in the level bar. The level list then shows that building's floors, and + Add level adds a floor to it. The other buildings show in gray with their names on the ground floor, to line things up by them.
  • The building tools are at the bottom of the Building picker: its name, ground height and services; Move or turn this building (its outline follows the mouse again, every floor goes with it); Connect with a driveway or path; Select the whole building (every floor, ready to move with M or turn with R, and its upper floors go with it); Move the selection to another building (to split a plan where a house and a detached garage were drawn together: select the garage and move it to a new building); and Delete this building. Delete down to one building and the plan is an ordinary one-building plan again.
  • Driveways and paths between buildings: Connect with a driveway or path… picks two buildings, a 12' driveway or a 4' path, and gravel, asphalt, concrete or pavers. Arc runs it door to door (garage doors first for a driveway), sloping from one building's floor height to the other's, and shapes the ground along it; drag its points to bend it. It's priced in the site work.
  • Shared services: in a building's settings, tick Power, Water and Sewer from the first building (a new building starts with power). The budget's site work gets the trench (about 3' deep for power alone, below frost at about 8' with water or sewer), the feeder cable and a 100 A sub-panel, and the water and sewer lines, measured from the gap between the buildings plus 10' at each end. They show dashed on the ground floor and the site plan, marked P, W and S.
  • The site (property lines, ground shaping, paths, driveways, fences and the Site & landscaping layer) belongs to the whole site: it's drawn on the first building's ground floor and shows on every building's ground floor. The first building's ground floor is the site's elevation 0.
  • 3D: a second picker beside the floor picker shows the Whole site, The plan's building (it follows the plan), or one building by name; the floor picker then lists that building's floors.
  • Budget: Budget ▾ → Takeoff & cost estimate… counts each building on its own and the site work (with lines added by hand and PDF counts) as its own part, lists each one's subtotal, and adds them up to the site total. Showing picks one building's lines. The spreadsheet has a Building column.
  • Sheets: Create a standard set starts with one site plan of every building, then each building's own sheets, numbered with the building's number in front (1A-101, 2A-101…) and its name in the title, with elevations and a 3D view of it alone. Room numbers start with the building's number (2-101), and the IFC export has one building each.
  • Design check: each building is checked on its own (upper floors, height limits and the building area count its own floors and ground), setbacks are checked for every building, and two buildings closer than 10' are flagged on their ground floors (fire separation).

Layers

Every item sits on a layer (walls, doors and windows, furniture, electrical…). In the Layers list under the panel, click a layer to draw on it, the eye to hide it and the lock to stop it being selected; double-click a name to rename it. Select items and use the Layer section of the panel to move them to another layer.

Freeze the layers you don't want to see. The snowflake on a layer freezes it (it shows in place of the count when you point at the layer; on an iPad, tap Quick sets and use the snowflakes there): it's hidden on the plan, in 3D and on pictures, nothing on it can be picked or snapped to, and it stays hidden when you use a quick set, Show everything or the clean floor plan. You can't draw on a frozen layer (new drawing moves to the first layer still showing). Unfreeze all at the top of the list brings every frozen layer back in one click; the snowflake or the eye unfreezes one. If you add something that goes on a frozen layer (a chair on Furniture, say), that layer unfreezes by itself, so new work is never out of sight. Frozen layers are saved with the plan, and undo leaves them frozen. A sheet that picks its own layers still prints them.

Quick sets (the button at the top of Layers, or Layers on the 3D toolbar) show just the layers for a job, in one click:

  • Everything: every layer (frozen ones stay frozen).
  • Structure only: the bones: walls, doors and windows, stairs, beams and posts (Structural) and roofs.
  • Shell: the house closed in, as you see it from outside: the structure and the yard, nothing inside the rooms.
  • Finishes: the structure with feature walls and trim, cabinets and fixtures, room names and the yard: no furniture, wiring or notes.
  • Wiring, pipes & ducts: the structure with Electrical, Plumbing and Mechanical, for planning the rough-in.
  • Furniture off: hides Furniture and Staging & seasonal decor and leaves the rest as it is.

Quick sets change Arc's own layers; layers you made yourself keep their eye (Everything shows them too). Arc Hobby and Arc Engineering have Parts only, Frame only (Engineering) and Sketch lines & text off; Arc Landscape has Yard only, House off and Notes off.

The Floors & layers panel (Layers on the 3D toolbar, under More ▾ when the toolbar is narrow; or Quick sets in the Layers list) has the floor arrows and the switch, the quick sets, the walls-as-built switches and every layer with its eye and snowflake, in one window you can drag out of the way. It's sized for a finger on an iPad and grows with Bigger text.

A clean floor plan for reading: Show ▾ → Clean floor plan shows just the layout (walls, doors, windows, stairs), cabinets and fixtures, furniture, the room names and the outside walls' dimensions: no small labels, notes, areas, floor patterns or warning markers. Electrical, plumbing, mechanical, structure, roofs, the site, text and drafting lines hide, in the plan and the 3D view, until you tap a layer's eye to bring one back. Everything still works as normal: anything you add goes on its own layer, and a hidden layer shows itself when something is added to it. Untick it (or Show everything at the top of Layers) to put every layer back as it was. It's saved with the plan, and printed sheets still show everything.

Design check

With nothing selected, the panel's Design check lists things worth a second look on this level: clearances, door swings, stair sizes, bedroom windows, railings, smoke alarms and more, each with its code section. Click Show to zoom to it, Ignore to hide it, or Note to keep a note with it. Warning triangles mark them on the plan (purple ones are your own project specs). The checks are a guide: your building department has the final say.

What the Design check counts. Blocks on a hidden layer still count: a smoke alarm on a hidden Electrical layer, or a fan on a hidden Mechanical layer, isn't reported missing. When a hidden layer holds walls, rooms, doors and windows, stairs or roofs, the check says so at the top, since it can't see them. Every range hood block is a range hood, the plaster, metal-strapped and wood mantel hoods too; type the fan insert's rated flow in its panel (Arc takes 600 cfm until you do). Under the NBC a hood over about 300 cfm (142 L/s) gets the make-up air check (9.32.3.8). Wall cabinets, a wall hood or a range with no wall behind them (along an open side of a kitchen) are flagged. Bedroom checks (escape windows, smoke alarms) go to bedrooms only: Guest bedroom is one, Guest bath and Guest walk-in closet aren't. In Edmonton, the grading check uses the City's lot grading numbers: the ground falls at least 5% for the first 2 m from the house, then at least 1.5%; elsewhere it asks for 6″ in the first 10′.

How many warnings a plan shows. The Warnings picker at the top of the Design check (also in Project settings, and Ctrl+K → "warnings") is saved with each plan: All warnings; Safety only (stairs, guards and railings, escape windows, smoke and CO alarms, radon, fire separation and exits, pool barriers, buried lines, and structure that won't hold; the clearances and planning tips wait); or Off while drawing. With Safety only or Off, a small N warnings · Show note at the bottom says how many are waiting; click it to pick a level, and they all show at once. A new house, addition or renovation starts with All; a plan started as New interior design or Decorate a room starts with Off. Plans from before this setting show All. The permit papers and the code summary sheet always list every warning, whatever the level. (Don't check codes for this project still drops the code warnings for a historic or existing building.)

Checks & analysis: only what you ask for. The Checks & analysis ▾ drop-down at the top of the Design check (also in Project settings and Review ▾ → Checks & analysis…) has a tick for each extra check or calculation, saved with the plan:

  • Code checks: the code warnings, the Code summary sheet and its line in the Permit-set check.
  • Structure checks: spans, braced walls and tall walls.
  • Renovation hazards: what to test for before opening up an older house (asbestos, lead paint, old wiring), from the year it was built.
  • Energy (NBC 9.36 and tiers): the 9.36 worksheet, the energy performance tier, and their lines in the code summary and the Permit-set check.
  • Heat loss, heating and cooling: Heat loss, room by room and Budget ▾ Heating & cooling.
  • Sun study: the Sun & shadow study sheet in a new sheet set.
  • Planning tips: clearances, door swings and layout tips while you draw.

Anything not ticked shows nowhere: not in the menus, the panel, the Permit-set check, the warnings or a new sheet set. Ctrl+K still finds it, marked (turn it on): pick it and it's ticked and opened. A new Arc plan starts with code, structure and hazards ticked; ArcRes adds planning tips. Plans from before this drop-down show everything, as they always did. Safety warnings are not ticks: stairs, guards, escape windows, alarms, radon, fire and structure that won't hold follow the Warnings level above, whatever is ticked. Sheets you already made are never taken away; to add the sun study to a set you have, Ctrl+K → "sun study".

Tall freestanding furniture (a bookcase, a wardrobe, a dresser, a TV on a stand) gets a note to anchor it to the wall with an anti-tip strap into a stud.

An indoor golf simulator

Pick Blocks (B) → Game room for the golf pieces: the enclosure (8, 10 or 12 ft screen with side nets, or a wide 16:9 one), an impact screen on its own frame, the hitting mat, and three kinds of launch monitor: a radar behind the golfer, a camera beside the ball, or a camera on the ceiling. The projector is under Living. Or press Ctrl+K and type golf.

Put the enclosure's back near a wall, then the mat in front of it with its arrow pointing at the screen. Select the mat to set Who hits (right-handed, left-handed, or both with the ball in the middle), the Tallest golfer and the Clubs (every club, or irons only). The dashed circle round the golfer is the swing.

The Design check then looks at the room the way a golfer would:

  • The swing: a circle about 7′ (2.1 m) across round the golfer, clear of walls, posts and furniture. 9′ is comfortable.
  • The ceiling over the swing, and anything hanging in it: a beam, a duct, a light, the projector. A driver needs about 3′4″ more than the golfer's height (9′4″ for a 6 ft golfer); makers ask for 9′ to 10′ (2.7 to 3 m). Irons need about 2′ over the golfer (8′).
  • The screen: the ball about 10′ (3 m) from it, 8′ at the least, lined up with its middle; 4′ to 7′ behind the ball; 12″ to 16″ (300 to 400 mm) clear behind the screen.
  • The launch monitor: a radar 6′ to 8′ straight behind the ball (about 10′ for larger units), with at least 8′ of ball flight, so the room is about 16′ deep or more; a camera 1′ to 2′ beside the ball, across from the golfer (it moves across for a left-handed golfer); a ceiling camera 9′ to 10′ up, about 3′6″ toward the screen from the ball.
  • The projector: whether its picture fills the screen. Throw ratio × picture width is how far it sits from the screen (0.7 × 10′ = 7′).

Most warnings have a Fix: move the mat to a spot where the swing and the ceiling both pass (its launch monitor goes with it), move it back from the screen, line it up, move the screen out from the wall, put a launch monitor in its place, or move the projector so the picture fits.

A room that works: 14′ × 20′ (4.3 × 6.1 m) with a 10′ ceiling lets right- and left-handed golfers both hit from the middle. 10′ wide is the least, with the golfer off to one side; 15′ deep is the least with a camera unit.

In a basement, the ceiling is usually the hard part. Fix the cheap things first: move the lowest duct up between the joists, pick a hitting spot between beams and ducts, choose a camera unit (no extra depth needed), or plan for irons only. Moving a beam or a post, cutting joists or lowering the floor needs an engineer and a building permit.

The shopping list prices each piece (a 10 ft enclosure, a mat, a radar, a camera unit, a ceiling camera, a projector) at typical Canadian prices, in US or UK money if that's your region. They're estimates: check the store.

Room to play: pool, ping-pong, darts and more

Each game in Blocks → Game room shows the room it needs round it as a dashed purple area on the plan (turn it off in Show ▾ → Play space round games; it isn't printed). The Design check says when a wall or furniture is inside it, and Fix → Move it to fit slides the game to a spot that clears.

GameRoom it needs
Pool tableAbout 58″ (1.47 m) all round for standard cues. An 8′ table wants a room about 13′ × 17′.
Ping-pong4′ (1.2 m) behind each end, 3′ (0.9 m) at the sides: a room about 17′ × 11′ (5.2 × 3.4 m) at least.
DartsBull 5′8″ (1.73 m) off the floor. Throw line 7′9¼″ (2.37 m) from the board's face for steel tip, 8′ (2.44 m) for soft tip; pick which on the board. A lane 3′ wide from the board to 2′ past the line, with no furniture, walkway or door swinging across it. Add a Dart throw line and the check says if it's in the right place.
Shuffleboard2′ to 3′ at each end, and about 18″ along one side (the other can be against a wall). A 12′ table needs a space about 16′ × 6′.
Air hockey3′ (0.9 m) at each end where the players stand.
Foosball3′6″ on each players' side: the rods stick out about 6″.
Arcade game, pinball3′ in front to stand and play, and a plug nearby. A pinball machine weighs about 120 kg: plan the trip down the stairs.
Card or poker table36″ from the table's edge to pull a chair out and sit; 42″ to 48″ where people walk behind the players.
Bar and stoolsAbout 24″ for each stool along the bar (26″ to 30″ is comfier), and 36″ in front for the stools; 44″ lets people walk behind them.

These are comfortable playing sizes from the makers and the games' own rules, not building code.

Converting a drawing to walls, stairs, cabinets and fixtures

File ▾ → Turn a drawing into walls… finds the walls, doors and windows in an imported DXF, DWG or CAD-made PDF, or in a scanned plan that's been scaled. From a CAD drawing it also finds stairs (a run of evenly spaced riser lines; the "UP 8R" note gives the way up and the riser count), cabinets and appliances (the counter line 24" off a wall for base cabinets, 12" for wall cabinets, split by the lines across it; a DW, F, W or D note, three or more burner circles or a sink outline name the dishwasher, fridge, washer, dryer, range or sink base; an oval basin makes a vanity) and bathroom, laundry and mechanical fixtures (a toilet's oval and tank, a tub's rectangle with the oval inside, a shower's square with its note or diagonals, a pedestal sink, washer and dryer, water heater and furnace by their notes). Green bands are walls, blue are doors, teal are windows, orange a stair, purple a cabinet or appliance, plum a fixture: click or tap any you don't want, then create them. Everything made is a real wall, stair or block, so you can move, resize or change any of it afterwards, and one Undo takes the whole conversion back. A stair marked DN on an upper floor is the floor below's stair seen from above, so it starts left out. Scans give walls only.

Converting a plan: a checklist

  1. Get the drawing in. File ▾ → DXF / DWG / room scan… (or PDF plan, then turn into walls… for a PDF made by a CAD program; a scan or photo goes in through Plot plan or survey… and only gives walls). Pick the drawing's units when asked; leave "show in black" ticked. A big drawing is read in the background with a progress box (and Cancel). When the sheet holds several drawings (a main floor, a foundation plan, a second floor, elevations), Bring in offers each one by its title and size, floor plans first, or everything on the sheet: pick the floor you want and only its lines come in, so there's nothing to drag a box round or delete.
  2. Check the scale. Hover a known length (a door, a dimension string) and read the Size box, or measure it with Measure. If it's off, undo and import again with the right units.
  3. Pick one floor. If you brought in just one drawing, skip this. Otherwise drag a box round the one you want (left to right, fully inside) so only its lines are selected; the conversion uses the selection.
  4. Choose the level. In the Level bar at the top, pick (or add) the floor this plan is for, so the walls land on the right storey.
  5. Convert. File ▾ → Turn a drawing into walls…. The panel shows how many walls, doors, windows, stairs, cabinets and fixtures were found and which layers the walls came from; tick or untick layers if walls were missed or notes were taken for walls.
  6. Review on the plan. Click or tap anything you don't want (it turns grey); click again to bring it back. Check the stair's UP label is at the bottom end, that cabinets sit against the right wall, and that nothing was found in a dimension string or a title block.
  7. Set the wall height and leave "Round thicknesses" ticked unless the drawing's walls are unusual sizes. Press Create (or Enter).
  8. Tidy up. Select a converted stair and check its risers and width in the panel; drag any cabinet or fixture that landed a little off; add doors and windows the drawing showed only as notes. The traced drawing is hidden on its own layers (Layers panel) if you need to look back at it.
  9. Repeat for each floor, then look at it in 3D.
If something isn't found: stairs need at least three evenly spaced riser lines 30"–66" long (on any layer but notes and dimensions); cabinets need a counter line drawn 21"–27" (or 10"–14") off a wall face; a kitchen island is a closed outline 3'–12' long and 2'–7' deep clear of the walls; a tub, shower or toilet needs its outline (closed, or four lines meeting at the corners) and, for a shower, its note or two diagonals; a washer or dryer can be drawn open on one side; a furnace or water heater is found by its note on any layer, even a detail layer. Anything else is quick to place by hand from the Blocks library, snapped to the imported lines.

5. Decorating: palettes, styles and decor

Arc can paint and dress rooms the way a decorator would: pick a palette and click a room, restyle the whole house with a style package, and furnish it from the decor library. Everything stays editable, and Ctrl+Z takes back each step.

Colour palettes

Open Finishes on the tool bar and pick Decorate (or press Y). The panel shows 32 palettes, two or more for each of the 16 styles; the Style list narrows them down. Each palette has seven colours, and each has a job:

ColourWhere it goes
WallsEvery wall face in the room
Accent wallOne wall: the longest stretch with the fewest doors and windows
Trim & baseboardsDoor and window casings in those walls, and the room's baseboards
CeilingThe room's ceiling
Curtains & fabricsCurtains, sheers, Roman shades and poufs in the room
Pillows & art, Throws & accentsThrow pillows, blankets, framed and canvas art, bedding, and a softened version on rugs

Untick any of Walls, Accent wall, Trim, Ceiling or Decor to leave that part alone. Then click a room on the plan, or a floor or wall in the 3D view. Every room on this level and Whole house do many rooms at once, and Clear this level takes the palette paint off again.

Each side of a wall is painted on its own, and a long wall that faces several rooms gets each room's colour on its own stretch. Under the palette the panel lists each colour's name and code, and notes how they read together: trim that will disappear into the walls, an accent that's too close to the walls to show, or a strong colour that will look stronger on four walls than on a chip.

Making your own palette

  • From a photo…: choose a picture of a room you love, a rug, a painting or a fabric. Arc finds its main colours and places them: the softest light colour on the walls, the lightest on the trim and ceiling, the strongest on the accent wall. Click any of the colours it found to use it as the accent instead.
  • From one colour…: pick one colour and a harmony (complementary, analogous, triadic, split complementary or monochrome). Walls come out soft, the accent deeper, trim and ceiling near white.
  • Edit a copy of this one…: change any colour of the palette you're looking at.

In each, every colour has a picker and a name box, so you can type the paint names or codes from a brand's fan deck. Save palette keeps it under My palettes on this computer, and it is saved with each plan you use it in. The × on a palette card deletes it.

Style packages

Design ▾ → Style packs… restyles the whole house in one go: siding, roof and trim, cabinets, counters and appliances, floors and baseboards, porch columns, and now the inside paint from the style's palette. There are 16: Modern farmhouse, Craftsman, Contemporary, Traditional, Coastal, Scandinavian, Industrial, Mediterranean, Mid-century modern, Japandi, Bohemian, Transitional, Rustic / lodge, French country, Art deco and Minimalist. Untick any part you want to keep as it is. New things you add afterwards follow the style too.

Inside, each style also sets:

  • Floors that suit each room. Bathrooms, the kitchen, the laundry and the mud room get the style's tile; a room named for a living space as well (an open "Great room / kitchen", a "Bedroom / ensuite") gets the main floor. A gym or fitness room gets rubber, a basement gets vinyl plank in the wood's colour (hardwood doesn't go on a slab), and a polished-concrete style uses a concrete-look vinyl plank upstairs. Garages, mechanical rooms, storage and unfinished rooms are left as they are, floor and paint.
  • Light fittings, mirrors, sinks, tubs, stools, sofas and furniture wood: pendants, chandeliers, sconces, mirrors and tub fillers in the style's metal; its kitchen sink (farmhouse apron, undermount or workstation), shower (glass door or curbless walk-in) and freestanding tub colour; stool, sofa and bed finishes; and floating or legged vanities for the looks that have them.
  • Tile: its kitchen backsplash behind every run of counters, and tub and shower surrounds to the ceiling with a 4" splash behind each vanity, on every floor (each replaces the same kind of tile put there before).
  • Built-ins and mantels (bookcases, closets, the fireplace mantel) take the cabinets' finish, and panel-ready appliances take the cabinets' colour.

Exterior looks. Design ▾ → Exterior looks… does just the outside, in one click: Modern farmhouse (white board & batten, black window trim, corner boards), Traditional colonial (white lap siding, black shutters), Craftsman (sage siding, wide trim, stone along the bottom), Coastal cottage, Mountain lodge, Contemporary and Mediterranean. The siding goes on every outside wall, and the trim, shutters, corner boards and stone are fitted round each wall's own windows and doors, on the Feature walls & trim layer and in the takeoff. Change any wall afterwards in its panel or with Work on this face in 3D; undo takes the whole look back in one step.

Design options (A, B, C)

To try different versions of the design in one file (a bigger kitchen, another layout, a different look): Design ▾ → Design options (A, B, C)… → Make Option B from this plan. The plan as it was is kept as Option A, and a copy comes onto the plan as Option B to change. The window lists every option with a note and its estimate; Show it brings that option onto the plan (the one you leave is kept as it is), and Compare shows it next to the option showing, side by side or with a wipe, in 3D or on the plan, ready to save for the client. Each option is its own drawing (walls, rooms, furniture, floors); the project, sheets and prices are shared. Options are saved in the plan file. Undo starts again after switching options.

Each option has its own budget lines. Costs you add by hand to the estimate (an allowance, a bought part, a hot tub) and quantities you type over counted ones belong to the option showing, so Option A and Option B add up separately and the Design options window shows each one's own total. An option kept before Arc 0.71 starts with the lines showing when you first switch to it.

Tile: backsplashes, bathrooms and floors

Design ▾ → Tile: backsplash & bathrooms… tiles a kitchen backsplash (between the counters and the upper cabinets, around windows, to the ceiling behind the range if you like), a tub surround, a shower, a vanity wall or a wainscot in one go. Choose the look (26 ceramics, porcelains, stones, glass, zellige and more), the size, the pattern (stacked, brick, ⅓ offset, vertical stack, herringbone, double herringbone, basketweave, chevron or diagonal; hexagons, penny rounds, arabesques and other shaped tiles lay in their own pattern), the grout colour and joint, and an edge (bullnose, a metal edge in brushed, black or brass, or a pencil liner). In Design ▾ → Wall design the Tile kit draws a tile area, a band or a niche on any wall, and a room's floor set to tile gets the same chooser in the panel. The 3D shows the pattern with its grout lines.

The takeoff counts it the way you buy it: boxes of tile (by the size's coverage, with waste), bags of thinset and grout, backer board, waterproofing in wet areas, edge trim in feet and niches. Every quantity in the estimate can be changed (see below), so an extra box for breakage or a different grout count is one click.

More patterns and any colour. Patterns include Versailles (a French 3-piece pattern of oblongs and squares on a grid of half tiles: 12" × 24" gives 12 × 24, 12 × 12 and 6 × 12 pieces) and Hopscotch or pinwheel (each square with a half-size square at its corner). Colour gives any tile any colour (slate grey, a beige vinyl…); The tile's own goes back to its look's colour.

Basketweave, double herringbone, arabesque and borders.

  • Basketweave: oblong tiles side by side in square blocks, each block turned from the next. 3" × 6" or 12" × 24" gives pairs; 2" × 8" gives fours. Square tile can't make blocks, so it shows as a grid. About 12% extra for cuts.
  • Double herringbone: herringbone with the tiles in pairs. Looks best with long, thin tile (2" × 8", 3" × 12"). About 15% extra.
