  • Arabesque (lantern): a tile shape, in white or blue, 4" × 5" or 6" × 7½". The lanterns fit into each other in their own pattern. About 15% extra, as the edges need cutting.
  • More looks (after the older ones, which look just as they did): large-format slabs (48" × 96" porcelain panels in a Calacatta or Statuario look, or concrete, with hairline joints), terrazzo (white, blush or charcoal, with chips of stone), fluted (ribbed) tile, kit-kat finger tile, more zellige colours, picket tile (long, with pointed ends that nest), black and terracotta hexagons, green penny round, and pre-cut chevron in marble or an oak look. Picket and chevron need about 15% extra for cuts.
  • Finish: how shiny the tile is in 3D and Photo renders: the tile's own (glazed tile shines, the others don't), Matte, Satin or Glossy.
  • My tile: Save as My tile… names the tile design you've set up (its look, size, layout, colour, grout colour and width, and finish) and keeps it in the plan and in My library. Its My tile list lays one again anywhere: a backsplash, a shower wall or a floor, keeping that spot's own edge trim and border.
  • Border: in the room's panel, Border lays one or two rows of the tile round the walls, with the pattern inside, like a picture frame. It's a shade darker (or lighter on dark tile) unless you pick a Border colour. It shows in 3D and on the plan (its rows, a mitre at each corner and a joint every tile length), the swatch shows one corner, and the budget counts the border tile on its own line. Wall tile can have one too: pick Border on the wall face designer's Tile tab, and it runs round the tile's edges and round any window in it, but not along the joins where the tile steps round the window. Square-edged tile only (not hexagons, pennies, fish scales, mosaics or arabesques).

The decor library

Six new categories in the Blocks library (B), and in search (Ctrl+K). There's also Outdoor living: chaise lounges, an outdoor sofa and lounge chairs, an outdoor dining set, a round fire pit and a linear fire table, a market umbrella, a hot tub and a kayak, with cushions and canopies in a second colour a palette can set.

CategoryPieces
Window treatmentsCurtain panels on a rod, sheer curtains, Roman shade, roller blind, vertical blinds (patio doors and wide windows), horizontal blinds (1" and 2" slats), plantation shutters
LampsFloor lamp, tripod lamp, ceramic and glass table lamps, wall sconce, globe, drum and cluster pendants, picture light
Wall decorFramed art, a seven-frame gallery wall, canvas art, round and arched mirrors, styled floating shelves, wall clock
Indoor plantsFiddle leaf fig, snake plant, monstera, olive tree, hanging plant, small table plant, floor vase with pampas grass
Pillows, throws & accentsThrow pillows (2, 3 or 5), folded throw, pouf, woven basket, vases, books, candles
Wall panels & trimWainscoting, board & batten, picture frame moulding, crown moulding, chair rail

Wall pieces (curtains, art, mirrors, shelves, panelling) turn to sit against the nearest wall. Table pieces (lamps, vases, books, candles) start at table height; change Off the floor to set them on a dresser or shelf. Every decor piece takes any colour from its row of swatches (the rainbow swatch is any colour at all), and pillows and art have a second colour. Decorate's palettes colour them too.

Luxury kitchen, spa bath and statement lighting

Four more groups in the Blocks library (B) of Arc and ArcRes, for a custom high-end home, and in search (Ctrl+K: try steam, sauna, chandelier or lantern). Arc Landscape has the outdoor lanterns. Each piece comes in real sizes, with its finishes and choices in the panel, a starting price in the estimate (Canadian dollars first, through Preferences → Prices and money), and a Good to know note when it's selected: what it needs (a circuit, a water line, a vapour-proof enclosure) and the clearances to ask about.

GroupPieces
Luxury kitchenBuilt-in coffee machine, warming drawer, steam oven and speed oven (alone, over a wall oven or over a warming drawer), full-height wine column, pro range 36" to 60" with a griddle, char grill, both or a French top, workstation sink with a ledge, board, colander and rack (undermount or apron front), scullery sink with drainboard, butler's pantry with glass uppers, appliance pantry with pocket doors, plaster, metal-strapped and wood mantel hoods, refrigerator drawers, 42" or 48" built-in fridge
Spa bathSteam shower with a bench and a sealed, sloped ceiling, curbless shower with glass panels, wet room (a tub in the shower), barrel sauna (electric or wood heater, with a porch), indoor sauna room, bidet, smart toilet (floor or wall-hung), vessel sink (round, square or carved stone), trough sink, heated towel rail, drop-in tub with a stone deck, sit-down makeup vanity, linen tower, cold plunge tub, deep soaking tub
Statement lightingLinear, tiered crystal, ring, sputnik, orb and candle chandeliers, lantern pendant, a cascade of glass pendants for a stair hall, candle, globe and swing-arm sconces, a vanity light, a pair of bath sconces beside the mirror, a hardwired picture light and a cove light (LED strip)
Outdoor lanternsCarriage lantern for the wall, hanging porch lantern, up-and-down wall light, gooseneck barn light, pillar or post lantern

The lights count in Lighting study by their Light output (bigger ones give more: a 60" linear chandelier more than a 36" one; type your fixture's own lumens to change it) and in the electrical plan as lights. A hanging light's Ceiling height sets how long its stem is. The pro range, steam and speed ovens, coffee machine, built-in fridge, wine column, refrigerator drawers, steam generator, sauna heater and cold plunge chiller each get a circuit of their own in the electrical plan. The plumbing summary counts the new sinks, toilets, bidet, showers and tubs (a wet room as a tub and a shower, a smart toilet as a toilet) and the built-in fridge's water line.

A tiled shower you draw yourself

In Arc and ArcRes, Blocks (B) → Spa bath → Tiled shower (draw your own): click one corner and then the opposite one (or type a size like 60x36 and click). Its back goes on the far side from the way in; R turns it before you draw. In its panel pick the Walls round it (an alcove, a corner or open both ends), Curbless or a curb, the Drain (a linear drain along the back wall or at the way in, or a centre drain), the Slope, a Bench, one or two Niches, the Glass (a fixed panel and a door, fixed glass with an open way in, or a door only), the wall and floor tile, and a sheet or liquid waterproofing membrane. The plan shows the curb, the glass and the door's swing, the drain, the bench and arrows down to the drain with the slope; 3D shows the sloped floor, the tile, the niches, the bench and the glass.

The panel adds up the tile (floor and walls), the membrane (floor, walls, bench, niches and curb) and the glass, and checks the shower against the plan's code: in Canada, safety glass (NBC 9.6.1.4); the NBC and the plumbing code set no slope, so Arc gives the tile trade's 2% (¼" a foot) as advice. In the US the IRC's slope of ¼" to ½" a foot and curb of 2" to 9" above the drain (P2709.1), a shower at least 900 sq in and 30" each way with a 22" way in (P2708.1). A curbless shower tells you how far to set the floor down; a centre drain warns when its short sides get too steep or the floor tile is too big to fold four ways. The estimate gets the tile with its thinset, grout and the Waterproofing membrane line, the drain, the valve, the sloped mortar bed, the curb or the set-down floor, the bench, the niches and the glass.

Millwork, wine and bar, fine furniture, and outdoor rooms

Six more groups in the Blocks library (B) of Arc and ArcRes, for the rest of a custom home, inside and out. Search finds them too (Ctrl+K: try library, wine, canopy bed, cupola or pergola). Arc Landscape has the outdoor rooms and the entry and garden accents. Like the luxury kitchen pieces, each comes in real sizes with its finishes and choices in the panel, a starting price in the estimate (Canadian dollars first), and a Good to know note when it's selected.

GroupPieces
MillworkFull-height panelled wall (flat, raised, picture-frame or grid), fluted or reeded panel, built-up crown for the top of a built-in, library wall with a rolling ladder, window seat nook with storage and bookcases, mudroom bench with cubbies and hooks, butler's pantry run with a sink and wine fridge, floating vanity shelf, wall niche (arched or square, lit), fireplace built-ins
Wine & barWood wine racking (cubbies, a lit display row or case bins), a metal peg wall with the labels forward, racking with a tasting arch, a glass wine room front with its door, back bar with lit shelves, bar sink with a glass rinser, beverage fridge and ice maker, tasting table (on a barrel, a pedestal or steel legs), hanging stemware rack, wine cellar cooling unit
Fine furnitureUpholstered, panel (including sleigh), canopy and platform beds; benches; console and sofa tables; counter and bar stools (upholstered, wood and metal); chaise longue; daybed; curved sofa; U-shaped sectional; executive or writing desk and desk chair; nightstands and dressers by style; live-edge, pedestal and trestle dining tables; upholstered and wood dining chairs; accent chairs; an étagère
Exterior characterPorch post on a stone base, timber gable truss, timber knee brace, corbels, gable vents and gable accents, shutters by style (louvred, panel, board and batten, Bahama), chimney cap and chimney pots, cupola with a weathervane, door surround with a pediment, door canopy on brackets, corner quoins
Entry & garden accentsAddress plaque and house numbers (type the number in Label), tall entry planters, a garden urn on a pedestal, a rain chain; and in Outdoor lanterns, a craftsman wall lantern and a gas lantern
Outdoor roomsCustom timber pergola (open rafters, slats, louvres or a clear roof, on its own or against the house), timber pavilion, outdoor kitchen pieces that line up in a run (built-in grill, side burner or griddle, fridge or kegerator, sink or bar centre, pizza oven, raised bar counter), round or square fire table, fire bowl, water feature, outdoor TV cabinet

The bar sink, the butler's pantry run and the outdoor kitchen sink count in the plumbing summary; the wine cellar cooling unit, beverage fridge, outdoor fridge, fountain pump and outdoor TV get a circuit of their own in the electrical plan; the two lanterns count in the Lighting study.

Finish a basement: an office with a 3D printer, and a home gym

  1. Rooms. Draw or find the office, the gym and the bath (Rooms, R), and name them: a room named "gym" or "fitness" gets rubber flooring from a style package, and one named "storage" or "mechanical" is left alone.
  2. The gym. From Blocks → Fitness: the treadmill and bike facing a TV, the power rack against a wall with the bench in front, the dumbbell rack, a yoga mat. For the floor, Floors (A) → Rubber (gym), 24" tiles. For the mirror wall, select the wall → ✎ Work on this face in 3D → Start from a design… → Mirror wall (home gym), or place Gym wall mirror panels.
  3. The 3D printer. A bench or desk on an outside wall (so the exhaust has a short way out), the printer enclosure on it (set its height to the bench top, 36" for a workbench), the 3D printer inside it at the same height plus 1", and the filament dry box beside it.
  4. The exhaust. Fumes from printing (ABS, ASA, resin) go outside: the enclosure's 4" port, an inline duct fan up near the ceiling, a round duct to the wall, and an exhaust hood on the outside face of the wall, above the ground outside (in a basement, high on the foundation wall or through the rim joist). They're on the Mechanical layer and in the takeoff; check the hole through a foundation wall with your builder.
  5. See it. Turn on the Mechanical and Electrical layers (or leave Show light fittings, feature walls and equipment ticked in Render…), stand the camera in the room, and render: inside rooms the render uses the indoor look.

Design this room for me

Design ▾ → Design this room for me… (also on a selected room's panel, and in the Decorate tool) suggests three furniture arrangements for a room. Pick the room, the kind of room (living or family room, bedroom, kids' bedroom, nursery, dining room, home office; Arc guesses it from the name) and a style for the fabrics and woods. Each arrangement is scored for comfort, and the best is marked ★ Most comfortable. The notes under each picture say why:

  • walkways and clearances, from the same rules as the Design check (room beside a bed, in front of a dresser, behind a desk chair)
  • doors that swing and open clear, and windows not blocked by tall pieces
  • living rooms: the TV 7'–12' from the sofa, seats close enough to talk, the rug under the front legs
  • bedrooms: the bed on a solid wall, not under a window, with room to walk round it
  • dining rooms: 36" (better 42") round the table to pull out chairs
  • offices: a view of the door, and daylight from the side rather than behind the screen

In each picture the blue edge is the front of a piece and the dashed squares are door swings. Use this arrangement places the pieces (sofa, tables, lamps, rug, plants…) in the room in the chosen style, coloured from the room's palette if you've decorated it. Move anything afterwards; running it again replaces only the suggested pieces. With the 3D view open it shows that floor on its own, roof and ceilings off, and looks down into the room (pick Whole house on the level bar or in the 3D floor list for every floor again).

Shopping list & budget

Budget ▾ → Shopping list & budget… works out everything to buy from the plan: paint by the gallon (two coats, from the painted wall area less doors and windows, with ceiling and trim paint), wallpaper by the roll, flooring and wall finishes by the square foot with 10% for cuts, baseboard and panelling by the foot, and each piece of furniture, lighting, window treatment, decor and plant. Show the whole plan, one level, or one room; tick Include kitchen, bath and built-ins for those too.

Prices start as rough retail figures. Type your own in Each, paste a product link (the ↗ button opens it in your browser), change a quantity, and tick items off as you buy them. Set a Budget and Tax %: the bar shows the total against the budget, with what's bought in dark green. A selected piece of furniture can carry its own Price and Link in its panel. Save as spreadsheet (CSV) opens in Excel, Numbers or Google Sheets. Everything you type is saved with the plan.

Cabinet hardware: the list counts every cabinet's pulls or knobs, concealed hinges (two a door, three on a tall one) and drawer slides, priced like Arc Hobby's hardware, in the cabinets' hardware finish. I own this on a piece of furniture or decor leaves it out of the total, and Arc remembers it for every plan on this computer.

Trim cut list (Budget ▾): each room's baseboard as the pieces to cut, which 8', 12' and 16' sticks to buy, and the saw setting at every corner (cope or miter inside corners, miter outside ones).

Woodworking tools: Preferences → Woodworking tools adds Arc Hobby's Make, Join, Finish and Shop menus the next time Arc starts: real board sizes, joints and fasteners, a cut list, a shopping list and a build guide for a mudroom bench, a window seat or a built-in. A board's Change this wood… makes it look like your own board (My wood), keeping its wood's strength and price, as in Arc Hobby. Wood decks show their wood's grain and stain in 3D whether it's on or not.

Client inspiration

Design ▾ → Client inspiration (pictures)… keeps the pictures a client sends you (magazine pages, screenshots, photos from their phone, a tile they liked) with the plan. Drop JPEG, PNG or WebP files on the window or click + Add pictures…; big photos are made smaller so the plan file stays light. For each picture, choose the room it's for (or Whole house) and type what they like about it. Its colours pulls the main colours out of a picture into a palette you can paint with (see Decorating). Save as PDF prints them six to a page with their room and note, and the mood board can add the room's pictures as pages after the board (tick Client's inspiration pictures).

Mood board

Design ▾ → Mood board… makes a one-page board (11" × 8½", landscape) for the whole plan or one room: a 3D picture, the colour palette with its codes, the materials and fabrics, the furniture and decor, the budget from the shopping list, and your notes. The picture can be a bird's-eye view into the room with the roof lifted off, a view from inside the room, or the 3D view as it is (set up a saved view or the sun first). Untick any part you don't want, then Save PDF to email or print, or Save picture.

The Interiors workspace

For decorating only, switch to the Interiors workspace: File ▾ → Workspace… → Interiors, Preferences → Workspace, or New interior design on the welcome screen. The tool bar keeps Select, Measure, Wall, Room, Door, Window, Floors, Paint, Decorate, Blocks and the editing tools; stairs, roofs, porches, outdoor and pipes & wires tools, the framing and pipes & wires views, sheets, CAD drafting and the DXF, DWG, IFC and STL exports are tucked away, and the library leaves out the mechanical, structural, electrical, garage, exterior and landscaping pieces. It's the same program and file format, so a decorator can open a builder's plan and the other way round. File ▾ → Workspace… → Full brings everything back.

The Model maker workspace

For printed models, dollhouses and dioramas: File ▾ → Workspace… → Model maker (or Preferences → Workspace). The walls, rooms, doors, windows and roofs stay, for houses to print, with the 3D shapes (Draw ▾ → 3D shape), the model base and ground (Outdoor), text, measuring and moving. The wiring and ducts, framing members, markups, the Budget and Review menus, sheets and the contractor's files are tucked away, and search leaves them out. The 3D Print workspace opens beside the plan, and New from a template… opens on the 3D print templates. Switch back the same way: the plan is the same in every workspace.

Start from a single room

To try out colours, furniture or a layout for one room without drawing walls first, choose File ▾ → New from a template… (or Start from a template on the welcome screen) and pick a room: a bedroom 12' × 14' with a closet, a kitchen 12' × 14', a bathroom 8' × 10' or a living room 16' × 18'. Each one comes with its door, windows and a floor. Drag a wall to change the size, then decorate, use Kitchen layout or Design this room for me.

Send to my contractor

Export ▾ → Send to my contractor (one PDF)… puts everything a contractor needs to price the job into one PDF file you can email or print:

  • a cover page with who it's for, your name and phone or email, the size of the plan, a 3D picture, and your notes and questions (type one per line; end a line with ? and it's marked as a question)
  • a floor plan of each level with dimensions and room sizes
  • the outside views (elevations)
  • the mood board
  • the shopping list with prices and the total

Untick any part you don't want; the list underneath shows the pages in order and the preview shows the cover. Save PDF takes the 3D picture and builds the file. What you type is saved with the plan, so the next pack only needs a new date.

Before & after

To show what a makeover changes: Export ▾ → Before & after… and click Keep the plan as it is now as "before". Then change what you like (paint, floors, furniture, even walls). Open Before & after again and Arc takes the same picture of both versions; drag across it to wipe from before to after.

  • Bird's-eye, roof off (the first view): looks down into the house, best for changes inside.
  • Use the 3D view as it is: set up the 3D view first (orbit, a saved view, Go inside a room), then take the pictures from there. Fit the model brings the whole model into the picture.
  • Plan: the plan drawing before and after, as it shows on screen.

Save side by side or Save this wipe makes a picture for the client. "Before" is saved with the plan; Make the plan now the new "before" starts a new comparison.

The existing building next to your changes. When you've marked what's already built as pre-existing, Export ▾ → Before & after / existing vs new… (or Compare… beside Existing in Layers) can compare with Existing building: everything marked pre-existing, including what's being removed, as it stands today, next to the plan with your changes. No need to save a "before" first. Choose Side by side or Wipe, in the 3D view or on the Plan, and save the picture for the client.

Present to a client

For a meeting, on an iPad or a big screen: Export ▾ → Present to a client…. Tick the slides to show and put them in order (↑ ↓); Arc keeps your choice with the plan. Then Start presenting: Arc goes full screen with the tools out of the way.

  • Title: the project's name and address, and today's date.
  • The 3D model and each saved 3D view (save views with the 3D view's Views ▾): the live model, so the client can turn it with a finger.
  • Each floor's plan, as the clean floor plan (room names and outside dimensions).
  • Design options side by side, with each one's price if you tick Show each design option's price (off at first): what the quote would ask, with your markup, labour and tax (your quote's, or your usual ones), never your own costs.
  • Before and after (or existing and new), with a line to drag to wipe between them.

Swipe, use ← →, the ‹ › buttons, or the strip of slide names at the bottom to move; ✕ Exit or Esc stops and puts the plan and 3D view back as they were. The client brief, your notes, quotes and invoices never go on a slide.

Share page

Export ▾ → Share page (3D model to email)… saves one web page file with the project in it: a 3D model to turn and zoom, the floor plans, the mood board, the before & after slider, the shopping list and your notes. Email it, or put it on a website or a shared drive (OneDrive, Dropbox, Google Drive): it opens in any up-to-date browser on a computer, tablet or phone, with nothing to install, and nobody can change your plan. If a browser can't show the 3D model, the page shows a picture of it instead.

On a phone or tablet the page is made for fingers. Under the 3D model is a row of buttons: Whole house, From above, each of your saved views, and each room. Tap one and the camera glides there; inside a room, drag to look around from where you stand. ▶ Take the tour plays through your saved views, and ⛶ Full screen fills the screen. Tick Pictures of your saved views to add a gallery of realistic pictures to swipe through (tap one for full size).

Post as a private link… (on your own copy of Arc on this computer, once it's signed in to Cloudflare) puts the page online at a long, random address such as arc-137-view.pages.dev/k7m2…/ and copies the link, so you can text it to a client: it opens straight away, with no sign-in and nothing to download, and search engines are asked not to list it. Anyone with the link can open it, so share it only with the people it's for. Your posted links are listed in the same window; Remove takes one down for good, and QR code shows a code to point a phone camera at (on the screen, or printed on a card): the page opens on the phone.

Realistic renders and photo mode

In the 3D view, Render… makes a picture of the view (or a saved view) at the size you choose, with a real sky, sunlight and soft shadows. Pick the Lighting (sunny afternoon, golden hour, overcast, your sun-study date and time, or Evening (lights on), where the sun has set and every lamp and light fitting in the model lights its room) and the Quality:

  • Draft and High take seconds.
  • Photo works the way architectural visualisation software does: light bounces off walls, floors and ceilings, so daylight fills a room the way it really does, glass and polished floors reflect, and shadows soften. The picture builds up in passes (Quick 64, Good 160, Best 400) and gets smoother as it goes; the window shows it as it builds and how long is left, and Stop keeps the picture so far. Indoor views are brightened the way a photographer would. It takes minutes rather than seconds, much longer on a computer with built-in graphics: try HD and Quick first. The first photo of a session spends a minute getting the graphics card ready.

Sky. Renders use a photographed sky to suit the lighting: partly cloudy for a sunny afternoon or inside rooms, overcast for overcast, and dusk for golden hour and evening. It's a 360° photo of a real sky, so there are real clouds and haze, the light has a real sky's colour, and the sky shows in the windows. It's turned so its sun is where your lighting puts the sun, so the shadows still follow your sun-study date and time. Pick another under Sky (partly cloudy, clear blue, overcast or dusk), or Computed sky for the clear sky that follows the sun exactly. Custom lighting and the Tour keep the computed sky. The first render with each sky loads its photo (about 5 MB on the web).

Glass and outlines. In a render, windows, glass doors, glass railings and shower glass are real glass: they let the light through and reflect the sky and the room at an angle, as glass does (frosted glass blurs what's behind it). The dark outlines the 3D view draws round each shape are left out of renders, as a camera wouldn't see them.

Bumps and shine. Brick, stone, siding, shingles, metal roofs, tile, floors, counters and wood grain have their relief and shine: mortar and grout sit back and are duller, glaze and polished faces shine. Fabric, velvet, leather, rugs, felt, jute, mattresses and brushed steel on furniture and fixtures have their weave, grain or brushing at its real size. You see it in the 3D view as well as in renders. If a slow computer struggles, turn off Bumps and shine on surfaces in Preferences.

Inside rooms. When the camera is inside (under a ceiling), High and Draft renders light the room the way a camera sees it: the sky's light is turned down so it doesn't come through the walls, a warm bounce from the floor and walls fills the shadows, colours stay true to the paint and tile you chose, and the exposure is set from the picture itself (metered on the bright surfaces, so white walls stay white). The Inside rooms (daylight, lamps on) lighting also turns the light fittings on at half strength. Show light fittings, feature walls and equipment (ticked) puts them in the picture even when their layers are hidden for the plan. If the graphics card gives up part way through a Photo render (it asks a lot of it), Arc renders the same picture at High quality instead and says so under the picture; Photo with Quick passes at HD size may work after closing Arc and opening it again.

Plants in renders. In every render quality (Draft, High and Photo) trees and shrubs have leaves: sprays of leaves round each clump, needles hanging from a spruce's tiers, grass blades, and flowers as heads of petals, not balls of colour. A flowering tree or shrub shows its blossom only in the months it flowers; with no month picked (Garden over time) it's in summer leaf. Perennials keep their flowers. Drawings and exports keep the plain plant shapes.

Plants grown from branches. With the real look and in every render, trees, shrubs and hostas grow the way real ones do: a trunk, limbs, branches and twigs, with sprays of real, photographed leaves out on the twigs (a linden's, a birch's, a dogwood's, a lilac's, a maple's or ninebark's own kind of leaf), darker deep inside the crown and with gaps of sky through it. In winter you see the bare branches and twigs; in fall the same leaves turn the plant's fall colour. Evergreens have photographed fir sprigs, and perennials photographed leaves. The plain look keeps the simple shapes. (The leaf and bark photographs are CC0, from ambientCG.)

Realistic trees, rock and water (Photo). With Photo quality, Realistic trees, rock and water shows them as you'd see them outside, not as drawn. It's ticked when the Ground cover is Forest floor, the plan has a lake, or the plan has plants from the plant library, and you can tick it for any plan. Woodland trees have slim trunks, branches down to fine twigs and sprays of leaves: spring's light green from March to May, full summer leaves the rest of the year (the month comes from the sun study). Hemlocks, rhododendrons, boulders and rock ledges are weathered and mossy, worn back underneath. Streams and ponds are clear water over a stony bed, and falls break into strands with foam and a light mist. On a forest floor, ferns, bushes and saplings fill the ground near the camera, and far trees fade into the haze. Out in the open, shade trees grow the way they do on a lawn, with a short stout trunk, low limbs and a full rounded crown; spruces keep their branches down to the ground and birches have white bark. A lake (still water at least 30' wide with no waterfall) mirrors the sky and the far shore, rippled by the wind, dark looking down and brown in the shallows. On Grass ground the lawn is real mown grass, with clover, faint mowing stripes in the yard and the fields beyond fading into the distance. Green shade trees, spruces, birches and stone boulders are swapped for these; every other plant (shrubs, perennials, grasses, flowering and purple-leaved trees) is drawn with real leaves too, as in Draft and High. The 3D view and the drawings don't change. It asks more of the graphics card: expect about 10 minutes for an HD Quick photo of a wooded site.

Photo of just one room. With Photo quality, tick Just the room I'm standing in when the 3D camera is inside: furniture, fixtures, stairs and plants in the other rooms are left out (the walls, floors and ceilings stay, so the room is lit as it really is). Far less goes to the graphics card, so a room in a big, furnished house finishes on built-in graphics. Low memory goes further for laptops: smaller textures (a little less sharp up close) and smaller pieces of work at a time.

Custom lighting. Pick Custom… under Lighting for your own: Start from one of the others, then set the Sun height (degrees above the horizon; below 0 is dusk) and where the sun shines from (90 east, 180 south, 270 west), its strength, the picture's brightness, lights on in the model and how bright they are, a background of sky or a plain colour, the ground (the lawn or a tabletop in a colour you pick), and the field of view (smaller is like a zoom lens; empty uses the camera's). It's saved with the plan, so the next render starts from it. In the Tour, a stop's Light can be My custom lighting too.

Try a new finish on a photo

Show a client their own room with new paint, a new floor or new cabinets: pick a surface in a photo and give it a new finish. The photo's own light and shadow stay, so it looks as though it was always there. Open it from:

  • a photo pin or a markup's photo: Try a new finish… in its panel;
  • the client's inspiration pictures: New finish… under a picture;
  • any picture on this computer: Design ▾ → Try a new finish on a photo…, saved as a new picture beside it.
  1. Pick the surface. Click to pick takes what's joined to the click and close in colour, stopping at edges; click more of it to add. How alike lets it reach further (or less far: lower it if it spills onto something next door). Brush and Unbrush tidy the edges.
  2. Choose the finish: a paint colour, any colour, your own colours and photos, a cabinet or countertop finish, or a wood, tile, stone, brick or wallpaper.
  3. For a floor or a wall with a pattern, tick Lay it flat in perspective and drag the four white corners onto the corners of the surface (they can go past the edge of the photo), then say how big it is (Across and Front to back). Planks and tiles then run the right way and get smaller into the distance, and the new finish stays inside the corners.

Hold to compare shows the photo as it was. Use it keeps the new picture in the plan (Undo puts the old one back); Save a copy to the computer… keeps a copy as a file and leaves the plan alone.

Lighting study: today vs new

Review ▾ → Lighting study: today vs new… settles "a brighter kitchen" with a number. For each room it adds up the light from its fittings (in lumens) and works out how much reaches the floor and counters, in foot-candles, beside what that kind of room wants (a kitchen 30 to 50, a bathroom 20 to 40, a living room or bedroom 10 to 20, a hall 5 to 10). Today is the saved Before (Before & after), or what's marked existing; lights being replaced or removed aren't in the new plan.

  • Each light has a typical output for its kind with LED bulbs (a recessed light 750 lumens, a ceiling light 1,100, a pendant 800, a chandelier 2,400). Select a light and change its Light output to the bulb's own number (on its box).
  • Render today and new makes two pictures from the 3D view's camera with the lights on and the sun down, at the same exposure, so the brighter one really looks brighter, and says how much brighter overall.
  • The foot-candles are for the room's general light. Counters, desks and vanities want 50 or more right where you work: under-cabinet lights and pendants over an island give that.

Photo eraser: take something out of a photo

Brush over something unwanted in a photo (a garbage bin, a hose, a sign, wires, a crack, a car in the drive) and Arc fills it in from what's around it. Open it from:

  • a picture traced over on the plan (a plot plan, survey or site photo): select it, then Erase from it… in the panel;
  • a markup's photo: Erase from photo… in its panel;
  • the client's inspiration pictures: Erase… under a picture;
  • any picture on this computer: Design ▾ → Touch up a photo (erase things)…, which saves it as a new picture and leaves the original alone.

Brush a little past the edges of the thing and over its shadow, then Erase. Unbrush takes brushing off again, Hold to compare shows the photo as it was, and Undo takes back the last erase. The ? beside the window's title opens this page. Zoom in for small things: the mouse wheel and right-drag, or on an iPad pinch and two fingers. Use this photo puts the touched-up photo into the plan; Undo on the top bar puts the original back.

It works best on smaller things over grass, siding, brick, sky, paving or a plain wall. Big things in busy scenes (a person in front of a detailed room) are a job for an AI photo tool, like the Erase tool in the Windows Photos app or Clean Up in an iPad's Photos app. Everything stays on your computer: nothing is sent anywhere.

6. Looking at the model in 3D

Click 3D view on the third row to show a live model beside the plan. Drag to orbit, right-drag to pan, scroll to zoom. The buttons along the top of the 3D view hold saved views, Tour, Draw pipes & wires and more.

See inside the walls, as built. In Layers on the 3D toolbar, Walls in 3D, as built has four switches: Framing (studs, plates, headers and sills), Insulation (pink batts in each stud bay: the wall type's fill, or batts in every outside wall that has no type), Drywall (on the inside faces) and Sheathing & siding (on the outside faces, in the siding's colour). Tick any of them and the 3D view shows the walls part by part, each with the door and window openings left open; tick them one at a time to build the wall up, or untick Framing to see just the board. Untick them all to go back to the full model. Picking Wall & roof framing from the view list is the framing alone, as before. Stone, block and concrete walls stay solid.

The real look, Night, the lens and the eye height (Look ▾)

The 3D view is lit the way the Render window lights a picture. Outdoors (any plan with a lot, ground, landscaping or a fence, and every Arc Landscape plan) there's a sky behind, the sky's light on every surface, sun shadows without opening the sun study, lawn out to the horizon fading into the haze, and soft shading where things meet the ground and each other (it comes in a moment after the camera stops, and goes while you turn the view, to keep it smooth; on a very big plan it is left out). Paving, gravel, mulch and deck boards have real relief that catches the light, lawns have lighter and darker patches and mowing stripes that change as you walk round them, and ponds reflect the sky. Shadows near the camera stay sharp on a big lot: they're worked out for what you're looking at. In Arc Hobby and Arc Engineering, and for a house with no lot, the background colour stays and the light is a soft studio.

Inside a building the light changes by itself as soon as the camera goes in through the walls (orbiting or in Walk), and back when you come out: the sun shines in at the windows and casts shadows (lot or no lot), the roof keeps the sky's light out, the picture opens up as your eyes do indoors, light bounced off the floor lights the ceiling, and the biggest windows of the room you're in send daylight in. Corners where walls meet the floor and ceiling are softly shaded and furniture has a soft shadow under it, even while you move. Counters, tile, stone and glass reflect the room you're in (worked out when you stop in a new room). It's made for looking round quickly; for a finished picture, use Render or Blender.

Look ▾ on the 3D bar (under More ▾ when the bar is narrow):

  • Real look, Balanced look or Simple look. The balanced look has the real look's sky, sun shadows and inside light, but leaves out the extra shading pass and draws fewer pixels; if the view gets slow it steps down by itself. The simple look is the plain light from before, the quickest to draw, for a slow computer. The web and iPad versions start with the balanced look; the desktop app starts with the real look. Arc remembers your choice (it is also in Preferences → 3D look).
  • Night: the sky goes dark, windows glow warm, and lamp posts, path lights, uplights, wall floods and fire pits are lit. The ten nearest the camera (and four uplights or floods) shine on what's around them, chosen again as you move, so a long path of lights stays quick. Pick it again to go back to day. For a finished night picture, Render → Evening (lights on).
  • Lights on: inside, the room's own lights (pendants, pot lights, lamps) light it, brightest for the fittings with the most lumens; the twelve nearest the camera, those in your room first. At Night they come on by themselves inside. Pick it again to turn them off.
  • Lens: 24 mm takes in a whole yard, 35 mm is like a phone, 50 mm is close to your eye; Standard is the 45° view from before. Saved views keep the lens.
  • Eye height: the camera at standing, seated on the patio or a child's height above the ground under it, looking out level: the way the yard will really be seen.

Plants in the real look have leaves: each clump of leaves (or a spruce's tier of needles) is a shell of small leaf sprays round a darker crown, so trees and shrubs read as trees and shrubs and let a little light through. Plants of the same kind and size share their shape (each is one of a few variations, turned its own way), which keeps a yard of hundreds of plants quick to turn. Beyond about 25 m a plant is drawn as a picture of itself, taken from eight sides; closer, it's the real plant again.

The season: the month picked under Garden over time (Arc Landscape) shows in 3D. From November to March (unless snow is turned off) snow lies wherever the sky can see the ground and the surface is flat enough to hold it: on lawns, beds, paving, roofs, furniture and shrubs, but not under a spruce, a deck or a roof, or inside the house; drifts lie along fences, deeper on the side away from the north-west wind; ponds and rinks are ice with skate marks, reflecting the sky. In October fallen leaves lie on the lawn.

Match a photo of the yard… (in Look ▾) shows your design over a real photo of your yard. Pick the photo, then click a point on it you can also find in 3D (a corner of the house, a fence post, the corner of the deck) and the same point in the 3D view; do this four times or more, spread across the photo. Clicks in 3D snap to the nearest corner. Match works out where the photo was taken from and how wide it is, and shows the design from there with the photo behind it: the plan's ground and the house are left out (they're in the photo; Show the house brings the house back), and the ground still catches the design's shadows. The panel says how closely the points agree. Save as a view… keeps it with its photo in Saved views; turning the view leaves the photo, and going to the saved view brings it back. The photo is kept in the plan file. In the Render window, pick the saved view to get a finished picture of the design over the photo, the photo's shape (at High quality: Photo can't leave the background clear for the photo).

7. Commercial drawings, takeoff on a PDF and quotes

When the project type is Commercial (IBC) (set in the panel with nothing selected), these extra drawings and checks switch on. Takeoff on a PDF, price books and quotes, at the end of this part, work for any project.

Life safety plan

Reflected ceiling plan

Building code summary

What the Status column says. OK: checked, nothing to fix. Not in plan: nothing of that kind is drawn. Check by hand: it's drawn with blocks Arc can't check (a shower block rather than a Tiled shower you draw, a plain island block rather than an Island from cabinets); the line under it says what to check. Distances to the property line read not surveyed until the lot lines are ticked From a survey (Real Property Report) in the property line's panel (New lot ticks it when the sides came from a Real Property Report's bearings). With Energy off in Checks & analysis, the energy row says so instead of "Not in plan".

In Alberta (the plan's province, from Project settings → City), the house checks follow the National Building Code – 2023 Alberta Edition, in force since 1 May 2024. Its Part 9 is the same as NBC 2020's, clause for clause, so the numbers don't change. The summary lists what Alberta allows on top (its STANDATA variances), and the checks say so where it matters:

  • A window opening control device to ASTM F2090, with an emergency release, on a bedroom escape window (23-BCV-001).
  • Bathroom, kitchen and dryer exhaust outlets 900 mm from an air intake beside or below them (23-BCV-008).
  • Unvented roofs designed to the "Alberta Unvented Roofs" report (23-BCV-017), and an intumescent vented soffit near the property line (23-BCV-018).
  • Spans from the CWC Span Book (23-BCV-007).
  • Energy: Tier 1 of 9.36 is the minimum (23-BCB-001). The permit checklist reminds you to send in the 9.36 papers.

Schedules

Below the rooms are Walls (each wall's level, length, thickness, height, material and whether it's new or existing) and Fixtures & furniture (each kind of block with how many there are), folded away until you open them. Each schedule has two buttons:

  • Columns…: untick any of its own columns to leave it off, or tick a field to add it as a column: a wall's height, a window's width, a room's ceiling height, the layer, or one of your own fields. The schedules on the drawing sheets and in the CSV show the same columns.
  • Save as CSV…: the schedule as a spreadsheet for Excel, Numbers or Google Sheets, to send to a window supplier or keep with the budget.

Click any row to select that item on the plan.

Your own fields

Keep what you need to know about each item with the plan: who's supplying it, which phase it's in, the trade, whether it's paid. Review ▾ → Schedules… → Your own fields… (or search for your own fields). Give the field a name, pick its kind (text, a number, yes / no, or a list of choices) and tick which items it's for: walls, rooms, doors & windows, blocks or stairs. Then:

  • Select an item and fill the field in on the side panel, under Your fields.
  • Select several of the same kind (three chairs, six windows) and the side panel's Properties sets one value on all of them at once, your fields and their sizes too. "varies" means they differ now. It's one undo step.
  • Add the field as a column on any schedule with Columns…, and Ctrl+K finds items by its value.

Removing a field takes its values off every item. The fields are saved with the plan.

The cabinet schedule. When the plan has cabinets, the same window lists Cabinets: every cabinet, filler, panel, island and built-in with its tag, room, type, width, height and depth, construction, door style, finish, hardware and notes (crown, light rail, specialty insides). Tags are filled in for you (B1, B2… for base cabinets, W for wall, T for tall, F for fillers, P for panels, I for islands, BI for built-ins); type your own in a cabinet's Tag box. Save as CSV… makes a spreadsheet for the cabinet maker, and Add the cabinet schedule to the drawing sheets puts it on its own sheet; once it is on a sheet, the floor plan sheets show each cabinet's tag.

Shop drawings and the cut list. For a cabinet maker (in Arc and ArcRes): select any cabinet and open Shop drawings and cut list in its panel, or use the buttons under the cabinet schedule. Shop drawings (PDF)… saves them; Add to the sheets puts them in the drawing set (A-711 on). Each run of cabinets (the cabinets along one wall, or an island) gets a sheet with its front elevation (every box's width, each door and drawer front's size, the toe kick, countertop, uppers and crown heights, the tags), a plan with the depths and the countertop's edge, and a section through a base and an upper with each part named; and a cut list sheet: every box's sides, bottom, top or stretchers, back, shelves and toe kick, the face frame on framed and inset cabinets, the doors (stiles the full height and rails between, unless their corners are set otherwise) and drawer fronts, and the drawer boxes, with the sheets of plywood or melamine to buy. Pick the boxes (Baltic birch 18 or 15 mm, white melamine ¾" or ⅝"), the backs and the drawer boxes for the whole plan. Sizes are to the nearest 1/16" (millimetres in a metric plan); drawer boxes are sized for undermount slides (inside width = opening − 42 mm): check your slides' instructions.

An island from cabinets. Blocks → Cabinets → Island from cabinets builds an island of real base cabinets: on one side with a seating overhang on the other, or back to back. Its panel sets the countertop (2 cm, 3 cm or a thick mitred edge), waterfall ends (the top running down to the floor at one or both ends), finished end and back panels framed like the doors or plain, the cabinet widths (or even boxes), the fronts, supports under the overhang (corbels, legs or hidden steel brackets; Arc adds corbels when a 3 cm top overhangs more than 10"), and the receptacle the electrical code asks for at a fixed island (CEC Rule 26-724 in Canada; NEC 210.52(C)(2) in the US): in an end panel or a pop-up in the top. The doors take the island's door style, construction, frame corners and hardware. Its cabinets are in the schedule as I1-1, I1-2…, in the shop drawings and the cut list, and in the estimate with the top, the waterfall ends and their mitres, the panels, the supports and the receptacle.

Wall types made of layers

A wall type is what a wall is built of, layer by layer from the outside face to the inside face: siding, house wrap, sheathing, studs with the batts between them, the vapour barrier and drywall. Select a wall and pick its Wall type in the side panel; the wall takes the type's thickness and stud size. Review ▾ → Wall types… (or the Wall types… button on a wall, or search for wall types) lists every type and its layers.

  • Effective R-value. Each type shows its RSI and R the way NBC 9.36.2.4 works it out: the studs and the batts between them together (the parallel-path method, with studs taking 23% of the wall at 16" on centre), then the other layers and the air films. A 2x6 wall with R-22 batts comes to about R-17 effective, not R-22. Layers outside a ventilated gap (siding on strapping, brick) don't count.
  • Starter types for Canadian houses: 2x6 R-22, 2x6 with mineral wool outside, 2x4 R-14, brick veneer, 2x4 and 2x6 interior, a suite wall (Type X both sides, batts, resilient channel: 45 min and the STC 43 path), an ICF basement wall, an 8" foundation, and Existing 2x4, insulation unknown for an older house (Arc assumes R-8 until you look).
  • Change a layer (its material, thickness, the stud size or spacing, what's in the cavity, or the order) and every wall of that type changes thickness to match. It's one undo step. The plan keeps its own copy of each type it uses, so a change stays in this plan. Copy this type makes a new one to change.
  • Use it in other plans. Keep in My library keeps the type with all its layers; From My library… brings it into another plan, where its R-value and cost are worked out again from its layers (in that plan's money). A layer this Arc doesn't have becomes the nearest one it has, and Arc tells you, with the R-value before and after. See My library.
  • What uses it: the heat loss (each outside wall's own R-value), the framing (the stud size), the detail drawings (each layer drawn and named), the takeoff (a line for each layer), the walls schedule (Wall type and Effective R columns), and the property-line cladding check (the type's siding and gypsum sheathing).
  • Costs are rough material prices per m² (CAD) for comparing types; the takeoff and quote price the job. Fire ratings and STC are only given where a type is built to a known assembly: confirm them with the building department.

A wall with no type works as before: just a thickness and a material. Typing a new thickness takes a wall off its type.

Windows and doors as products

A window's or door's look (casement, slider, French door…) says how it opens; its Product says what it is. Select a window or door and pick its Product in the side panel. Review ▾ → Windows and doors (products)… (or the Windows and doors… button on an opening, or search for u-value) lists every product.

  • What a product has: its frame and glass (or a door's core), the U-value (W/m²·K, with the imperial U-factor), the solar heat gain coefficient (SHGC), the Energy Rating (ER), a fire label for doors, the maker and series, and a price (per ft² of window or per door, CAD).
  • Built-in products are typical values to compare with: vinyl double and triple low-e, fibreglass triple, aluminum-clad wood, thermally broken aluminum, insulated steel and fibreglass entry doors, a 20-minute garage-to-house door, a patio door, and four Existing windows (single glass, single with a storm, old aluminum, 1980s–90s vinyl) for a renovation. For the window you buy, put the values from its label in your own product.
  • A maker's list. Import a maker's list (CSV)… adds a row for each product. Columns, in any order: Name, Kind (window or door), Maker, Series, Frame, Glazing, U, SHGC, ER, Price per ft2, Price, Fire, Core, Glass. A U under 0.7 is read as the imperial U-factor. Save an example list… gives you one to fill in.
  • Use it in other plans. Keep in My library keeps a window or door with its U-value, SHGC, ER and price; From My library… brings it into another plan. Prices here are typed in Canadian dollars: if Preferences → Prices and money is set to another country, Arc says so when it comes in, because the estimate changes the price with a rough price level, not today's exchange rate. See My library.
  • ENERGY STAR. A window meets ENERGY STAR in Canada (2020 criteria) with a U of 1.22 or lower, or an ER of 34 or higher; Arc says so beside the product.
  • Replacing windows. A window marked Replace (Existing vs new) also gets Old window: name what's there now and the panel and schedule show the old and new U side by side, and how much less heat goes out through it.
  • What uses it: the heat loss (each window's and door's own U, and its SHGC for sun through the glass), the takeoff (the product's name and price), and the window and door schedules (Product and U columns, once any opening has a product; Maker, SHGC, ER and Price can be added with Choose columns).

An opening with no product counts as before: a generic window (U 1.70 W/m²·K) or door.

Energy worksheet (NBC 9.36)

Review ▾ → Energy (NBC 9.36)… fills in the prescriptive path of NBC 9.36 (Tier 1), the energy form a permit office asks for on a new house or an addition.

  • Climate zone from the place's heating degree-days (Project settings → Location; or type them in). Edmonton has 5120, so it's zone 7A.
  • Which place? Project settings → Location lists the places a province at a time. Under the picker Arc says how far to trust the numbers: checked against the building code's own table (NBC 2020 Appendix C, Table C-2), not yet checked line by line, or, for a town the table doesn't list (Airdrie, St. Albert, Okotoks and others), borrowed from the nearest place that is listed, with how far away it is. For a borrowed town, ask the building department for its own numbers. Where a city publishes its own frost depth for footings (Calgary, Edmonton, Lethbridge, Airdrie and others), the foundation notes give it with where it's from.
  • Walls use their wall types' effective R-values; a wall with no type says so. Foundation walls (on levels below the ground floor) are checked against the below-grade values.
  • Windows and doors use their products' U-values (or the Energy Rating instead, for windows). Generic ones count at U 1.70 and say so. A garage's overhead doors, and its doors and windows to the outside, are left out: the garage isn't heated, so it's outside the part of the house the code checks. For a garage kept warm all winter, set Garage to Heated in the worksheet: its doors, windows and ceiling are then checked with the house. In Edmonton (zone 7A, U 1.44 or less) a double-glazed patio door (U 1.8) falls short: pick Vinyl patio door, triple low-e, argon (U 1.1) in its Product.
  • Ceilings, vaulted ceilings and the slab take their insulation from the choices in the window (blown cellulose for attics, batts in rafters for vaulted ceilings, XPS under the slab), worked out as effective values the way the code's appendix says.
  • With a heat-recovery ventilator (an HRV on the plan, or chosen in the window) the code's second table applies, which asks less of walls and ceilings in the colder zones.
  • The trade-off (9.36.2.11): when walls or ceilings fall short, Arc tries trading them against each other by area, as long as none goes below 55% (walls, vaulted ceilings) or 60% (attics) of its own value.
  • A renovation counts only the new work and the parts marked Replace.
  • It prints as a sheet: in Sheets, add a view → Energy (NBC 9.36). The permit checklist shows its result.

Floors over unheated spaces are found from the plan: the parts of a heated room over a garage, a porch or another unheated room on the floor below, and the parts that stand out past the floor below (a cantilever or a bump-out). Pick their insulation under Floors over unheated space in the form. The degree-days and the 2020 window values are as Arc knows them: check them with your building department.

Heat loss, room by room

Tick Heat loss, heating and cooling in Checks & analysis first (a new plan leaves it off). Then Review ▾ → Heat loss, room by room… (or the button in the Energy window) works out how much heat each room loses on the coldest design day and gains on a hot one, the numbers a heating contractor sizes the furnace or heat pump and each room's supply from.

  • From the plan's own parts: each room's outside walls (their wall types), windows and doors (their products' U-values and SHGC, and the way they face), skylights, the ceiling under the attic or a vaulted ceiling, a floor over the garage or out past the floor below, the slab edge, and basement walls and floors, where the ground helps.
  • Vaulted ceilings and dormers: a vaulted room counts its ceiling at its true sloped area and its air at its real height, not as if it were flat; a dormer's window counts in the room its opening is over (at a generic window's U-value).
  • The weather is your place's design temperatures from the building code's table (Project settings → Location), or type the winter one in. Inside is 22 °C in winter and 24 °C in summer.
  • Air leaking in comes from the airtightness you pick (air changes an hour at 50 Pa, from a blower-door test; in Alberta an untested house counts as 2.5). Ventilation comes from the principal fan or HRV on the plan, less what an HRV gets back.
  • Each room shows walls, glass, ceiling, floor and air in watts, the total in watts and BTU/h, and the heat gain. Save the sheet for the heating contractor prints it as a PDF, with every room's parts, areas and U-values.

It is an estimate in the style of CSA F280, not a stamped F280 calculation: the heating contractor's own F280 goes in with the heating permit. Use Arc's to talk it over and check theirs. (Heating & cooling under Budget keeps its own simpler numbers for equipment and ducts.)

Energy performance and tiers

Review ▾ → Energy performance & tiers… estimates the house's energy over a year against the code's reference house: the same rooms and windows, each part built just to the prescriptive values for your climate zone, at 2.5 air changes, with the same kind of heating at the code's lowest efficiency.

  • It shows how much less energy the house uses, and the tier of the 2020 National Building Code (9.36.7) it would likely reach: Tier 2 at 10% less energy and 5% less heat loss, Tier 3 at 20% and 10%, Tier 4 at 40% and 20%, Tier 5 at 70% and 40%. Tier 2 and up also keep the peak cooling no higher than the reference house's. In Alberta, Tier 1 is the minimum.
  • What would move it: triple-glazed windows, tighter air sealing, a better HRV, insulation outside the walls, more in the attic, basement wall insulation or a heat pump, each with how many points it adds.
  • Heating, ventilation and hot water (9.36.3, 9.36.4): pick the furnace, boiler or heat pump and the water heater and type their efficiencies from the labels (AFUE, HSPF, UEF, the HRV's recovery). Arc checks each against the code's minimum (a gas furnace 95% AFUE, an HRV 60% at 0 °C, a gas tank water heater by its size).

It is not HOT2000 and not an EnerGuide rating. For a tier above 1, an energy advisor models the house and a blower-door test confirms the airtightness. The permit checklist and the code summary show these results.

Size up? (beams and headers near their limit)

When Arc sizes a beam or header and the smallest that holds is past 95% of what it can take, it offers the next size up: a porch beam (porch panel), a header over a window or door (the wall's panel, or a big-glass opening's own panel), a ridge beam over a vaulted room (the roof's framing), the beam that replaces a wall you take out (the Can I take this wall out? window), and a beam you draw. The code allows 100%: the room above 95% is for a snowier winter than the code's, snow sliding off a higher roof, or a heavier roof later. Click Use … and that size is drawn, listed and priced, and kept in the plan. If the plan changes so your size no longer holds, Arc goes back to the smallest that does and says so. Back to the smallest that holds undoes your pick.

Can I take this wall out?

Select a wall and click Can I take this wall out?… in its panel (or find it with Ctrl+K). Arc works out what the wall holds up from what's drawn: the roof (with your city's snow), ceiling joists under a stick-framed roof, and the floors above.

  • Not load-bearing: it says so, with what to check first (wires, pipes, ducts, and the attic and basement for anything not drawn).
  • Load-bearing: it sizes a beam to replace it (built-up lumber by CSA O86, or an engineered LVL beam as a starting point), the posts at each end with the load on them, and the concrete pad under each post (NBC 9.15.3.4). Posts on an upper floor need solid blocking down to a beam or footing.
  • When to call an engineer: an engineered beam, a wall that holds up more than one floor, or a post load more than built-up studs carry. Many building departments ask for an engineer's letter for any removed bearing wall.
  • Take it out and put in this beam marks the wall as being removed and draws the beam and posts, in one undo step.

View ▾ → Load path on the plan shows the bearing walls as orange bands with what they carry, beams dashed red, and posts with their loads and pads. The roof and floor framing plan sheets show it too. These are Arc's estimates for planning: confirm them with your building department, a designer or an engineer.

Print this floor plan

For a quick printout without setting up sheets: File ▾ or Export ▾ → Print this floor plan… (Ctrl+P). The floor you're on, as it shows (hide layers or turn on the clean floor plan first to leave things off), goes on one Letter page, landscape, with a title block, at the same scale as the other floors (the one the biggest floor needs), so the plans compare. The window shows the page: Print… opens the print window (on an iPad too), and Save PDF… keeps a file to email or print later.

Sheets

Sharp lines in the PDF. Plans, schedules, the code table and the title block go into the PDF as real lines and text at true scale. They stay crisp however far you zoom in or however large you print, wall lines are heavier than furniture, patterns and dimensions, and you can search the PDF for a room name or a door mark. Drafted elevations and sections are lines and text too (below); 3D views and the other looks are still pictures. If a sheet doesn't look right in the PDF, pick it in Sheets and set In the PDF to A picture of the sheet: the whole sheet is then one picture, as it was in earlier versions.

Drafted elevations and sections. In Arc and ArcRes, a new sheet set draws its elevations and sections the way a designer draws a permit and tender set: the ground line and cut walls heaviest, the building's outline heavy, edges further back lighter, and light hatches for siding, brick, stone and the roof taken from your finishes. Sections show each cut wall layer by layer with its insulation, the floors and roof, the footings, slab and earth. On the right are markers for grade, each floor, top of plate, window heads and the ridge, with floor-to-floor heights and the overall height (and your height limit, if you set one in Design ▸ Specs). On the left are notes with leaders made from the plan, such as "Fibre-cement lap siding, 6" exposure" or each wall's assembly ("R-22 batt, 2x6 @ 16" o.c., 1/2" drywall"). Pick a view on a sheet and press Notes on this drawing… to reword a note, take one off, or add your own by clicking where it points; your notes are saved with the plan. To go back to a picture, set the view's Look to Rendered or Line drawing. Plants are left off drafted drawings.

Create the sheet set also makes:

  • A-100 Foundation plan: the lowest floor's walls with the footings under the outside walls drawn dashed, pads under any posts, and notes giving the footing size for the number of floors above, the foundation walls, the slab and the drainage. The sizes are typical starting points for good soil; have the building department or an engineer confirm them.
  • The foundation as it's built. The same footings are in the 3D view (under the ground: turn the ground off or look from below), in sections and in exports: a basement's under its floor; a slab-on-grade house's below the frost line for the plan's place (1.2 m when Arc doesn't know it), with an 8" frost wall up to the floor. Inside walls that carry a floor get their own strip footing under the slab (NBC Table 9.15.3.4). A garage (a room named Garage or Carport) gets its own 4" slab, and beside a basement, a frost wall and footing that step down to the basement's footing, at most 24" up for each 24" along (NBC 9.15.3.8). The foundation plan draws them all, and the takeoff counts the footing and frost wall concrete in cubic yards.
  • More of the foundation. Each of these comes from the drawing, so redraw or set it and the footings follow:
    • ICF walls (an ICF wall type on the basement): counted as blocks, corner blocks, the concrete fill in cubic yards and the rebar, with drywall on their inside face.
    • Pads under posts and beams: a pad under each drawn post, and under a beam's ends and any post on the floors above whose load comes down to it, sized roughly from those loads (to be engineered).
    • A walkout: where the ground outside is low, the footings go the frost depth below it on a frost wall, stepping along the wall with the ground. The digging, backfill and damp-proofing follow the ground drawn round the house, or the design grades.
    • A garage: beside a heated house its frost wall goes to the house's frost depth; a garage standing on its own goes to the city's figure for an unheated garage where it gives one (Edmonton: 1.5 m). The foundation notes say which depth was used and where it's from.
    • A renovation: walls, posts and rooms kept as pre-existing keep the foundation they have, so they get no new footings, digging, weeping tile or sump.
  • Load-bearing walls. Select a wall: its panel's Load-bearing says whether it holds up a floor or roof. Auto lets Arc work it out from what's drawn above it; Yes or No sets it, for a wall Arc can't judge (a partition under the joists, or a wall carrying something you haven't drawn). A wall set as load-bearing with nothing Arc can see on it gets headers by their width and a note to have them checked; one set as not load-bearing holds up no joists in the floor framing.
  • A-201 Exterior elevations: all four sides on one sheet. For a long house or one on a big site, where four together would be smaller than 1/8" = 1'-0" (1:100), they go two to a sheet, one above the other, so they print larger: south and north on A-201, east and west on A-202.
  • Interior elevations (A-202 and on, or A-203 and on when the outside elevations take two sheets) of each kitchen, bath and laundry, and any room with cabinets or plumbing fixtures: every wall seen straight on from inside, with the floor, the ceiling height and the wall's length. The floor plan sheets show a marker in the room pointing at each wall, with the view's number and the sheet.
  • A-701 Door & window schedules for a house (a commercial set gets door and room schedules on A-701 and the window schedule on A-702). The floor plan sheets then show the door and window tags that match.

To add one yourself, pick a sheet and click Foundation plan, Interior elevation, Door schedule or Window schedule under Add a view. For an interior elevation, select the room first (or pick it afterwards) and choose the wall.

A view's own layers: select a floor plan view on a sheet and set Layers to Its own, then tick the layers it shows. It keeps them whatever the plan shows, so a clean A-101 can sit beside an electrical or plumbing plan of the same floor. As the plan shows them now follows the Layers panel, as before.

Detail drawings

A close-up you draw on its own page, off the floor plan: a wall corner, a window sill, a stair nosing. Draw a Detail callout box around the spot (Draw ▾), select it and click Make a detail drawing. It opens on its own page, starting with the plan's lines in the box and the wall layers drawn in: drywall, studs (with the usual X), batt insulation in outside walls, sheathing and siding, with notes beside it. Edit it with the drafting tools in Draw ▾ and Modify ▾ (lines, hatch, text, dimensions, trim, offset…); walls, rooms and furniture stay on the floors. ← Back to plan (or picking a floor in the Level list) goes back. To start a blank one, pick + New detail drawing… in the Level list.

Every detail drawing is listed in the Level list under Details. With one open, Settings renames it and sets the scale it prints at (3" = 1'-0" and so on), and Delete removes it. Create the sheet set puts them on Details sheets, and in Sheets you can add one to any sheet as a Detail drawing view.

Permit-set check

Review ▾ → Permit-set check… goes through what building departments usually ask for and marks each item ready (green), to look at (amber) or missing (red):

  • Title block: project name, site address, owner and designer (from Project settings).
  • Drawings: a site plan with property lines, a plan of every floor, all four elevations, a building section, roof and floor framing, a detail, the code summary and an electrical layout, a foundation plan and the door and window schedules (and, for commercial projects, life safety plans and the room schedule). Create the sheet set fixes most of these in one click.
  • On the plans: rooms named, wall dimensions on, and how many design-check warnings are left to fix or explain.

Check the DXF export saves each floor as DXF and reads it back with the program's own DXF reader (the one that imports AutoCAD drawings), comparing the lines, text, wall size and room names. AutoCAD itself and the ODA converter for DWG aren't part of this check. Fill in the building department, application number, Prepared by and Reviewed by, then Save review set PDF…: the checklist and DXF results, signature and date lines and a stamp box, followed by every drawing sheet. Each department has its own list, so check theirs before you submit.

Takeoff on a PDF (count and measure)

For a trade pricing someone else's drawings: Budget ▾ → Takeoff on a PDF (count & measure)…. Pick the PDF (or a picture of the plan), then click two ends of a dimension you know and type its length so the drawing is to scale. The Takeoff on a PDF tool then opens with a list of items in the panel.

  • Add item (or a starter chip such as Outlets, Lights, Pipe run, Floor area) and choose how it's measured: count, length or area. Each item gets its own colour.
  • Count: click each symbol. A numbered dot marks it.
  • Length: click along the run, then press Enter, double-click or right-click to finish.
  • Area: click round the outline and finish the same way, or click the first point again.

The totals show beside each item. To remove a mark, pick the Select arrow, click it and press Delete. The items join Takeoff & cost estimate as a PDF takeoff section, so they're priced and go into the quote with everything else. They're saved with the plan.

Electrical: circuits & panel

Budget ▾ → Electrical: circuits & panel… lays out the circuits the way an electrician would: general outlets and lighting on AFCI circuits, two 20 A kitchen counter circuits, a GFCI circuit for each bathroom, laundry, garage and outdoors, and a dedicated circuit for each big appliance (range, dryer, water heater, heat pump, EV charger, hot tub…). Rooms with no outlets or lights drawn are estimated. It shows the panel schedule with each breaker's slots and leg, the service size (NEC 220.82 load calculation, with the minimum and the size worth having), cable by gauge measured from the panel, and the boxes, devices and breakers. Tick Put these in the takeoff and quote to price them (it replaces the electrical blocks and drawn wires there). Save PDF… gives the panel schedule and an order list. Have a licensed electrician confirm it for your local code.

In Canada: the Canadian Electrical Code. When the plan uses the NBC (File ▾ → Plan info, or Canadian plans by default), the electrical plan follows the CEC instead of the US NEC: NMD90 cable, kitchen counter receptacles on 20 A circuits with no more than two receptacles each (Rule 26-724), so a long counter gets more circuits, and none of the counter more than 900 mm from a receptacle; tamper-resistant receptacles (26-712) and arc-fault protection (26-656). The service comes from Rule 8-200: 5 000 W for the first 90 m² of living area (basements at 75%) and 1 000 W for each 90 m² more, electric heat (the first 10 kW in full, 75% after), air conditioning in full, the range at 6 000 W plus 40% over 12 kW, EV chargers and hot tubs in full, and other loads over 1 500 W at 25% with a range. A home of 80 m² or more gets at least 100 A. The window shows the working, for your electrician and the electrical permit.

Plumbing: fixtures, pipes & water heater

Budget ▾ → Plumbing… (ArcRes: Budget ▾ → Plumbing summary…) counts the toilets, sinks, tubs, showers, dishwasher, washer and hose bibs on the plan; baths, kitchens and laundries with none drawn get the usual ones (marked assumed). From their supply and drainage fixture units it works out the peak flow and the water main size, the building drain (3" or 4"), the stacks and roof vents (fixtures within about 15' share one), and a water heater: a tank sized by the bathrooms, or a tankless heater by the showers that can run at once. Pipe runs are measured from a PEX manifold at the water heater (place a Water heater block to measure from it) and from each fixture to its stack. It lists the pipe by size, fittings, valves, traps, flanges, manifold, heater, cleanouts and flashings, and flags long hot-water runs, island sinks and basement fixtures that may need a pump. Tick the box to put it in the takeoff as Plumbing. ArcRes shows the same results in plain words.

In Canada: the National Plumbing Code. On an NBC plan the drains use the NPC's hydraulic loads (Table 2.4.9.3): a toilet with a tank 4 fixture units, a basin 1, a tub, shower or kitchen sink 1.5, a washer or floor drain 2, and a dishwasher draining through the sink nothing more. A bathroom group (a toilet, basin and tub or shower in one room) counts as 6 instead of their sum, and as 3.6 supply units. Stacks are 2" up to 24 units and 3" up to 102 (Table 2.4.10.6.-A), and the building drain is 3" up to 27 units at 1:50 and 4" from the third toilet on (2.4.10.6.-C). The fixture schedule marks each bathroom group.

Heating & cooling: loads & ducts

Budget ▾ → Heating & cooling… works out each room's heat loss and heat gain from its outside walls, windows and glass doors, the ceiling under the roof, the slab edge and air leakage (a simplified Manual J), for the climate you pick (cold, mixed or hot). From those it sizes a furnace and air conditioner or a heat pump (two systems when cooling is over 5 tons), the airflow each room needs, the supply ducts, trunk, registers and returns, measured from a furnace or air handler on the plan. Rooms with lots of glass are flagged. Tick the box to put it in the takeoff as HVAC. Have a heating contractor run a full load calculation before you buy equipment.

Framing: structure & hardware

Budget ▾ → Framing… (ArcRes: Structure & lumber…) gathers the frame in one place: the wall studs and boards from the cut lists, the headers over every opening (an engineered LVL over 8'), the floor joists under each upper floor and the roof framing, each with its span checks; the roof sheathing and subfloor; the hardware that ties it together (hurricane ties, anchor bolts every 6', sill gasket, joist hangers and hold-downs); a basic nailing schedule; and everything an engineer should look at. Tick the box to put the sheathing and hardware in the takeoff as Framing package. Shear walls and wind and snow connections depend on your area: an engineer or the building department confirms them.

Assemblies, waste & cut lists

Budget ▾ → Assemblies, waste & cut lists… has three tabs:

  • Cut lists: the framing of every new wall in boards to buy. Full-height studs come as precut or stock-length studs, one per board; plates, headers, sills, jacks and cripples are packed into 8'–16' boards with an ⅛" saw cut, and the PDF lists every board's cuts and offcut. Drywall sheets are counted room by room (walls less doors and windows, plus the ceiling, moisture-resistant board in baths and laundries), and flooring by room in boxes (or square yards of carpet).
  • Assemblies: the small materials that go with every square foot: house wrap and vapour barrier on outside walls, compound, tape, screws and corner bead with drywall, underlayment under floors, roofing underlayment and nails, and flashing tape, shims, foam and casing for each door or window. Change the rates to match how you build, add your own, and tick the box to put them in the takeoff as Assemblies & sundries.
  • Waste: a percentage for each kind of material (framing 10%, drywall 12%, tile 15%, roofing 15%…). Tick the box and every takeoff line includes its waste, with sheet counts worked out the same way.

The parts list and your own prices

In Arc and ArcRes a new plan's Budget ▾ → Takeoff & cost estimate… is a parts list first (ArcRes calls it Parts list and prices): everything you need to build it, counted and measured, with no prices filled in. Type Your price on a line when you know it; it's saved with the plan and dated. Arc's Typical price shows greyed beside it, to check a quote against: use copies it in for that line, use typical on a section's heading for that section's blank lines, and Use typical for every blank line (ArcRes: Fill in typical prices) for the lot. Under the totals it says how many lines are priced (12 of 85 lines priced), so a total isn't taken for the whole cost too soon.

Your last price. A price you typed on an earlier job is remembered on this computer and shows under the line as Your last price: $X (March 2026). It's never filled in by itself, since prices change: click it to use it. A plan made before November 2026 still fills in typical prices and your price book as it always did, so its totals don't change; Count only my prices… at the top switches it.

What's on the list. Besides what's drawn, it counts what goes with it: the framing hardware (hurricane ties, anchor bolts, sill gasket, hangers, nails, subfloor glue, roof sheathing) and the assemblies (house wrap, vapour barrier, drywall mud, tape, screws and corner bead, primer, flashing tape, shims, foam, casing); the soffit, ridge and hip cap, valley flashing, roof vents (1 sq ft for each 300 sq ft of attic), attic insulation, baffles and hatch; rebar, the gravel and poly under the slab and a radon pipe, and for a basement the weeping tile, damp-proofing, digging, backfill and sump; a countertop on cabinets with no slab picked, cabinet hinges, pulls and slides; door locksets, hinges and stops; an open porch's floor, columns, beam, roof, ceiling, railing and steps; a deck's guard and stairs; a plumbing rough-in for each fixture; and a dormer added to a roof that's already there. Nothing is counted twice: an island's top with a slab, the floor tile under a tiled shower, the siding, and the assemblies' transition strips, ice and water and casing where the takeoff measured them itself. The cabinet shop drawings and cut list leave out cabinets that are already there, and a long cut list carries on onto another sheet.

Services: gas, ducts, wiring and the lines to the street. The list counts what the drawing shows only as a symbol, or not at all:

  • Gas: a line from the gas meter to each gas appliance (furnace, boiler, water heater, gas fireplace, garage heater, and a range, cooktop or rangetop whose Style in its panel is Gas or Dual fuel), along the walls and floors, each piece sized for the load past it from the low-pressure table (1/2" to the far ends, bigger near the meter), and a shut-off valve and connector at each. No gas meter drawn: it's measured from the outside wall nearest the furnace. Gas pipe you draw yourself is counted as drawn instead. A gas fitter sizes and permits it (CSA B149.1). One choice, the Style of a range, cooktop or rangetop (Gas, Electric, Induction, and for a range Dual fuel), sets how it looks (burners and grates, or a glass top), its gas line and its wiring: gas gets a 120 V, 15 A circuit for its igniters and clock, dual fuel a 40 A, 240 V circuit for its electric oven, electric or induction a 240 V circuit (50 A for a range, 40 A for a cooktop or rangetop).
