A range or cooktop with no style chosen is electric, drawn and wired; a rangetop with none is gas. A pro range has its own Fuel: dual fuel unless you set it to gas. The new lines keep Arc's typical price beside them, not in the total, until you click use.
  • Ducts and vents: return air from each return grille to the furnace (one in each bedroom and a central one when none are drawn); an HRV's two ducts and wall hoods to the outside and its ducts inside; each dryer's 4" vent and hood; each bathroom fan's 4" insulated duct and cap (one assumed in a bathroom with no fan drawn, unless an HRV takes the bathrooms' air); each range hood's duct and cap, with make-up air (a damper that lets air back in when the hood runs, NBC 9.32.3.8); and each gas fireplace's direct vent. A duct drawn from the appliance is counted as drawn instead.
  • Wiring: a home run drawn as an arrow toward the panel, the way electrical plans show it, is counted the whole way to the panel along the walls and floors with 10' to spare, by its wire size (an EV charger's 6 AWG run, for example).
  • Heated floor: Blocks → Bathroom → Heated floor mat, sized to the open floor (a spa ensuite, under the tile). The list prices the mat by its area and adds its thermostat, its 15 A circuit to the panel and the breaker; with Electrical: circuits & panel in the estimate, the circuit is on the panel schedule instead.
  • Services to the street: the water, sanitary and (with a basement) storm services and their trench, from the house to the property line on the street side (the side the driveway goes to; draw the property line for the real length), and a backwater valve on the sanitary line. Edmonton requires one on every new house.
  • Drywall by type: sheets of regular 1/2" board, 5/8" Type X on the garage's walls and ceiling, and moisture-resistant board in the bathrooms, worked out from the room on each side of each wall. The ceilings' sheets are listed to order (the Ceilings section prices ceiling drywall by the square foot already).
  • Concrete in cubic yards: a garage slab above a basement, an apron at each overhead garage door when no driveway is drawn, and a porch slab, besides the slab, footings, walls, driveway and patios, which already were.

The owner's list. Furniture, fitness and game-room equipment (a golf screen, a treadmill, a pool table), lamps and decor go on their own list at the bottom, Furniture & equipment (owner's list), out of the builder's total. Tick Count it in the total on its heading to add it in. Lights the electrician hangs (pendants, chandeliers, sconces) stay in the builder's list.

Lines with no price. Under the totals, Arc says how many lines have no price yet, so the total leaves them out, and names the first few: get a quote for them, type a price, or click Use typical for the ones Arc has a typical price for.

Each wall as it's really built. The notes on elevations and sections, the wall's Material and the parts list name what each outside wall is clad in, from its Exterior finish: stone, stucco, panels or fibre-cement, and vinyl only where it really is vinyl. Some things come with their build-up:

  • Stucco: a second layer of building paper, galvanized lath, the scratch, brown and finish coats in bags, weep screed along the bottom of the ground-floor walls, casing bead round the windows and doors, and corner aid. The plastering crew's hours go in the build budget.
  • Stone veneer: the stone in boxes of about 10 sq ft and its corners, Type S mortar, a veneer tie every 2.6 sq ft, rainscreen strapping, base flashing with weep vents and flashing over the openings, at about $14 a sq ft of materials (CAD; US and UK prices worked out from it), plus a mason's 0.2 hours a sq ft: about $27–32 a sq ft installed.
  • A stone or brick panel from the wall designer on an outside face, and a block or 3D shape painted in stone or brick outside the rooms or up a gable, count as that stone, not as a feature or as furniture, and the wall's own siding behind a panel comes off.
  • Roofs: a porch's or covered deck's roof is counted with the roofs, by its material (a standing-seam deck roof as standing-seam metal). The eave protection comes in whole 3′ courses (a 24″ eave takes two) and down the valleys; there's starter strip, step, apron and kick-out flashing where a roof meets a wall above it, intake vents in a wood soffit, and the shingles' waste on its own line (10%, or 15% with hips or valleys) unless Waste is on.
  • A floor painted with a material from the library (walnut over a vinyl-plank room, say) is counted as that floor: Hardwood – Walnut. An island's countertop line says its own finish. Porch posts from My columns are drawn in the recipe's finish and counted as the recipe's posts, brackets and piers. A deck's stairs are drawn in 3D and on its framing plan, and counted once: not again when you draw a stair against the deck yourself. The Solid-core, 20-minute (garage to house) door is counted only on a door between the garage and the house, and that door gets it when it has no product picked.

A sub's price for a section

When a sub (the stair guy, the roofer, the cabinet shop) gives you one price for their part, click + Sub price (ArcRes: + Trade's price) on that section's heading and type the amount (before tax), who, the date of the quote and a note. It becomes the section's total; a green line under the heading says who and when, and the parts stay listed with their quantities, greyed and not added, so you can see what's in it and check it against Arc's typical prices. In the build budget it's one installed line, with no labour added. If you are the sub, leave it blank and price the parts yourself. It takes the place of the old Contractor tick in Arc and ArcRes (a plan that had one ticked keeps it, to take off); Arc Landscape keeps the tick.

The price list

Budget ▾ → Price list (typical prices)… is every typical price Arc knows, to look up for a rough estimate or to see whether a sub's price is fair. This plan lists each line of your plan with its typical price and yours; Everything lists every price by section, and the trades' hourly rates. Type in the box to find one (shingles, 2x6, plumber). Prices are Canadian dollars first (or your country's, from File ▾ → Preferences… → Prices and money), with the date they were checked; where each comes from is in Arc's price sources (docs/price-sources-canada.md). Nothing goes into your estimate until you click copy to my price, which puts it on this plan's lines it prices, dated today.

Items that aren't in the drawing

Some costs never show in the model: permit fees, a dumpster, temporary power, door hardware, cleaning, labour by the hour. In Budget ▾ → Takeoff & cost estimate… (ArcRes: Budget ▾ → Cost estimate…), click + Add beside any category, or + Add an item not in the drawing… under the list for a new category such as Permits & fees. Give it a name, a quantity, a unit (each, feet, square feet, hours, lump sum…) and, if you know it, a price. It's marked Added by hand; type over its quantity to change it, or click × to take it out. These lines go into the totals, the quote, change orders and the spreadsheet, and they're saved with the plan.

Changing a quantity in the estimate

Every line in the takeoff has its quantity in a box: type over it (one more box of tile for breakage, the grout you'll really use) and the line, the totals and the quote follow. A line you've changed shows the counted amount beside it and ↺ to go back to it. The changes are saved with the plan and undo like any other change.

How old the prices are

Arc's typical prices say when they were last checked against suppliers (November 2026), on the takeoff, cost room by room, the quote, change orders and the parts and shopping lists. A price you type, or load from a price book, is dated that day. The takeoff and the quote say when your prices were set, and a price more than a year old gets an old tag and a warning. Rows to re-price… in the takeoff saves a spreadsheet of every line whose price is Arc's typical one, missing, or more than a year old, with why: fill in the Unit price column and load it back with Load price book…. The price book spreadsheet has a Checked column with each price's date.

Where each price comes from

Under every price in the takeoff a small tag says where it came from: yours (typed for this plan), price book (prices you typed before or loaded from a spreadsheet), trade pack (the plumbing, heating, electrical or framing pack's own starting price), typical (Arc's typical prices, a starting point for budgeting: by default Canadian prices from Canadian suppliers, western ones first, checked in November 2026; or US or UK ones worked out from them, from File ▾ → Preferences… → Prices and money; they're for learning and estimating, so check with local suppliers) or no price (counted as $0 until you type one). Hover over a tag for more. Prices you type can be taken back with ↶ Undo in the window or Ctrl+Z afterwards, in order with your other changes; undo also takes the price out of the remembered price book.

Options & upgrades

Budget ▾ → Options & upgrades… lists the parts of the plan a homeowner can choose to have or not, each priced against the standard way to build it. Arc finds them by itself: a coffered, tray or wood slat ceiling (instead of a flat painted ceiling), ceiling beams (instead of none), a premium floor such as hardwood, stone or tile (instead of the standard floor you pick at the bottom: vinyl plank, laminate, carpet or sheet vinyl; tile in a bathroom counts as standard), feature walls and panelling room by room, exterior trim, shutters and stone, each fireplace, and built-ins room by room. Anything else can be optional too: select it (a deck, a pergola, a finished basement) and choose Optional in the panel's Layer section, or Make the selection optional in the window, giving the group a name.

Each option shows what it adds and Materials lists its lines: the upgrade's materials, and the standard ones it replaces. Untick an option and the standard version goes into the plan and 3D (the coffered ceiling becomes flat, the hardwood becomes vinyl plank, the fireplace or the optional deck comes out), so the plan, the takeoff and the quote always show what's chosen; tick it again and the upgrade comes back exactly as you drew it. The totals show the standard build, the options chosen and the price as chosen. Each tick is one undo step, and the choices are saved with the plan. Options page for the homeowner (PDF) lists every option with its price, what it replaces and what's in it, with tick boxes and a signature line.

In Arc, each option shows Your cost (materials at the takeoff's prices, plus labour hours at your own labour cost), the Client price (materials with your markup, plus labour at your rate, from the quote) and your Margin. Your cost and margin are only shown to you: the homeowner's page and the quote show the client price. In ArcRes, each option shows what it adds to the budget.

Your cost and margin on a quote. In Budget ▾ → Quote for the client…, set Your labour cost $/h (what an hour of labour costs you) next to the labour rate you charge. The green box beside the page shows materials at cost and as charged, labour at cost and as charged, and your margin in dollars and as a percentage of the price. It never appears on the quote PDF.

Build budget

The takeoff's estimate is the materials. Budget ▾ → Build budget… (or the link under the takeoff's totals) is the whole cost to build the house. At the top: the total, the cost per square foot and per square metre, the construction cost per square foot, and the contingency split into a design contingency (for what isn't drawn or decided yet: 5–10% early on, 0 once the drawings are finished) and a construction contingency (surprises while it's built; the same number as the takeoff's contingency).

By trade: site work, foundation, framing, roofing, windows and doors, exterior, heating and ventilation, plumbing, electrical, insulation and drywall, paint and tile, flooring, cabinets and trim, stairs, fixtures, landscaping. Each trade shows its materials and its labour: hours from rules of thumb for every line, times the trade's hourly rate (typical Edmonton and Calgary charge-out rates; type your own in the Rate box). Click a trade to see its lines; each line has an hours box, so you can type your own hours where you know better (a crew's quote, a harder job), and blank or ↺ goes back to the rule of thumb. The builder's fee is a box too: type any %, or 0. Untick Count the labour for materials only: a new plan starts with it unticked (your own prices, or a sub's, are what count), so tick it to add the labour. A section with a sub's price is installed, with no labour on top. A stair priced by its parts gets hours for each tread, riser and stringer. Tick I'm the owner-builder when you're running the job yourself: there's no builder's fee, the warranty line becomes Alberta's Owner Builder Authorization, and you can tick I'll do it on the trades you'll do yourself (their hours still show, at no cost).

Soft costs start at typical amounts for a new house in Edmonton, each with where it comes from and the date: permits and inspections (the City's 2026 fee schedule, by floor area), engineering, survey and Real Property Report, design, the new home warranty (Alberta requires one on a new house), utility hook-ups, builder's risk insurance, and construction loan interest. Type your own amount, or 0 for none. A renovation starts with no warranty or hook-ups.

Tax: a new plan charges the sales tax where it's built (Project settings → Location): 5% GST in Alberta and the territories, GST and 7% PST in BC (on materials), GST and 6% PST in Saskatchewan (on labour too), GST and 7% RST in Manitoba, 13% HST in Ontario, 14% in Nova Scotia, 15% in New Brunswick, Newfoundland and Labrador and PEI, GST and 9.975% QST in Quebec, 20% VAT in the UK; in the US, type your own. GST isn't charged on permits, insurance, the warranty or loan interest. A plan saved before keeps the tax it had. Save spreadsheet (CSV) keeps every line with its labour; Save PDF the headline, the trades, the soft costs and the totals. A plan with its own prices shows only the link under the takeoff's totals, not a whole-cost figure. Don't want the whole-cost line under the takeoff's totals? Click its Hide; untick or tick Show the whole cost in the Takeoff window in the build budget to change it back (on this computer).

Allowances

Budget ▾ → Allowances… (or the button in the build budget) lists each category of finish (paint, flooring, lighting, furniture…) with its allowance, what you've chosen and how far over or under it is, and each item's rooms, cost, supplier, lead time in weeks, and Ordered and Delivered ticks. It's the same information as the shopping list and Selections & allowances, not a copy: an allowance typed here is the one on the selections page, Ordered is the shopping list's Bought tick, and invoices filed under the category in Purchases & invoices show as Spent. It warns about the longest wait not yet ordered. Save it as a spreadsheet or a PDF.

Cost room by room

Budget ▾ → Cost room by room… splits the estimate by room: each room's walls, doors and windows (a wall between two rooms is shared between them), its floor and baseboard, and the cabinets, fixtures and furniture standing in it, with the room's total and cost per square foot. The roof, foundation, outside work, stairs, pipes and wires and lines added by hand are a Whole house line, so the rooms, that line, and contingency and tax add up to the estimate's total. Click a room's name to find it on the plan; Save spreadsheet (CSV) keeps the table.

Compare a contractor's quote

Budget ▾ → Compare a contractor's quote… lines a real quote up against Arc's estimate, room by room. Paste the quote (copy the text from the PDF or email) or type it a line at a time, with the contractor's name, and click Read the quote.

  • Every line with a dollar amount becomes an item. Arc guesses its room from the words: a room's name in the plan, kitchen words (cabinets, counters), bathroom words (shower, vanity, toilet), and so on, or a heading above it ("KITCHEN" then its items). Permits, electrical and "all rooms" work go to Whole house; tax goes beside contingency and tax; subtotals, totals and deposits are left out so nothing counts twice. Sizes and counts (36", 42 sq ft, 12 boxes) aren't read as money.
  • Open The quote's lines to change any line's room or amount, or take a line out.
  • A difference over 20% (and about $700) is flagged: Quote is higher (ask what's included), Quote is lower (check nothing is missing), or Not in the quote. Big gaps are usually something one side counted and the other didn't.
  • Keep several quotes in the plan and switch between them; + A new quote… adds another.

Purchases & invoices

Budget ▾ → Purchases & invoices… keeps what's been bought for the job with the plan. Drop invoices and receipts on the window (PDFs or photos, several at once) or click + Add an invoice…. A PDF invoice is read for you: the supplier, invoice number, date, subtotal, tax and total are filled in to check; a photo is kept as a picture. Give each one its category (the estimate's categories, plus permits, rentals and subcontractor labour), the room it's for and whether it's paid. The window shows what's been spent and what's unpaid, and the estimate's budget against what's been spent for each category, in red where it's over. Tick Client on the invoices the client may see: PDF for the client lists only those.

Client brief & priorities (only you see it)

Budget ▾ → Client brief & priorities (only you)… holds everything about the client in one place: their details, the all-in budget, a contingency and the finishes & upgrades allowance, their first, second and third priorities and what matters least (the kitchen, the primary suite, curb appeal, the basement…), a quick survey with drop-down answers to fill in together (how long they'll stay, who lives there, entertaining, cooking, working from home, pets, upkeep, energy, accessibility, storage, a basement suite), your notes and a dated meeting log.

From that it suggests, from the designer tips: Put the money here (the biggest visual difference for what matters to them), Save here (what costs a lot and shows little for this client, or looks the same for less) and Don't skimp (what doesn't show but is hard to fix later), each with the reasons, such as First priority: Kitchen or Selling within 5 years: finishes that pay back at resale. It sets the budget against the plan's cost estimate after the contingency and splits the allowance across the rooms and trades, leaning to their priorities.

The brief is saved with the plan but never goes on the quote, the options page, the share page or a PDF. To send the plan file itself, use Save a copy for the client…: the copy leaves out the brief and the invoices you haven't shared. In ArcRes it's My brief & priorities: the same survey and suggestions for your own home.

Designer tips: where the money shows

Guide ▾ → Designer tips: where the money shows is a sheet to print and keep beside you with a client, in four columns: the biggest difference for the money (paint, the garage and front doors, the front elevation, layered lighting, the kitchen cabinets and island, one continuous floor, a fireplace wall, the stair…), the same look for less, what costs a lot and shows little, and what doesn't show but shouldn't be skimped (insulation and windows, ventilation, waterproofing, sound insulation, wiring for later), each marked for impact and relative cost, with where the eye goes first and a few rules that make a house look designed. The Client brief draws its suggestions from the same list.

Price books

In Budget ▾ → Takeoff & cost estimate…, Save price book… writes your prices to a spreadsheet (CSV) and Load price book… reads one back, so your own prices are ready for every job. Edit it in Excel or Google Sheets; lines are matched by their key, or by the item name and unit.

Quote for the client

Budget ▾ → Quote for the client… turns the takeoff into a one-page quote with your logo:

  • Your company: name, phone, email, address, licence number, logo and colour, typed once and used on every quote.
  • Client: who the quote is for and the job address.
  • Prices: your markup on materials, tax, the deposit and how many days the quote is good for.
  • Labour: your hourly rate and crew size. Arc estimates the hours for each section from the quantities; type your own hours over any of them.
  • Extra lines for anything not drawn (permits, disposal, travel), and the scope of work and terms in your own words.

The preview updates as you type. Show the work as one total, section by section, or line by line. Save PDF numbers the quote (Q-2026-001, …) and marks it as sent; change its status to accepted, deposit paid or paid as the job goes on.

The first time the quote is saved as a PDF (or marked accepted), Arc remembers that version of the plan as the agreed starting point for change orders.

Pre-existing work (renovations)

For a renovation or addition, draw what's already there, then tell the budget what isn't new. Select the walls, doors, windows, floors, furniture or anything else that's already built and click Mark as pre-existing… in the side panel (or Modify ▾ → Mark as pre-existing). Then choose what's happening to it:

  • Keep as it is: it stays on the plan but isn't costed. Tick any work on it you do want costed: repainting, a feature wall (accent paint, wallpaper or panelling), patching drywall, refinishing a floor, painting a ceiling, new baseboard, new door hardware or trim, refinishing cabinets, and so on.
  • Being replaced: the new one is costed as usual, plus taking out the old one.
  • Being removed: only taking it out is costed. It leaves the finished plan, the 3D model and the sheets (a door or window taken out of a kept wall is filled in).

The whole house at once: Modify ▾ → Mark the whole house as pre-existing… (or Whole house… beside Existing in Layers). Choose every floor or just this one and tick what's already built (walls, doors and windows, floors, roofs, stairs, cabinets and fixtures, ducts, pipes and wires; furniture and the yard if you like), then the same keep, replace or remove question. Anything you draw afterwards is new and costed.

When you mark a wall, its doors and windows can be marked the same way. The estimate gets a Demolition & removal section (with a haul-away line) and a Work on existing section; Arc's quote adds labour hours for both. Footings, slab and floor framing under pre-existing rooms and walls are left out too.

At the bottom of the Layers list are two reference layers, hidden and locked to start; the plan, model and sheets work as normal whatever they're set to:

  • Existing holds everything marked pre-existing. Show it (the eye) to see those things highlighted: kept walls hatched, everything else outlined in brown. While it's locked, pre-existing things can't be selected or moved by accident; unlock it to change one (a new window in a kept wall, say, or the work ticked on it), and use Status in the side panel to set it back to New (costed).
  • Removal holds what's being taken out. Show it to see red dashed outlines where those walls and things were, for reference. List… lists them all, with a way to put any of them back.

Measuring an existing home

These in Review ▾ help when the plan is a real house you've scanned and measured:

  • Scan vs tape measurements…: walls brought in from a LiDAR room scan remember the length the scan gave them. Once you've typed in what the tape says (the wall's Length in its panel), this lists every scanned wall with the scan's length, its length now and the gap, biggest first. Gaps over 3" are flagged to measure again. A scanner draws each inside wall as one line, so a few inches either way is normal in small rooms; Show finds the wall.
  • To confirm on site…: a checklist kept in the plan for the guesses you made (which way a door swings, a window's type, a closet's depth). Select a door, window or wall first and the item is tied to it, so Show finds it on the next visit. Tick each off when it's checked.
    Renovating an older house? Type the year the house was built at the top of the list (or in Project settings, or when you mark the whole house pre-existing; an Edmonton lot brings it from the city's record). Arc adds what to test for in a house of that age before walls, ceilings and floors are opened: asbestos (built before 1990), lead paint (before 1960, possible to 1990), knob-and-tube wiring (to the 1940s), aluminum wiring (mid-1960s to late 1970s), foam insulation, UFFI (1980 or earlier), Poly-B pipe (mid-1970s to 1998), Kitec pipe (about 1995 to 2007) and a radon test (every house). Each says where to look, why it matters and what to do, with a link to the Health Canada or Alberta page it comes from. They are reminders to test, not findings: only a lab or a licensed trade can say what is really there. When the plan takes out part of the house, Design check adds one note until asbestos and lead paint are ticked off.

For tear-out, the estimate sizes the haul-away: one 20-yard dumpster per 18 cubic yards or so of debris (drywall, flooring, roofing and so on, by what's marked Being replaced or Being removed), and a Disposal line for the tipping fees by the yard.

Room scan plus tape measurements

A room scan gets the shape right and the sizes nearly right: a scanner draws each inside wall as one line, so rooms come out a few inches off. Type what your tape measure says, and Arc moves the walls to suit:

  • Along a wall: select the wall; its panel has a Tape box for each side, named by the room that side faces (Tape (Kitchen), Tape (Hall)). Type what you measured along that face, inside corner to inside corner.
  • Wall to wall: select two facing walls (Shift+click the second) and open Review ▾ → Tape measurements & fit the walls…, then type the distance between them.
  • Fit the walls to the tape (in the wall panel, that window, or Scan vs tape measurements) works out where each wall has to be. Each wall keeps its direction and only slides sideways, corners stay joined, and walls nobody measured stay where the scan put them. Walls marked Can't be moved stay put.
  • The preview shows the plan now (grey, dashed) and after the fit (dark), with a tag on each wall saying how far it moves, and each measurement: the tape, the plan now and after the fit. A measurement that can't be met without upsetting the others is flagged: check it with the tape (or which side of the wall it was taken on).
  • Apply moves the walls; doors and windows keep their distance from the nearer corner and rooms follow their corners. Undo puts it all back.

Aging-in-place check

Review ▾ → Aging-in-place check (bathrooms)… looks at each bathroom (rooms named bath, ensuite or powder, or with a toilet, shower or tub in them) for what lets someone use it safely for life:

  • Grab-bar backing in the walls: wood blocking by the toilet, shower and tub while the walls are open, so grab bars can go up any time. Add blocking marks the room and adds a line to the estimate.
  • Curbless shower: no curb to step over. Make it curbless sets the shower to Walk-in. A tub alone gets advice (a curbless shower in a 60" × 30" tub space is the usual change).
  • Lever door handles open with an elbow or a closed hand. Doors now have a Handle setting (Knob or Lever, shown in 3D; Not shown, as before, until you choose). Use levers sets the bathroom's doors.
  • Night light: a low light by the door for the way in at night. Add one by the door puts a Night light block (Electrical) on the wall beside the latch side, 16" up.
  • Door wide enough for a walker: 32" clear (a 34" or 36" door, or a pocket door). This one is a note only: widening a door is a design decision.

Fix buttons change the plan; Undo puts it back. It's a guide, not a code check: an occupational therapist or a certified aging-in-place specialist can look at the person and the house.

Change orders

When the client asks for something different after the quote, change the plan, then open Budget ▾ → Change orders…. Arc compares the plan with the agreed version (or with the last change order) and lists every line that changed: what it was, what it is now, and the cost of the difference with your markup, plus the change in labour hours at your rate.

  • Give it a title and a reason, the days added, and any extra lines that aren't drawn (a permit revision, haul-away; type a minus amount for a credit).
  • Make change order numbers it (No. 1, No. 2, …). Its page shows the changes, this change's total, the original contract, earlier approved changes and the new contract total, with signature lines for the client and you.
  • PDF… saves it to send and marks it sent. Set it to Approved when it's signed: the contract total at the top goes up, and the next change order starts from there. A declined one is left out.

Plans quoted before this version have no starting point yet: if the plan hasn't changed since the quote, click The plan as it is now is what was agreed.

Selections & allowances

Budget ▾ → Selections & allowances… lists what was chosen for each room (its paint colours from the room's palette, flooring, trim, furniture, lighting, fixtures and built-ins, with prices from the shopping list) and compares each category with its allowance. Type the allowance as a total (Lighting $2,000) or a rate per unit (Flooring $8 per sq ft); the summary shows what's over and under. The PDF ends with a client approval page to sign. Add the overage to a change order puts a line for each category over its allowance into the next change order. With Pro tools off (ArcRes) it is your own sign-off: the window asks for Your name, the page is a homeowner approval, and there is no change order.

Phase schedule

Budget ▾ → Phase schedule… (in ArcRes, Budget ▾ → Project timeline…) puts the job on a working-day calendar: permits and site prep, foundation, framing, roofing, windows, doors and siding, rough-ins, insulation, drywall and paint, interior finishes, outdoor work and the final inspection, each starting when what it depends on is done. Red diamonds mark the inspections, with a couple of days to book each one before the next phase starts. Pick the start date and whether you work Saturdays.

  • In Arc, each phase's length comes from the quote's labour hours for its trades divided by the crew on it (8-hour days); phases with nothing priced get a typical length for a house this size, and phases with nothing in the plan (no new walls, say) are left out.
  • In ArcRes, the lengths are typical for a home of this size: just phases and dates.

Type over any length (clear it to go back), untick a phase you're not doing, and Save PDF… for a chart and a table of dates. Weather, permits and material lead times can move the dates.

Renovation diary (Budget ▾): as the job goes, the day each phase really started and finished beside the planned days, notes, and up to four photos a phase. It's saved with the plan and Save as PDF prints it for your records, the insurance file or the next owner.

House phases: building or renovating in stages

Most renovations, and many new houses, are done in stages: the kitchen this year, the basement next, an addition in a few years. Budget ▾ → House phases: stage by stage… (Arc and ArcRes; Ctrl+K → phases) keeps the stages in order:

  • The phases: + Add a phase, give each a name (Phase 1: basement, Future addition) and a target date, and put them in order with ↑ ↓. Existing is always first: everything marked pre-existing (see Pre-existing work).
  • Put work in a phase: select walls, doors, windows, rooms, blocks or anything else that gets built, then pick its Phase in the side panel, or right-click → Put in phase…. Work with no phase counts as the first phase. A door or window goes with its wall when the wall is new. For something pre-existing, its phase is when the work on it happens: when it's taken out, replaced or repainted.
  • Finish later: a wall's or room's Finishes in (drywall, paint, tile, flooring, trim) can be a later phase: frame the basement now and finish it next year. The estimate moves those costs to the later phase.
  • See the house after a phase: Show the house picks As it is now or After any phase; the plan and the 3D view show what's built by then, with what that phase takes out gone. Later phases are hidden, or drawn faint so you can see what's coming. A note at the bottom says what's showing; click it to show everything again. The takeoff, the budget and the sheets always count the whole plan.
  • What each phase costs: each phase's estimate (the takeoff's materials with contingency and tax) and build budget (with labour, the builder's fee, tax and contingency), against a budget you type, with what's left over. The soft costs (permits, design, insurance) are counted once for the whole job. The takeoff and the build budget show the same split.
  • Drawings for one phase: Drawings on a phase's line (or Make drawings for each phase) adds sheets numbered P1-, P2-…: for each floor that phase works on, the floor before it, a demolition plan of what it takes out, and the plan with its new work (earlier phases drawn as existing, later ones left off). That's a permit set for phase 1 alone. Making them again replaces them.
  • What to test for first: when a phase takes out, replaces, works on or cuts into part of an older house, the window lists what to test for before it starts, from the year the house was built (Project settings): asbestos, lead paint, old wiring, foam insulation, Poly-B or Kitec pipe. Work on the lowest floor reminds you to test for radon again the winter after. Review ▾ → To confirm on site has what to look for and who to call.

A plan with no phases is just as it was. Taking a phase out (✕) gives its work no phase again.

Where the diary photos go. On a computer, Save keeps each diary photo once, as its own file, in a folder beside the plan named after it: House.aarc and a House photos folder. That keeps the plan file small, so it saves quickly. Keep the two together when you move, copy, rename or email the plan (OneDrive and Dropbox sync both). Arc also keeps a copy of each photo for itself, so on your own computer Backup copies and a plan moved on its own still show them. If a photo can't be found, the plan still opens, the diary says Photo not found where it was, and saving keeps its place, so it shows again once the folder is back. Arc never deletes these photo files. In a browser or on an iPad the photos stay inside the plan, and a plan saved on a computer shows its diary photos as not found there. Plans saved this way need Arc 0.90 or newer; an older Arc warns before opening them.

Markups & RFIs

To send questions back to the builder, pick the Markup tool under Notes on the tool bar (key I) and mark up the plan, or a builder's PDF you brought in:

  • Cloud round an area: click two corners (or drag).
  • Arrow to something: click the thing, then where the words go.
  • Note on a spot: one click drops a pin.

Each one asks for words and can carry a photo from the site. Tick It's a question for the builder and it becomes a numbered RFI (RFI 001, 002, …), red while open and green once answered. Click one with the Select arrow to edit it, type the builder's answer or change its status. Review ▾ → Markups & RFIs… saves one PDF with the RFI log (question, where it is, status, answer), the marked-up plan of each level (with the builder's PDF under it) and the photos.

Photo pins: where each site photo was taken. In the Markup tool, choose Photo pin, click where you stood, then the way the camera faced, and add the photo. The pin shows on the plan as a camera with a wedge as wide as the photo. Select it to see the photo, say what it shows, and set the Lens (a phone's main camera is about 65° wide; its 0.5× wide camera about 100°) and the Camera height (60" for a phone held at eye level).

  • Line up with this photo opens the 3D view from the pin (wider than the plan while you work), with a card holding the photo. Slide See-through to lay the photo over the model.
  • Nudge until the corners, windows and doors line up: turn left or right, look up or down, wider or narrower (the lens), camera higher or lower, a step forward or back. Each nudge is kept on the pin, so next time it opens lined up. Done puts the views back.
  • A pin lined up this way is a ready camera for side-by-sides of the real room and the model, and for checking a model against the photos.

8. Hands-on lessons

36 lessons you do in the program, with a panel over the plan that walks you through each step and ticks it off by itself when it sees the work done. Open them from Guide ▾ → Hands-on lessons…, the welcome screen, or Ctrl+K → lessons. They're in groups, as in the program: start with the first few, then pick any you like. The first time you use a feature that has a lesson, a small card offers it: Start the lesson or Skip (it won't ask again).

  • Show me points at the button or setting the step needs (it opens a menu first if it has to).
  • Skip and Back move between steps; click any step in the list to jump to it. The ▾ button folds the panel down to one line; × closes it and keeps your place.
  • In the way? Drag the panel by its title bar (the ⠿ dots) to anywhere in the window, with the mouse or a finger. It stays where you put it until you close Arc; double-click the title bar to put it back. The same goes for the other floating cards: the lesson offer, the video player, the wall face designer, the 3D tour list and the photo line-up card.
  • Each lesson opens its own practice plan, so save yours first if it asks. Many start from the Lesson house sample: every stage is in File ▾ → Samples… → Lesson house (the Empty lot, the Finished simple house and the Elevated one), to compare with your own.
  • At the end, the panel says where to find the lesson again, and Do it again starts it fresh.

Start here: a house

Lesson 1: Getting started with the tools

Draw a wall and a room, add a door and a window, measure, move around the plan, see it in 3D and find any command. About 10 minutes · 13 steps · starts with a new plan

1Welcome

This panel walks you through each step and ticks it off when it sees it done. The tools are on the left, the panel on the right shows the settings for the tool or whatever you select, and the status bar at the bottom always says what to click next.

2Pick the Wall tool

Click Wall on the tool bar, or press W. The letter in each tool's corner is its key.

3Draw a wall by typing its length

Click once on the plan to start the wall. Point to the right, then type 12' and press Enter. You don't need to click in the Size box: just type. Press Esc to stop drawing.

4Undo it

Everything can be undone. Press Ctrl+Z (or the curved arrow on the top bar) until the wall goes away. Ctrl+Y brings it back.

5Draw a room

Pick Room (R). Type 12' x 10' and press Enter, then click where the top-left corner goes. Four walls and the room appear at once.

6Name the room

Pick the Select arrow (V), click inside the room, and type a new name in the panel, like Office. Press Enter or click away to keep it.

7Add a door

Pick Door (D) and hover over a wall: the distance to each end shows. Click to place it. The door swings toward the side your cursor is on; F flips the hinge.

8Add a window

Pick Window (N) and click another wall. Choose the window's type and size in the panel first if you like.

9Measure something

Pick Measure (E) and click two points, like opposite corners of the room. The length shows on the plan; nothing is added. Esc clears it.

10Move around the plan

Scroll the mouse wheel to zoom toward the cursor, and drag with the right button (or hold Space and drag) to slide the plan. On an iPad, pinch and drag with two fingers. Z (on an iPad, Fit on the top bar) fits everything back on screen.

11See it in 3D

Click 3D view on the top bar. Drag to turn the model, right-drag to slide, scroll to zoom. Everything you draw shows up there straight away.

12Find any command

Press Ctrl+K (or the magnifier on the top bar) and type a word, like stairs or pdf. Pick a result with the arrow keys and Enter, or press Esc to close it.

13Help is one click away

Most headings in the side panel have a small ? on the right: it opens the guide at that topic. The Guide ▾ menu on the top bar lists every video lesson (each plays in a small window beside your drawing), the hands-on lessons, this guide and the quick reference card. That's the tools: next, build a whole house.

Lesson 2: Build a simple house

On the Lesson house lot: outside walls, inside walls, rooms, doors, windows evenly spaced, floors, smoke alarms, a roof and a porch. About 25 minutes · 14 steps · starts from the Lesson house (empty lot)

1The lot

This is your lot: the red dashed line is the property line, with the street at the bottom. The blue dashed outline is where the house goes: 40' × 28' inside. You'll build a living room and kitchen on the left, two bedrooms and a bath on the right.

2The outside walls

Pick Room (R). In the panel, set Preset to 2x6 Exterior and Size measured to Inside (room size). Type 40' x 28', press Enter, and click the top-left corner of the blue outline.

3The long inside wall

Pick Wall (W) and set Preset to 2x4 Interior. Draw a wall from the front to the back of the house 24' from the left side: click on the front wall, click on the back wall, then Esc. Hover first: the distances show as you move. The room splits in two by itself.

4The cross walls

Still with the Wall tool, split the right side into three: a wall 11' up from the front, and another at 17' (that's the bath between them). Then on the left, a wall 15' up from the front, from the outside wall to the long wall: living room in front, kitchen behind. Press Esc after each.

5Name the rooms

Pick the Select arrow, click each room and name it in the panel: Living room, Kitchen & dining, Bedroom 2, Bathroom, Primary bedroom. Names matter: the design check, the furniture suggestions and the takeoff all read them.

6Doors

Pick Door (D). Put the front door on the front wall of the living room, and a door from the living room or kitchen into each bedroom and the bath (make the bath door 30" wide in the panel). The door swings toward your cursor; F flips the hinge.

7A wide opening

Between the living room and the kitchen, use an opening with no door: in the Door tool's panel set Type to Cased opening (no door) and Width to 8', then click the wall between them.

8Windows, evenly spaced

Pick Window (N). In the panel's Several at once section set How many to 2 and Spacing to Equal sections, then click the living room's left wall: two windows, evenly spaced. Set How many back to 1 and add one over the kitchen sink. In the bedrooms, set Type to Casement first: it opens all the way, so it's big enough to climb out of in an emergency (the design check tells you if a bedroom window is too small).

9Floors

Open Finishes on the tool bar and pick Floors (A). Choose Hardwood and click the living room and kitchen, Carpet for the bedrooms, and Tile for the bathroom. Each click sets that room's floor and baseboard.

10Read the design check

Click an empty spot on the plan: the panel shows the Design check. It asks for smoke alarms in each bedroom and outside them. Pick Blocks, choose Electrical → Smoke / CO detector, and click inside each bedroom and the living room. The warnings clear as you go.

11The roof

Open Roof on the tool bar and pick Roof (F) and click inside the house: the roof follows the outside walls. Select it later to change the style (gable, hip, shed…), the pitch and the colours.

12A front porch

Open Stairs & decks on the tool bar and pick Porch (G). Click two points along the front of the house, either side of the front door, then click how deep the porch goes (about 6').

13Look at your house

Click 3D view and turn it round. Try Go inside… on the 3D toolbar to stand in a room.

14Save it

Press Ctrl+S (or Save on the top bar) and give your house a name. Lesson 3 starts from a finished house, so you can use yours or the sample.

Compare with the finished version: File ▾ → Samples… → Lesson house → Finished.

Lesson 3: Elevate it: materials, layouts and styles

Take the simple house further: a style pack, a colour palette with an accent wall, materials, a kitchen, furniture, a bath, the outside and the garden. About 25 minutes · 12 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Start from a finished house

This is the house from Lesson 2. You'll restyle it inside and out, furnish it and plant the garden. Open the 3D view beside the plan to watch each change as you make it.

2A style pack

Open Design ▾ → Style packs…, pick a look (say Craftsman or Modern farmhouse) and click Apply style. Siding, roof, floors, cabinets and inside paint all change at once; Undo takes it back.

3A colour palette

Open Finishes on the tool bar and pick Decorate (Y), choose a palette in the panel, tick Accent wall if you like, and click the primary bedroom. Each colour has a job: walls, accent, trim, ceiling and fabrics.

4A material on one wall

Open Finishes on the tool bar and pick Paint (K). Choose a category like Wallpaper or Stone, pick one, and click a wall in the living room: only the side you click changes.

5A new floor

Open Finishes on the tool bar and pick Floors, choose a different floor (try Tile with a pattern, or a darker wood with wider planks) and click a room to change it.

6The kitchen

Select the kitchen, then Design ▾ → Kitchen layout…. Pick a layout: cabinets, counters, sink and appliances go in, in your style.

7Design this room for me

Select the living room, then Design ▾ → Design this room for me…. Three arrangements are scored for comfort; click Use this arrangement on the one you like.

8Furnish a bedroom

Do the same for a bedroom: select it and choose Design this room for me… again (the room type follows its name).

9The bathroom

Pick Blocks (B), choose Bathroom, and place a tub, a vanity and a toilet. Bring each near a wall: it turns to sit against it.

10The outside

Select an outside wall and change its Exterior finish or colour in the panel (or keep the style pack's and change the roof colour instead: select the roof).

11The garden

Pick Blocks → Landscaping and add a few shrubs along the front and a tree. Then open Yard on the tool bar, pick Outdoor (O), choose Patio, and click the corners of a patio outside the back door.

12Share it

Open Design ▾ → Mood board… for a one-page board of the palette, materials and furniture, and Budget ▾ → Shopping list & budget… to see what it all costs. In the 3D view, Render makes a photo-like picture. Your house is elevated!

Compare with the finished version: File ▾ → Samples… → Lesson house → Elevated.

Lesson 4: Do it with me: the Lakeside House

Build a high-end modern house on a lake from the empty lot: stone and wood, walls of glass with a glass corner, an upper floor that overhangs the terrace, flat roofs, a style in one click, a kitchen and furniture designed for you, a terrace, a pool and outdoor living. About 30 minutes · 19 steps · starts from the Lakeside House (empty lot)

1The lot

The lot slopes down to the lake on the south. The blue dashed outlines are where the ground floor and the garage go; the teal one is the upper floor, which reaches 8' past the ground floor to shade the terrace. Open the 3D view to see the lake.

2The ground floor

Pick Room (R). Type 44' x 30' and press Enter, then click the top-left corner of the big blue outline. Four walls and the room appear at once.

3The garage

Still with Room, type 24' x 24' and click the top-left corner of the small blue outline, on the east side. Its wall joins the house.

4Stone outside

Pick the Select arrow and drag a box round the ground floor to pick its walls. In the panel set Exterior finish to Stacked shale / ledgestone and the colour to Charcoal shale.

5Walls of glass

Pick Window (N) and set Type to Curtain wall (floor-to-ceiling glass), Width about 17'. Click the lake-side wall near its west end, then again near its east end. Leave a gap in the middle for a door.

6A glass corner

Select the glass at the west end. In the panel set Glass corner to the corner it touches: the post disappears and the glass turns the corner onto the west wall.

7A big sliding door

Pick Door (D), set Type to Sliding patio door and Width to 11', and click the gap between the glass. It opens the great room to the terrace.

8Front door and garage door

Still with Door: a Hinged door on the north side for the front door, then Type → Garage door on the north wall of the garage.

9The upper floor

Click + Add level on the level bar. Pick Room, type 36' x 38', and click the top-left corner of the teal outline. It hangs 8' out over the terrace, and the floor below it is made for you.

10Warm wood upstairs

Select the upstairs outside walls and set Exterior finish to Fiber-cement panels, colour Wood look (or Board & batten in natural cedar).

11Glass upstairs

Pick Window again (still Curtain wall) and put glass on the lake side upstairs: the bedrooms get the view. Try a glass corner here too.

12Flat roofs

Open Roof on the tool bar and pick Roof (F), set Style to Flat and Overhang to 3'. Click inside the upper floor. Then switch the level bar to the Main floor and click inside the garage: the roof covers only the part that sticks out.

13Name the great room

Pick the Select arrow, click inside the ground floor and name it Living room in the panel. The furniture suggestions read the name.

14A style in one click

Open Design ▾ → Style packs…, pick Japandi and click Apply style. Floors, cabinets and the colours on every wall and ceiling change at once. (Untick Outside walls and Roofs to keep your stone, wood and flat roofs.)

15The kitchen

Select the living room, then Design ▾ → Kitchen layout…. Pick a layout with an island: cabinets, counters, sink, appliances and the island go in, in your style.

16Design this room for me

With the living room still selected, Design ▾ → Design this room for me…. Three arrangements are scored for comfort; click Use this arrangement on the one you like.

17A terrace and a pool

Open Yard on the tool bar and pick Outdoor (O). Set Draw to Patio and the stone to Bluestone, and click the corners of a terrace along the lake side. Then set Draw to Water (stream, pond, pool) and draw a long pool on the terrace.

18Outdoor living

Pick Blocks (B), open Outdoor living, and add a few chaise lounges along the pool, a fire pit with an outdoor sofa, and a hot tub.

19See it from the lake

In the 3D view, orbit round to the lake side and turn on Sun (3D bar) for evening light. That's a high-end house in a few dozen clicks! Compare with the finished one: File ▾ → Samples… → Lakeside House → Finished.

Compare with the finished house: File ▾ → Samples… → Lakeside House → Finished.

3D printing

Lesson 5: 3D printing: an object, then a house

Draw a small part to exact sizes in millimetres and cut a hole in it, read the print checks, then print a house as floors and a roof that peg together, fixing the warnings as you go. About 12 minutes · 11 steps · starts from the “3D print: an object in millimetres” template

1A plan for printing

This plan is in millimetres at full size. The dashed teal outline is your printer's bed: anything inside it prints in one piece. Two shapes are already there: a base plate and a round post.

2Pick the 3D shape tool

Open Draw ▾ and pick 3D shape. Its panel sets the shape (rectangle, circle or outline) and how tall it is.

3Draw a block to size

Set Draw to Rectangle and Height to 8 mm. Click one corner on the plan, then type the size, width by depth, like 40 x 25, and press Enter (or click the opposite corner).

4Cut a hole

Your block is picked, so the panel shows its settings. Press Esc twice, open Draw ▾, pick 3D shape again and set Draw to Circle. Draw a small circle inside the base plate: a shape drawn inside another cuts a hole straight through it.

5Read the print hints

Pick the Select arrow and click a shape. The panel's 3D printing box gives its printed size and warns if it's too thin for the nozzle, too big for the bed, or would need supports.

6Check it for printing

Open Export ▾ → 3D print (STL files, plates, checks)…. Arc checks every part for your printer and lays them out on a plate. Green ticks mean it should print well.

7Now a house

Close the window. Next you'll print a house: open File ▾ → New from a template… and pick 3D print: a house model (two floors and a roof).

8Floors that peg together

Open Export ▾ → 3D print… again. With Pegs and sockets ticked, each floor gets small posts in its corners with a peg on top, and the floor above (and the roof's flat base) gets matching sockets.

9Make it too big, then fix it

Set Scale to 1:20. A red warning says the parts don't fit the bed, with a button for the scale that does: click it.

10Thin walls

Set Scale to 1:200 and untick Thicken walls too thin for the nozzle: the walls would print thinner than the nozzle can lay. Click Thicken thin walls for printing on the warning to fix it.

11Save the files

Set the scale back to 1:48 and click Save files…: one file per plate, one per part, and a print guide PDF with the slicer settings and how the parts go together. Open the plate files in Bambu Studio and print. That's it!

Watch it first: Guide ▾ → 13. 3D print a small object and 14. Print your house.

Lesson 6: Do it with me: rock walls and cliffs

Draw a rock wall on a small landscape with the Rock tool, change its rock, colour and the size of its stones, colour some stones lighter, turn it into a mountain cliff, and save a test tile to print. About 15 minutes · 15 steps · starts from the “3D print: a small landscape (plates, inserts and parts)” template

1Rock, as it really is

The Rock tool draws walls, ledges and cliffs in real rock: every stone stands out in 3D and in the print files, and it always keeps to the 45° rule, so it prints standing up with no supports. This board is 400 × 300 mm, at full size, so the rock is sized in millimetres.

2Pick the Rock tool

Open Yard on the tool bar and pick Rock. The panel shows a picture of each rock: laid stone on top (for walls and house finishes), natural rock below (for ledges and cliffs).

3Pick a rock

Click the Rough shale picture: thin slabs with lumpy faces and chipped corners.

4Draw the wall

Click near the back-left corner of the board, then near the back-right corner, and press Enter. The wall stands where you clicked, from the ground up to its Top of wall.

5See it in 3D

Click 3D view on the top bar and zoom in on the wall. Each slab has its own shade, and the joints are darker.

6Select it

Pick the Select arrow and click the wall on the plan. Its panel has the same pictures, and everything below them.

7Its colour

Click one of the colour squares under Colour: Rustic brown, say. Or click the box beside them for any colour at all. Every stone takes it, each a little lighter or darker.

8Some stones lighter

Drag How many to about 30%. A third of the stones turn the colour under Some stones in. In print they become a second piece, for a second filament.

9The size of the stones

Drag Stone size to bigger or smaller. Depth, Roughness and Wobble change how far the stones stand out, how lumpy they are and how much their lines wave. Reset the shape puts them back.

10Your own stone

Click Change this stone…: a window opens beside the panel, with a picture of the stones. Drag Thinnest row and Thickest row up, and let go: the wall's rows get taller. Then Save as My stone… and give it a name: it shows under My stones, and in My library for your other plans. Close the window when you're done.

11Carved blocks

Click the Carved blocks picture: square-cut blocks in rows, with rounded edges, chisel marks and deep joints, like a castle or a storybook wall. Two more sliders come up: Block length and Joint depth.

12A mountain cliff

Click the Mountain cliff picture: the same wall becomes a cliff of buttresses, bays and long cracks. Try Granite boulders and Limestone cliff too.

13More stones

Back to laid stone: try a few of the newer stones: Storybook boulders (fat rounded stones of mixed sizes, the village look), Castle wall, Railway stone, River rock or Split-face wall block. Each has its own sliders in Change this stone…: a split-face wall's Bond, say, or how many storybook stones are joined into big ones.

14A test tile

Click Save a test tile… near the bottom of the panel: a piece 50 mm long and 30 mm tall of this rock, as a Bambu Studio project, to try on the printer before the whole wall. In two colours it takes much longer, because the printer changes filament on every layer.

15Printing it

Open 3D Print on the top bar: the wall is a part of its own, standing up, with no supports. On a house, the Rock tool makes rock garden walls and retaining walls, and walls finished in Stacked shale or Stone veneer show real stones in 3D.

Lesson 7: Do it with me: a stone wall with arches

Cover the front of a small landscape with a carved stone facing, make it a storybook wall with columns and arches with lanterns, move an arch, add a little arched window, and see how it prints. About 10 minutes · 10 steps · starts from the “3D print: a small landscape (plates, inserts and parts)” template

1A stone facing

A stone facing is a carved stone face that covers the side of a model, like the walls of a storybook village. It is a piece of its own: it prints separately and lifts off. This board is 400 × 300 mm at full size, so everything is in millimetres.

2Pick the Rock tool

Open Yard on the tool bar and pick Rock.

3Draw a stone facing

In the panel, set Draw to A stone facing. (It's also Draw ▾ → Stone facing (on a model).)

4Along the front edge

Click right on the board's front edge (the bottom edge on the plan) near its left corner, then near its right corner, and press Enter. Because both clicks are on the edge, the facing covers the board's side, its face looking out.

5See it in 3D

Click 3D view on the top bar and zoom in on the front of the board: carved blocks in level courses, with longer, flatter stones along the foot.

6Select it

Pick the Select arrow and click the facing on the plan (the stone strip along the board's front edge).

7A storybook wall

Click Storybook wall in its panel: a stone column at each end and arches between, each in a ring of wedge stones with a keystone, a lantern hanging inside, a snow cap rolling over the top and drifts at the foot.

8Move an arch

Above the list, the face is laid out flat. Drag one of the numbered markers left or right: that arch moves along the wall. Or type where it goes in its row.

9A little window

Click Add arched window: a small arched lantern window goes in the widest stretch of plain wall.

10Printing it

Open 3D Print on the top bar: the facing is a part of its own. A lantern needs an arch at least 12 mm wide, because its glazing bars are 0.64 mm: smaller, and Arc leaves the lantern out and says why.

Lesson 8: 3D Print workspace: plates and the colour change

Open the 3D Print workspace, see how a model base is cut into plates with puzzle tabs, change the plate sizes, and find the layer where the filament changes colour. About 8 minutes · 7 steps · starts from the “3D print: a small landscape (plates, inserts and parts)” template

1A small landscape to practise on

This practice board is 400 × 300 mm, in millimetres at full size. It has a grey cobbled street, a raised cobbled square with a turntable hole, two trees and a lamp. Nothing here touches your own projects.

2Open the 3D Print workspace

Click 3D Print in the top bar. All the 3D printing tools are in its seven sections, beside the plan.

3Select the board

Open 2. Model ground and plates, then click the board's edge on the plan. The board is the model base: the ground on it is cut into plates that fit the printer's bed.

4Change the plate sizes

Type 150 in Column widths and press Enter. The plate line on the plan moves: L1F is 150 mm wide now and L2F takes the rest (250 mm), with puzzle tabs between them. (The last column always takes what's left.)

5Put it back

Press Ctrl+Z to undo. Changing plate sizes changes every plate in that row or column, so only do it if you'll print all of them again.

6The colour change

Read the colour-change line in section 2: below it the plates print in the base colour (grey), above it in the ground cover colour (white). It tells you the layer to type into Bambu Studio. The street is below it, so its cobbles print grey; the raised square is above it, so its cobbles would print white (the next lesson fixes that with an insert).

7A map of the plates

The plate map shows every plate by name. Save the plate map as a picture keeps one to print out and lay the plates by. Click ← Back to the plan when you're done. That's it!

Lesson 9: 3D Print workspace: surfaces and printed inserts

Soften the cobblestones on a street, make it your own paving, turn a raised square into a printed insert that drops into a pocket, change how much snow lies on it, and split it to fit the bed. About 10 minutes · 10 steps · starts from the “3D print: a small landscape (plates, inserts and parts)” template

1Surfaces and inserts

Cobblestones, bricks and planks are pressed into the printed plates. But anything grey that sits above the colour change (like this raised square) prints white, so it becomes a printed insert: its own two-colour piece that drops into a pocket.

2Open the 3D Print workspace

Click 3D Print in the top bar.

3Change the street's cobbles

Open 3. Surfaces and click the street on the plan. Change Soft edges (how rounded each stone's edge is) or Groove depth, and press Enter. The 3D view shows the new stones.

4Pick a paving

Under the street's Surface pattern, open Pictures of every pattern and click Running bond pavers (it's in the list too, under More paving). The street becomes brick pavers, each row half a brick along from the last.

5Your own paving

Click Change this paving…: a window opens beside the panel, with a picture. Drag Each row starts along to 0% (stack bond) and let go: the street changes. Click Save as My paving… and give it a name: it's in My paving now, in this plan and in My library for your other plans. Close the window.

6Select the square

Open 4. Inserts and click the raised Town square on the plan. The section says Insert in: Town square.

7Make it a printed insert

Leave Stones or tiles on Soft cobblestones and click Make it a printed insert. The square drops 3 mm to make the pocket, and the insert's stones are made, with a hole round the turntable.

8Change the snow

Drag the Snow on the stones slider, or type a number from 0 to 100 in its box (30, say) and press Enter. The insert is made again: What was made shows how many stones have snow.

9Split it to fit

Set Split into to Four quarters. Each piece prints on its own; a big insert has to be split so every piece fits the bed (Automatic does that by itself).

10Have a look

Open the 3D view to see the stones and their caps of snow. The plates don't change when you change the snow: only the insert's pieces need printing again. To print them, select them and use Save the selected parts… in section 6; each comes as a two-colour .3mf. That's it!

Lesson 10: 3D Print workspace: parts and part numbers

See how Arc breaks a model into printable parts, print two shapes as one part, give a layer its own part-number letters, read the parts list, and re-make only what changed. About 8 minutes · 8 steps · starts from the “3D print: a small landscape (plates, inserts and parts)” template

1How a model becomes parts

The ground is cut into plates. Every other 3D shape is a part, except that shapes that touch print as one part (each tree's trunk and crown), and shapes with the same Print together as text print as one part even when they don't touch.

2Open the 3D Print workspace

Click 3D Print in the top bar.

3Make the parts list

Open 5. Parts and numbers and click Make the parts list. Each part has a number from its layer's letters (T for Trees, L for Lamps), its size, colours and grams. The lamp is two parts: its post and its globe don't touch.

4Select the lamp's two shapes

Click the lamp post on the plan, then hold Shift and click its globe.

5Print them as one part

Type lamp in Print together as and press Enter. Now they print as one part, glued at the slicer or joined by a peg.

6Part number letters

Type LP in Part number letters and press Enter. Every part on the Lamps layer gets them. You never type the numbers: Arc gives them in order when it makes the list or saves the files.

7Make the list again

Click Make the parts list again: the lamp is one part now, LP-01. New parts go on the list by themselves. Numbers are given in order, so a new part can move the numbers after it: always print from the newest files and list together.

8Re-make only what changed

In 6. Check and export, Save just these plates… makes only the plates you tick, and Save the selected parts… only the parts you've selected. 7. What to watch out for lists anything risky about your changes: seams, puzzle tabs, pockets, the colour change and the bed size. That's it!

More about the workspace: the 3D Print workspace and printed inserts.

Lesson 11: 3D Print workspace: the mesh doctor

Find out what "watertight" means: see the open edges of a broken cup in red, patch its hole, check it again, and check a shape you drew. About 6 minutes · 7 steps · starts from the “3D print: the mesh doctor (a broken cup to fix)” template

1Watertight

A part prints well when its surface is closed all the way round, like a balloon: every edge is shared by two faces. An edge with a face on one side only is an open edge, the rim of a hole, and there a slicer can't tell inside from outside, so it may leave out whole layers. This cup was imported from a file with a hole in its side.

2Open the 3D Print workspace

Click 3D Print in the top bar.

3Select the cup

Click the Broken cup on the plan (the round one).

4Ask the doctor

Open 8. Mesh doctor and collisions and click Check for open edges. The 3D view opens: the red lines are the open edges, all round the hole in the cup's side.

5Patch the hole

Click Patch the holes. Arc follows the red edges round into a loop and fills it with new faces, then checks again: no red lines, and the cup says watertight.

6Check the box you drew

Click the Box I drew on the plan and click Check for open edges again. Shapes drawn in Arc are closed when they print, so it's watertight too.

7Every part, every time

The print check does this for every part when it makes the files: it closes small gaps by itself and says which parts it couldn't. Imported STL and 3MF files from the internet often have holes, so ask the doctor before printing one. That's it!

Find your way round, and the budget

Lesson 12: Find your way round the menus

What each menu on the top bar is for, in the order you'll use them, and how to find anything by typing a word. About 5 minutes · 11 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1The top bar

Each menu on the top bar has one job, split into short groups with headings, so you can see at a glance where things are. Open each one as the lesson asks.

2File

Click File ▾: your plans (new, open, save, templates), bringing in a drawing (a DXF, a PDF plan, a survey) and settings.

3Export

Click Export ▾: showing it to people (a render, a share page, a PDF for your contractor), drawings, and 3D files for other programs.

43D files for Blender

In Export ▾, glTF .glb is made for Blender: each floor is a group, split by trade (siding, roofing, cabinets…), and materials are named by their finish (Brick_red, Siding_lap_gray), the same every time, so the materials you set up in Blender stay put when you export again. Tick With framing for the studs, joists and rafters too. On the desktop app, the Render window's Render in Blender makes the final picture in Blender, with the same camera and light.

5Design

Click Design ▾: only design: style (style packs, mood board), rooms designed for you (furniture, kitchen, closet) and suggested layouts.

6Review

Click Review ▾: checking the design (warnings, your own specs, the code summary) and what the permit office and builder need.

7Budget

Click Budget ▾: everything about money and time: what it costs, each trade, quotes for the client and the schedule.

8View, Draw and Modify

View ▾ chooses what the 3D view shows (framing, pipes & wires, roof off…). Draw ▾ and Modify ▾ hold the drafting tools, also in groups. Open Draw ▾.

9Show

Click Show ▾: what the plan shows. Clean floor plan keeps just the layout and furniture for reading.

10Can't find it? Type it

Press Ctrl+K (or the magnifier) and type a word, like quote or stairs: every command, setting and help topic is there.

11Help on every heading

Most headings in the side panel have a small ? that opens the guide at that topic, and Guide ▾ lists the videos and these lessons.

Lesson 13: The budget: costs, prices and what each wall costs

Open the parts list, type your own prices, use a typical one or a sub's price, look up the price list, add costs that aren't drawn, see one wall's budget, set a shopping budget, and plan the job. About 10 minutes · 11 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Money and time

Everything about cost lives in Budget ▾. It's counted from the plan, so it changes as you draw.

2The cost estimate

Open Budget ▾ → Takeoff & cost estimate…: every part counted from the plan. Your prices go in the Your price column; Arc's typical price shows greyed beside them.

3Your own price

Type your price over one line's price (the drywall sheets, say). The totals change straight away, and the price is kept for this plan.

4Typical prices

Arc's Typical price is greyed beside yours, to check a quote against: it isn't counted. Click use on another line to copy it in as yours.

5A sub's price

When a sub gives one price for their part (the stair guy, the roofer), click + Sub price on that section's heading and type it: it becomes the section's total, and the parts stay listed with their quantities. If you're the sub, leave it blank and price the parts.

6A cost that isn't drawn

Click + Add beside a section, or + Add an item not in the drawing…: a permit fee, a dumpster, hours of labour. Then close the estimate.

7What one wall costs

Click a wall: the panel's Budget for this wall lists its studs, plates, drywall, paint and the rest, with prices. (In 3D it glows.)

8A shopping budget

Open Budget ▾ → Shopping list & budget…: paint, flooring, furniture and decor to buy. Type a Budget to see how you're doing against it.

9The price list

Open Budget ▾ → Price list (typical prices)…: every typical price by section, and the trades by the hour, to look up for a rough estimate or to see if a sub's price is fair. Type in its box to find one, then close it.

10Plan the job

Open Budget ▾ → Phase schedule…: the job in phases on a calendar, with inspections. Change a start date or how long a phase takes.

11Keep it

Save the takeoff as a spreadsheet… (Budget ▾) gives you a file for Excel. In Arc, Quote for the client… turns it into a quote with your logo and markup.

Change what you've drawn

Lesson 14: Make and edit your own block

Choose a sofa's legs and cushions, take a block apart on its own (nothing else can move), change it and save it as your own, then build a block from 3D shapes. About 10 minutes · 9 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Blocks you can make your own

Every piece of furniture or fixture is a block. You can change its choices, take it apart and draw it your way, or build one from 3D shapes, and keep it in My blocks for every plan.

2Place a sofa

Pick Blocks (B), choose Living → Sofa, and click in the living room. Near a wall it turns to sit against it.

3Its choices

Pick the Select arrow and click the sofa. In the panel change Legs (try Skirted) and Seat cushions. The 3D view shows it straight away.

4Take it apart, on its own

With the sofa selected, click Edit the drawing in the panel. It opens on its own: everything else fades and can't be moved, so nothing changes by accident.

5Change a piece

Click one line of the sofa and press Delete, or drag one to move it. Draw a line with L to add one.

6Save it as your block

Click Save as block on the bar over the plan and give it a name. It goes into My blocks; Cancel would have put the old sofa back.

7Place it again

Pick Blocks (B) and set Category to My blocks. Click yours, then click on the plan to place another one.

8A block from 3D shapes

Open Draw ▾ → 3D shape and draw a tabletop and a leg (set each one's Height and Off the floor in the panel). Select both (Shift+click), and click Save as my block….

9Yours for every plan

Your blocks are kept on this computer (or this browser) for every plan. To change one later, place it, select it and use Edit the drawing again.

Lesson 15: Your own 3D models: bring one in, check it, keep it

Bring in a maker's 3D model (a lounge chair), check its size, give its parts your own finishes, and keep it in My models for every plan. About 6 minutes · 6 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Models from makers

Furniture and fixture makers, Blender and SketchUp give you 3D models (GLB, OBJ, FBX, DAE, 3DS, STL or 3MF). Arc brings one in as a block you can move, turn and resize, and keeps it in My models for your other plans.

2Bring one in

Pick Blocks (B) and click Try it with an example model in the panel: a lounge chair as a maker would send it, in metres. Check Model units shows a chair-sized number and click Import.

3Its size

Arc checks the size against the name as it comes in: a 14' sofa was likely drawn in other units, so it asks. A model already in a plan is checked too: its panel says when it looks the wrong size, with Make it … to fix it, or Keep this size.

4Your own finishes

Pick the Select arrow and click the chair. Under Parts and colours, give one part an Arc finish (a wood, a fabric, a metal). Only this copy changes; As imported puts it back.

5Keep it

Click Keep in My models…, pick its category and click Keep. It stays on this computer, with its size and your finishes.

6In every plan

Your models are in Blocks under My models, and in File ▾ → My library…, in this plan and your others. Two copies of a model share one file, so the plan stays small.

Lesson 16: Bring in a maker's product

Import a manufacturer's 3D model as a block (GLB, OBJ, FBX, DAE, STL or 3MF), fit it to size, then read in a product list with sizes, prices and links and place one. About 8 minutes · 8 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Makers' models and lists

Manufacturers and sites like 3D Warehouse or BIMobject offer models to download: choose GLB, OBJ, FBX, DAE, STL or 3MF. From SketchUp, use File ▾ → Export → 3D model as COLLADA (.dae) or OBJ. This lesson uses an example chair, so you don't need one yet.

2Open the Blocks tool

Pick Blocks (B). The import buttons are at the bottom of the panel's Placing section.

3Import the example model

Click Try it with an example model (or Import a 3D model… and pick one you downloaded). It comes in as a block at its real size, standing up the right way.

4Fit it

It's selected: move it into a room (drag it), turn it with Rotate, and change its Width in the panel if the size isn't right (models made in centimetres sometimes need it).

5See it in 3D

Open the 3D view: it's the maker's own model, with their materials.

6A product list

With the Blocks tool (B), click Import a product list (CSV)… and then Use the example list. A supplier's spreadsheet works the same: one row per product with its size, maker, model, price and link.

7Place a product

Set the library's Category to My products, pick one and click on the plan. Its size is the maker's, and its price and link come with it.

8It's in the budget

Open Budget ▾ → Shopping list & budget…: the product is listed at its price, with a link to buy it.

Lesson 17: Edit in 3D: one part at a time, and feature walls

Work on just one part (the rest fades and can't move), lock the 3D view while you click, and draw on a wall in 3D with a kit of real materials: shiplap inside, shutters and trim outside. About 10 minutes · 9 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Working in 3D

Two tools help with fiddly work: Work on this part shows just what you pick, and the wall face designer lets you draw on a wall standing in front of it. Open the 3D view beside the plan for both.

2Open the 3D view

Click 3D view on the top bar.

3Pick one part

With the Select arrow, drag a box round the bathroom (its walls, doors and fixtures), then click ◎ Work on this part at the top of the panel.

4Lock the view

On the 3D toolbar click 🔒 Lock view: the model stays still while you click on it. (If it's not on the bar, it's in More ▾.)

5Back to the whole plan

Click Done on the bar over the plan. Everything comes back; anything you changed stays changed (Cancel would have put it all back).

6Work on a wall in 3D

Click a living room wall. In the panel's Interior finish, click ✎ Work on this face in 3D on the side facing the room. The 3D view turns to face it, and a drawing board of the wall opens with a kit of real materials.

7A feature wall

On the Feature wall tab, click Shiplap and drag across the wall. Boards go on at their real size; try Chair rail or Floating shelf too. Click a piece to change its size, how far it sticks out, its material or colour.

8Dress up the outside

Click Done, then click an outside wall with windows and ✎ Work on this face in 3D on its outside. On the Exterior tab, click + Shutters (every window), then + Window trim.

9Unlock and look round

Click Done in the designer, then 🔒 View locked to orbit again. The trim is in the takeoff and in this wall's budget too.

Lesson 18: Draw on a wall: stone below a sloped line, shapes, half-timbering

Draw a line across an outside wall and fill one side with stone and the other with stucco, fill a circle or a freehand shape, and half-timber a zone. The wall stays one wall, and each zone is counted as what it is. About 8 minutes · 11 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Lines that split a wall

On the wall designer's Draw & fill tab, a line drawn across a wall (level, sloped or curved) splits it into two zones, and any closed shape is a zone of its own. Each zone takes stone, siding or paint from the siding list, laid flat in the wall's own siding.

2Open the 3D view

Click 3D view on the top bar.

3Work on an outside wall

Click a long outside wall with windows. In its panel, click ✎ Work on this face in 3D on its Outside face.

4The Draw & fill tab

Click the Draw & fill tab. Its tools are the plan's drawing tools, plus a freehand Pen and a Fill bucket.

5A sloped line

Pick Line. Click near the bottom of the wall at its left end, then higher up at its right end. The line carries on to the wall's edges (dashed), so it splits the whole wall in two.

6Stone below it

Under the drawing, set Fill with to Stacked shale / ledgestone and click the Charcoal shale swatch. Then click the wall below the line: the stone goes on, round the windows.

7Stucco above

Set Fill with to Stucco, pick a colour, and click the wall above the line.

8A shape of its own

Pick Circle (click its middle, then its edge) or Pen (freehand) (drag a loop back to where you started). Pick a finish and click inside it to fill it.
