9Half-timbering

Click Half-timber: curved, then click a zone: dark timber boards go on over stucco, kept inside that zone. With Select, drag the sloped line: the stone stays below it. Delete takes a line off and its zones join up again.

10Or straight from 3D

Click Done, then click any wall in the 3D view: ✎ Draw on this wall opens it flat and big with the same tools, and Save puts it on the wall. On an outside wall, Whole … side lays out every wall facing that way, so one stone band can run across the whole front.

11In the estimate

In the Budget, each zone is a line of its own by its area (the stone, the stucco), taken off the wall's own siding, and the timber boards are counted by the foot. The elevation sheets hatch and label the zones too.

Lesson 19: Stairs: sizes, single steps and curves

Change a stair's height and width, edit one step (a rounded bottom step), then draw a curved stair and fit it to the code. About 8 minutes · 7 steps · starts from the “Two-story shell” template

1Stairs you can shape

A stair works out its own steps from the floor-to-floor height. You can change the whole stair, one step, or draw a curved one.

2Select the stair

Pick the Select arrow and click the stair. The panel shows its steps and checks them against the code limits.

3Change its size

In the panel change the Width of a flight, or the Floor to floor height. The number of steps updates by itself.

4Pick one step

With the stair selected, click it again on the bottom step. The panel shows just that step.

5A rounded bottom step

Set that step's Shape to Rounded both ends: a bullnose step that sticks out past the stair.

6Draw a curved stair

Open Stairs & decks on the tool bar and pick Stairs (S), set Shape to Curved (follows a curved wall). Click the centre of the curve, then where the middle of the bottom step goes.

7Make it pass

Select the curved stair. If the panel lists a problem, change Each step turns or the Inside radius until it says Meets the code limits. Next to a curved wall, Fit to the curved wall lines it up.

Lesson 20: Big glass: bays, joined windows, sliding walls and skylights

On a small cottage: a bay window with a seat, a picture window joined to casements, a wall of sliding glass, a pivot front door and a skylight with a light shaft, then see them in the schedule and the budget. About 10 minutes · 8 steps · starts from the “Two-bedroom cottage” template

1Glass for a custom home

Door and Window have more types in their Type list: windows that stand out from the wall, windows joined into one unit, shaped windows, sliding and folding glass walls, and tall doors. Skylights go on the roof.

2A bay window

Pick Window (N), set Type to Bay window (angled sides), and click the middle of a bedroom's outside wall. It stands out from the outside of the wall, with a seat inside and a little roof over it.

3Its seat and roof

Pick the Select arrow and click the bay. In the panel try Side angle 30° or 90° (a box bay), and Over it → A copper roof. Open the 3D view to see it from outside.

4Windows joined as one

Pick Window again and set Type to Mulled unit (windows joined as one): a picture window with a casement each side and transoms above. Click the living room's front wall.

5A wall of sliding glass

Pick Door (D), set Type to Multi-slide / lift-and-slide door and Door height to 8' tall. Click the living room's side wall. In its panel, tick Open panels go into a pocket in the wall.

6A pivot front door

Select the front door. Click Make it a pivot entry door (or set its Type to Pivot entry door) and make it 48" wide and 8' tall: it turns on a pin set in from its edge.

7A skylight

Open Draw ▾ → Skylight (on a roof) and click the roof over the living room. It cuts the roof and opens a light shaft down to the ceiling. In its panel try Venting, or a Sun tunnel for a hall.

8In the schedule and the budget

Open Design ▾ → Door, window & room schedules…: the joined windows are one tag, and the skylight is S1. The energy worksheet counts the bay by all its glass, and the budget lists each one. A bedroom's bay passes egress through its opening side units.

Lesson 21: A custom kitchen and bath: an island, a tiled shower and shop drawings

Build an island from real cabinets with waterfall ends, draw a curbless tiled shower with a bench and a linear drain, and make the cabinet maker's shop drawings and cut list. About 10 minutes · 7 steps · starts from the “Two-bedroom cottage” template

1Custom pieces

Three things for a custom home: an island made of real cabinets, a tiled shower you draw yourself, and drawings a cabinet maker can build from.

2An island from cabinets

Pick Blocks (B), choose Cabinets → Island from cabinets, and click in the middle of the kitchen. Cabinets go on one side, stools on the other.

3Waterfall ends

Pick the Select arrow and click the island. In its panel set Waterfall to Both ends: the top runs down to the floor. Under it, Under the overhang shows the corbels a deep overhang needs.

4Draw a tiled shower

Pick Blocks again, choose Spa bath → Tiled shower (draw your own). Click one corner in the bathroom, then the opposite corner. (R turns it first: its back goes on the far side.)

5A bench and the drain

Select the shower. It is curbless with a linear drain to start; set Bench to Bench on the left. The panel lists the tile, the membrane and the code checks for the slope and the glass.

6Shop drawings

Select the island (or any cabinet) and open Shop drawings and cut list under the island's rows, or press Ctrl+K and type shop drawings. Click Add cabinet shop drawings to the sheets: each run of cabinets gets an elevation, plan, section and cut list sheet.

7In the estimate

Open the estimate: the island's cabinets, top, waterfall ends and receptacle are under Cabinets & built-ins, and the shower's tile, waterproofing membrane, drain and glass under Tile, Plumbing and Fixtures & appliances.

Lesson 22: An indoor golf simulator

Draw a 14 × 20 ft room with a 10 ft ceiling, put in the enclosure, the hitting mat and a launch monitor, and let the Design check find what would get in the swing. About 8 minutes · 11 steps · starts with a new plan

1Room to swing

A golf simulator needs more room than you'd think: a circle about 7 ft across for the swing, about 10 ft from the ball to the screen, and a ceiling high enough for a driver. You'll lay one out and let Arc check it.

2Draw the room

Pick Room (R). In the panel set Size measured to Inside (room size), type 14' x 20', press Enter, and click where the top-left corner goes.

3A 10 ft ceiling

Pick the Select arrow, click inside the room and set Ceiling height to 10'. A driver needs about 9′4″ for a 6 ft golfer; 8 ft is irons only.

4The enclosure

Pick Blocks (B) → Game room → Golf simulator enclosure. Click in the middle of one of the short walls: it turns to sit against the wall with the screen at the back.

5A gap behind the screen

Click an empty spot to see the Design check: the screen needs 12″ to 16″ clear behind it. Click Fix → Move it out from the wall on that warning.

6The hitting mat

Back in Blocks → Game room, pick Golf hitting mat and the 4′ × 7′ size. Press R until its arrow points at the screen, and click about 10 ft in front of it, in the middle. The dashed circle is the swing.

7Ten feet from the screen

Drag the mat until the Design check stops saying the ball is too close to the screen (about 10 ft, 3 m), with the ball lined up with the middle. Fix → Move the mat back or Line it up do it for you.

8Who hits

Select the mat and set Who hits to Both (ball in the middle): the swing circles show where a right- and a left-handed golfer stand. Set Tallest golfer too.

9A launch monitor

Add Golf launch monitor: radar behind the ball. If the Design check says it's in the wrong spot, click Fix → Put it in place: straight behind the ball, about 7 ft back.

10Try a basement ceiling

Select the room and set Ceiling height to 8', a typical older basement. The Design check now says how much room the swing has and what a driver needs.

11What you can do

In a real basement: move the lowest duct up between the joists, pick a hitting spot clear of beams, choose a camera unit (no extra depth), or plan for irons only (the warning's Fix). Moving a beam or a post, or lowering the floor, needs an engineer and a permit. The guide's An indoor golf simulator has all the numbers.

Lesson 23: Porches and decks, piece by piece

Move or remove one porch column, slide the steps, leave a railing off one side, then draw a deck and set each side's railing and posts. About 8 minutes · 8 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1One piece at a time

Porches and decks place their columns, posts, steps and railings for you. Select one and each piece gets an orange handle you can click, drag or delete.

2Select the porch

Pick the Select arrow and click the front porch. Orange handles show on its columns and steps.

3Pick a column

Click a column's orange handle. The panel shows Selected piece for it.

4Move or remove it

Drag the handle to move the column, or press Delete to take it out. The porch keeps your columns from now on (Back to automatic undoes that).

5Slide the steps

Drag the steps' handle along the front, or type Steps centred at in the panel.

6Railing on some sides

Set Railing to a style, then untick one side under Railing on.

7Draw a deck

Open Yard on the tool bar and pick Outdoor (O), set Draw to Deck, its height to about 30" and a railing, and click the corners of a deck at the back.

8One side open

Select the deck and click the handle in the middle of a side. Untick Railing on this side (for steps down to the yard).

Lesson 24: Framing: studs, cut-outs and joists

See the studs in 3D, double up studs or build a post, frame a cut-out, change one wall's spacing, and put closer joists under a heavy room. About 8 minutes · 9 steps · starts from the “Two-story shell” template

1The frame inside the walls

Every wall is framed for you at the spacing you choose. Each wall can also have its own spacing, extra studs and framed cut-outs, and a room upstairs can have closer joists under it.

2See the studs

Open View ▾ → Framing (studs): the 3D view shows every stud, plate and header.

3Extra studs

Click an outside wall. In the panel's This wall's framing, click + Extra studs. Set where they go (from the wall's start grip).

4Make it a post

Change those extra studs to Post (4 studs), for a beam to sit on. In 3D they show a shade darker.

5A cut-out

Click + Cut-out and set its width and height: a chase for a duct, or a recessed cabinet. It gets a header (or blocking) round it.

6This wall at 24"

Set this wall's Spacing to 24" on center.

7Draw framing on the wall

In the wall's Interior finish, click ✎ Work on this face in 3D, then the Framing tab: the studs show behind. Pick Header and drag across where a new opening goes, or Blocking where a TV or grab bar will hang. Watch it appear in the 3D framing.

8Closer joists upstairs

Switch the level bar to the Second floor, click inside the room and set Joists under it to 12" on center (or Doubled for a tub).

9It's all counted

Open Budget ▾ → Takeoff & cost estimate or Framing…: your studs, post, cut-out header and joists are in the counts and the cut lists.

Lesson 25: Only the checks you ask for, and the engineer's notes

Tick the heat loss on in Checks & analysis, open it, then keep the engineer's size for a window header as a Project note that shows on the drawings. About 6 minutes · 6 steps · starts from the “Two-story shell” template

1Only what you ask for

A new plan doesn't fill up with every calculation Arc can do. The heat loss, the energy tier and the sun study wait until you tick them in Checks & analysis. Safety warnings (stairs, railings, escape windows) always show.

2Tick the heat loss

With nothing selected, open Checks & analysis ▾ at the top of the panel's Design check and tick Heat loss, heating and cooling.

3Open it

Open Review ▾ → Heat loss, room by room…: it's in the menu now. Each room's loss on the coldest day, for the heating contractor. Close it when you've looked.

4A note about a window

Click a window on the main floor. At the bottom of its panel pick Add a project note about this….

5Engineer says

Tick Engineer says and type their size, for example 2-ply 1-3/4" × 9-1/2" LVL, with their name and the date. Tick Show on drawings, then Save note.

6Where it shows

That window's header now reads "… (per engineer)" in the schedules, the framing, the 3D labels and the parts list, with Arc's own size beside it, and Arc's warning on it is quieted. Shown notes print in the General notes block on the sheets. Review ▾ → Project notes… holds every note for this job: the inspector's, the client's, a supplier's.

Lesson 26: Pipes & wires: wiring, runs and their points

Have Arc place outlets, lights and the wiring, read the circuits, reroute a pipe by its points, draw your own wire, and look at a clean floor plan. About 8 minutes · 8 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Power, water and air

Pipes & wires are the wiring, plumbing and ducts (builders call them MEP: mechanical, electrical and plumbing). Arc can suggest a whole floor of it from your rooms, and you can change any run point by point.

2Suggest a layout with wiring

Open Design ▾ → Pipes & wires: outlets, wiring, plumbing, ducts…, keep Wiring ticked and click Suggest layout. Outlets, switches, lights, wires, pipes and ducts go in.

3Read the wiring

Curved lines join the outlets on each circuit, dashed switch legs run to the lights, and arrows with a number are home runs to the panel. The numbers match Budget ▾ → Electrical: circuits & panel.

4Pick a point on a pipe

Click a pipe or duct run. Orange handles show at its corners: click one.

5Reroute it

Drag that handle somewhere else, or click + Add a bend in the panel.

6An outlet on a wall, in 3D

Click a wall, then ✎ Work on this face in 3D in Interior finish, and the Electrical tab. Pick Outlet and click on the wall: it goes on at 16" up (or where you click, if that's well above or below), in 3D and in the takeoff.

7Draw your own wire

Open Pipes & wires on the tool bar and pick Ducts, pipes & wires (or press U), set the system to Wiring, and click from an outlet to another one, then Esc.

8A clean floor plan

Open Show ▾ and tick Clean floor plan: just the layout, furniture, room names and outside dimensions. Untick it to see the wiring again.

Newer tools: locking, painting, options and costs

Lesson 27: Lock part of the plan, move the rest

Use the quick bar over a selection, lock part of the house together so it can't move, box-select and move everything else around it, then unlock it in one click. About 5 minutes · 7 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Keep part of it still

When you're rearranging, you often want one part to stay exactly where it is. Locking it does that: it can't be picked, dragged or caught in a box until you unlock it.

2Pick a part

With the Select arrow, drag a box round the bathroom (its walls, door and fixtures). A small quick bar appears above what you picked: Lock, Move, Turn 90°, Edit, Delete.

3Lock it

Click Lock on the quick bar (or press Ctrl+L). A dashed outline and a padlock show it's locked as one group.

4Box round everything

Now drag a box round the whole house. Everything is picked except the locked part.

5Move the rest

Click Move on the quick bar, click a base point, then where it goes (a few feet over is fine). The locked bathroom stays where it was, and walls joined to it leave it alone.

6Unlock it

Press Ctrl+Z if you'd like the house back where it was. Then click any part of the bathroom: the quick bar offers Unlock, which frees the whole group at once.

7Good to know

Modify ▾ has Lock the selection together and Unlock everything (Ctrl+Shift+L). Locks are saved with the plan. If you'd rather not see the quick bar, turn it off in Preferences.

Lesson 28: Paint a room, a floor or the whole house at once

Paint every wall in a room with one click, then a whole floor, and put one wall's finish on all the walls from its panel. About 5 minutes · 8 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Many walls at once

Painting one side of one wall is the Paint tool's usual click. You can also paint every wall in a room, every room on a floor, or the whole house, inside walls only (the outside keeps its siding).

2The Paint tool

Open Finishes on the tool bar and pick Paint (K).

3Every wall in a room

In the panel set One click paints to Every wall in that room, and pick a paint colour.

4Click a wall

Click a wall from inside the living room. All four walls of the room take the colour (open the 3D view to see).

5The whole floor

Set One click paints to Every room on this floor, pick another colour and click any inside wall.

6From a wall's panel

Pick the Select arrow and click a bedroom wall. In Interior finish, choose a colour for the side facing the bedroom, then click Apply to all walls….

7Choose how far

Click This room (its name) to give the bedroom's walls the same finish. Undo takes it back in one step.

8In 3D too

The same Apply to all walls… is in the 3D wall designer (Work on this face in 3D, under Wall colour), and it works for wallpaper and panelling as well as paint.

Lesson 29: Options & upgrades a homeowner can choose

Give a room a coffered ceiling, see what it adds over a flat ceiling with its own material list, leave it out and watch the plan change, mark the patio optional, and make the homeowner's options page. About 8 minutes · 9 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Have it, or not

Some things are a choice: a coffered ceiling, hardwood, a fireplace, a deck. Arc prices each one against the standard way to build it, and leaving one out puts the standard version into the plan, so the plan and the estimate always agree.

2A coffered ceiling

With the Select arrow, click inside the living room (on its name). In the panel set Ceiling to Coffered. The 3D view shows the beams.

3Options & upgrades

Open Budget ▾ → Options & upgrades…. The coffered ceiling is listed with what it adds instead of a flat painted ceiling.

4What's in it

Click Materials beside it: the beams and moulding it adds, and the flat ceiling it replaces.

5Leave it out

Untick the coffered ceiling. The living room's ceiling goes flat in the plan and 3D, and the totals drop by what it costs.

6Put it back

Tick it again: the coffered ceiling comes back exactly as it was. Then close the window.

7Make something optional

Click the patio (or anything outside). In the panel's Layer section set Optional to Optional, in a new group… and call it Patio.

8The homeowner's page

Open Budget ▾ → Options & upgrades… again: the patio is listed too. Options page for the homeowner (PDF)… makes a page with each option's price, tick boxes and a signature line.

9Your margin (Arc)

In Arc, each option shows Your cost, the Client price (with your markup and labour rate) and your Margin; the homeowner's page shows only the client price. Budget ▾ → Quote for the client… has Your labour cost and a green box with your margin that never goes on the quote.

Lesson 30: Try another version: design options and exterior looks

Keep the plan as Option A, make Option B, give B a farmhouse exterior in one click, compare the two side by side, and switch back. About 6 minutes · 7 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1Two versions in one file

Not sure between two ideas? Keep both. Design options hold other versions of the design in the same file, each with its own estimate.

2Design options

Open Design ▾ → Design options (A, B, C)….

3Make Option B

Click Make Option B from this plan… and keep the name. The plan as it was is kept as Option A; Option B is now on the plan to change.

4A new look for B

Open Design ▾ → Exterior looks… and click Modern farmhouse: board & batten, black window trim and corner boards on every outside wall. Open the 3D view to see it.

5Compare them

Open Design ▾ → Design options… and click Compare beside Option A: A and B side by side (try Wipe).

6Back to A

Close it, open Design options… again and click Show it beside Option A. B is kept as it is, to come back to.

7Good to know

Each option is its own drawing; the project, sheets and prices are shared. The options are saved in the plan file. Undo starts again after switching.

Lesson 31: More budget tools: price tags, room costs, printing and backups

See where every price comes from and undo one, split the estimate room by room, print the floor plan in one click, and find Arc's backup copies. About 6 minutes · 5 steps · starts from the Lesson house (finished)

1The cost estimate

Open Budget ▾ → Takeoff & cost estimate…. Under each price a small tag says where it came from: typical, yours, price book, trade pack or no price.

2Type a price, then undo it

Type a price over one line: its tag says yours. Click ↶ Undo at the bottom and it goes back, in order with your other changes.

3Cost room by room

Close the estimate and open Budget ▾ → Cost room by room…: each room's walls, floor and furniture, its total and cost per square foot. The roof and foundation are a whole-house line.

4Print the floor plan

Close it and press Ctrl+P (or File ▾ → Print this floor plan…): the floor as it shows, on one page, to print or save as a PDF.

5Backup copies

Close it and open File ▾ → Backup copies…: earlier copies Arc keeps of your saved plans and unsaved work. Open one and it starts as a new plan, so nothing is overwritten.

Drafting by typing

Lesson 32: CAD drills: exact drawing by typing

Five short drills done with typed sizes and commands, the AutoCAD way: a 3600 × 4200 room, a 900 door centred on the long wall, walls from typed points, a rectangle with @, and a line squared off a wall. About 15 minutes · 7 steps · starts with a new plan

1Drills

Each drill is one exact thing to draw, done by typing, and Arc checks it to the millimetre. Type straight away: what you type shows in the Command box, and Enter uses it. Any unit works: 3600mm, 3.6m or 11'9 3/4".

2A 3600 × 4200 room

Pick Room (R, or type ROOM and Enter). Type 3600mm x 4200mm and press Enter, then click where its top-left corner goes.

3A 900 door centred on the long wall

Pick Door (D) and set its Width in the panel to 900mm. Hover one of the 4200 walls: when the door sits in the middle, Centered shows in green. Click.

4Walls from typed points

Type W and Enter (or press W). Then type each point and press Enter: 0,0 starts at the plan's origin, @5000mm,0 goes 5000 across, and 3000mm<90 goes 3000 straight up. Press Esc to stop.

5A 2400 × 1200 rectangle with @

Type PL and Enter for a polyline. Click a start point away from the walls, then type @2400mm,0, @0,1200mm, @-2400mm,0 and @0,-1200mm, pressing Enter after each. Press Enter once more to finish.

6Square off a wall

Type L and Enter for a line. Click a point about 1000 above the 5000 wall, then move straight toward the wall: Perpendicular shows in blue with the wall dashed. Click there, then Esc. (F10 turns these guides off and on.)

7More

That's drafting by typing. Snap & units ▾ switches each kind of snap (F3 to F11), and Snap & units ▾ → Keyboard shortcuts… lets you change any key and print the key map.

Start from your lot

Lesson 33: Start from your lot: its size, shape and setbacks

New lot from the lot's size and setbacks: a pie lot, any shape typed side by side or from a Real Property Report's bearings, an Edmonton address that fills it all in, and the building area the house has to stay inside. About 6 minutes · 9 steps ·

1Start from the lot

Every house starts with its lot: how big it is, which side faces the street, and how far the house has to stay from each property line (the setbacks, from your city's zoning). Arc draws it all from a few numbers.

2New lot

Open File ▾ → New lot (size and setbacks)….

3Its size

Set Units to Metres and Lot width to 12. The picture beside the numbers changes as you type: the red line is the lot, the thick red side is the street, and the blue dashed outline is the building area, where the house may go.

4A pie lot

Lots on a cul-de-sac or round a curve are wedges. Set Lot shape to Pie (wider at the back) and Width at the back to 20: the building area follows the wedge, each side kept back by its own setback.

5Any shape

Set Lot shape to Irregular: type its sides: the sides start from your pie. Go round with the lot on your left, side 1 along the street. Change a corner angle and watch the picture, then clear the last side's length: Arc closes the lot with a straight line (dashed in the picture) and says how long it is.

6From a survey

Have a Real Property Report or the lot's survey plan? Open Paste the bearings from a Real Property Report under the sides, type its sides one a line (a bearing and a length, like N 89°58'10" E 15.24, the street side first) and click Use these: Arc draws the lot exactly and sets true north from it, so the shadows fall the right way.

7In Edmonton

In Edmonton, type the address in Edmonton address and click Find (it needs the internet): Arc fills in the lot's size, the zone's setbacks, height and site coverage (Zoning Bylaw 20001) and the buildings already there. No Real Property Report? Open Find your lot's survey under the result. Elsewhere, click Next.

8Start with the lot

Click Start with the lot (or Use this house on a template that fits the building area).

9Stay inside it

The red line is the property line, with the street at the bottom, and the blue dashed outline is the building area. Draw the house inside it: the Design check warns if a wall goes outside, on any floor. To fix the lot later, select the property line and drag a corner.

Lesson 34: Do it with me: your Edmonton lot from SLIM Maps

Find your lot on the city's SLIM map (maps.edmonton.ca), measure its sides and take a picture of it, then make it in Arc: the address lookup, your measurements, the picture under the plan, scaled and lined up, and the zone's rules in the Design check. About 15 minutes · 12 steps ·

1What you'll do

You'll find your lot on the City of Edmonton's map (the public face of SLIM Maps), measure it and take a picture of it, then make the lot in Arc to design on. You need your address, a web browser and about 15 minutes. Keep this panel open beside the browser.

2Open the city's map

In your web browser open maps.edmonton.ca and click Address Lookup. Every lot line in Edmonton is on it.

3Find your lot

Type your address in Type to Search (top right) and press Enter. The map zooms to your lot and the panel shows its Legal description, like Plan 8822077 Blk 6 Lot 17A: write it down. It's what Land Titles and a surveyor ask for.

4Measure it, in metres

Click Draw & Measure (the pencil on the left), then Measurement Settings and set Length Units to Meters. Click Measure, click one front corner of your lot, then the other front corner, and double-click: that's the width along the street. Measure one side the same way: the depth. Zoom right in for each corner. Write both down.

5Take a picture of it

Zoom so your whole lot fills most of the screen and take a screenshot: on Windows press Windows + Shift + S, drag round the lot and save the picture (on a Mac, Shift + Command + 4). Arc will put it under the plan to check your lot, or to trace one that isn't a rectangle.

6Back in Arc: your address

Open File ▾ → New lot from an Edmonton address…, type your address and click Find (it needs the internet). Arc fills in the lot's size from the city, your zone's setbacks and the buildings already there.

7Your measurements

Type the width and depth you measured. If they're more than 8% off the city's lot area, the window says so: measure again. Not a rectangle? Set Lot shape to Pie, or to Irregular and type each side (the picture will help). If the street side is wrong, click The street is on the other side.

8Start with the lot

Choose how to draw the buildings already there (Pre-existing walls, kept for a renovation or a yard), then click Start with the lot.

9Bring in the picture

Open File ▾ → Plot plan or survey… and pick your screenshot. It goes under the plan, see-through.

10Scale it

Click the two front corners of your lot on the picture, then type the width you measured (like 7.57 m) and press Enter. The picture is now the right size.

11Line it up

Select the picture and drag it so its lot sits on Arc's red property line (turn it with Rotate if it's at an angle). Where they don't match, select the property line and drag its corners onto the picture's. Arc's existing house and garage come from the city's roof outlines, so they should land on the buildings in the picture.

12Your zone's rules

Click an empty spot on the plan: the Design check now knows your zone's height limit and site coverage, and Specs & guidelines… lists them with the bylaw section. Draw your house or yard inside the blue building area. For the lot's exact lines, open Find your lot's survey in New lot. That's your lot, ready to design on!

Lesson 35: Site plan: grading and drainage

A new house on its lot: see where the ground slopes, level a patio and read its cut and fill, follow the water from a downspout, and list what to sort out before digging. About 15 minutes · 6 steps · starts from the “New house, bare yard” template

1Welcome

A 50' × 120' lot with a new house. The ground has spot heights: 8" below the main floor at the house, falling to the street and the lane. The dashed box behind the house is a grading area: the part of the yard to reshape.

2See the slope

Click the grading area's edge. In its panel set Shade the area by to Slope (%) and tick Slope arrows: green is 2–5%, good for lawn; blue is too flat to drain.

3Level a patio

Open Yard on the tool bar and pick Outdoor, set Draw to Ground: flat pad, and draw a 4 m × 4 m pad just behind the house, inside the grading area. Set its height to -10" in its panel.

4Cut and fill

Select the grading area again and set Shade the area by to Cut and fill: red is dug down, blue built up. The panel says how much soil, and whether it goes away or comes in.

5Where the water goes

Click one of the downspouts at the back corners of the house. The dashed line shows where its water runs; its panel says where it ends up and sizes a rain barrel or rain garden for it.

6Before you dig

Open Review ▾ → Before you build: digging & permits…: the locate call, the permits this site needs and the utility right-of-way at the back. A commercial site still needs a civil engineer's grading plan.

One floor at a time, and layers

Lesson 36: One floor at a time, and freezing layers

Show just one floor on the plan and in 3D, step up and down floors, freeze a layer you don't want to see, use a quick set, and look inside the walls as they're built. About 5 minutes · 8 steps · starts from the “Two-story shell” template

1One floor, everywhere

The level bar over the plan picks the floor you're working on, and the 3D view follows it. One switch says how much of the house shows round it.

2Open the 3D view

Click 3D view on the top bar, so you can watch the plan and the model together.

3Just this floor

On the level bar, click This floor. The plan shows only this floor, and 3D shows it alone with its roof off, so you look into the rooms.

4Up a floor

Click ▲ beside the level picker (or press Page Up). The plan and the 3D view go upstairs together.

5Freeze a layer

In the Layers list, point at Room names (or any layer you don't want to see) and click the snowflake that shows beside it. (On an iPad: Quick sets, then its snowflake.) It's hidden on the plan and in 3D, and nothing on it can be picked.

6A quick set

Click Quick sets at the top of Layers, then Structure only: just the walls, doors and windows, stairs, beams and roofs. The frozen layer stays frozen.

7Inside the walls

In the same window, under Walls in 3D, as built, tick Insulation and then Drywall. The 3D view shows the studs, the batts in each bay and the board on the inside, with the openings left open.

8Everything back

Untick the wall parts, click Everything, Unfreeze all and Whole house. The plan and 3D show the whole house again.

9. Making a showcase tour video

A tour is a camera path through your model: a drone flight in, a walk through the door, special views along the way. The program plays it smoothly and saves it as an MP4 video, captions and all.

The pictures here are from a tour of a finished plan. To watch a finished tour in the program, open File ▾ → Samples… → Fallingwater or Lakeside House. Click 3D view, then Tour on the 3D toolbar, then ▶ Play. Each lasts a minute or two.

One floor at a time, and Back outside

The floor list on the 3D toolbar (it starts at Whole house) names every floor: Main floor only, Second floor only, Basement only, or a floor and below. Showing one floor leaves off the floors above it and that floor's own roofs and ceilings, so you look straight down into its rooms like a doll's house, without walking up through the second floor. It's the same floor picker as the plan's: a floor picked here is the floor the plan draws too, and the level bar's This floor / + below / Whole house switch follows (and the other way round).

⤴ Back outside (next to Fit) takes the camera out of the rooms, stops walking, unlocks the view and shows the whole house from the front corner. It turns purple while the camera is inside, so it's easy to find if the view gets stuck in a tight space.

The camera controls

Tours are made from camera positions, so it helps to know how to move the camera first.

🔒 Lock view on the 3D toolbar holds the model still while you click and drag on it (picking pieces, designing a wall face); click View locked to orbit, pan and zoom again.

To…Do this
Orbit round the modelDrag with the left mouse button (one finger on the iPad)
Zoom and slide on the iPadPinch to zoom; drag with two fingers to slide (the plan: two fingers pan, a pinch zooms). Inside, one finger turns your head and a pinch moves you
Pan (slide sideways)Drag with the right or middle button (two fingers)
ZoomScroll the wheel (pinch). It zooms toward the cursor, and stops 18" short of a wall, floor or the ground rather than passing into it. Zooming out always opens up quickly, however close you were; Fit shows everything again.
Look at somethingDouble-click it to centre the camera there
Square viewsLook from…: Top, Front, Back, Left, Right, 3D (corner) and Below (from under the floor). Or click a letter on the compass in the corner.
Find a 3D toolThe toolbar is one row. When the 3D view is narrow, the tools that don't fit wait under More ▾ at the end of it. Make the 3D view wider (drag the bar between the plan and 3D) and they come back onto the row.
Frame everything / the selectionFit / Selected
Perspective or flatPerspective switches to a flat (parallel) view, like a drawing
Look around insideGo inside… stands you in any room. Inside the walls the controls change by themselves: drag (one finger) to look around, wheel (pinch) to move about 2' at a time toward what's under the cursor, right-drag (two fingers) to slide, double-click (double-tap) a floor to go there. Step outside and the usual orbit comes back.
Walk throughWalk: W A S D or arrow keys to walk, drag to look. Stairs carry you up and down, F flies, Esc stops.
Change what you seeThe view list (or View ▾): Full model, Framing, Framing + pipes & wires, Pipes & wires check, Empty, Roof off, X-ray, Wireframe
Real sunlight and shadowsSun: pick the place, date and time
Keep a camera positionSaved views… → + Save this view…, then pick it from the same list any time

1 Open the Tour panel

3D view → Tour
  1. Click 3D view on the top bar.
  2. Click Tour on the 3D toolbar. The Tour panel opens over the 3D view. A new plan's tour is empty.

2 Frame the first shot and add it

Move the camera → + Add this view
  1. Orbit, pan and zoom until the picture is how you want the video to open. A high view of the whole building works well.
  2. Click + Add this view. It becomes stop 1, and its settings open underneath (see step 5).
  3. Move the camera to the next shot and click + Add this view again. Repeat for each shot. The tour glides smoothly from one stop to the next.

3 Add your saved views

Tour → + Add the saved views
  1. If you've saved views (Saved views…), + Add the saved views adds them all as stops, each captioned with its name.

4 Record a walk (optional)

Tour → ● Record a walk
  1. Click ● Record a walk. Walk mode turns on.
  2. Walk with W A S D and drag to look. Every few steps a path point is added, so the tour follows your route exactly: through doors, up the stairs, along the ramp.
  3. Click ■ Stop recording when you're done.
A path point is a stop with no pause. Use them to steer the camera round corners and through doorways, so it doesn't fly through walls.

5 Give each stop a caption, a view and the light

Click a stop → its settings
  1. Click a stop's name. The camera goes there and its settings open:
    • Pause: seconds to hold still there. Use 2–3 for a stop with a caption, 0 for a path point.
    • Caption: words shown at the bottom of the picture while the camera is there. They fade in and out, and they're burned into the video.
    • Switch to: a view to change to when the camera arrives, such as X-ray, Framing + pipes & wires, Pipes & wires check or Roof off. Set the next stop back to Full model.
    • Light: the sun's time of day. Give several stops a time and the sun moves smoothly between them, shadows and all. Studio light turns the sun off, and My custom lighting uses the Render window's custom lighting (its sun, brightness, background and field of view).
    • Go on at: how fast the camera travels on to the next stop: Slow stroll, Walking pace, Quick fly-through or Drone flight. Fly outside, walk inside.

6 Tidy up

The stop list
  • ↑ moves a stop earlier. × removes it.
  • Use this view re-aims a stop to where the camera is now. Move the camera first, then click it.
  • Go there flies the camera to the stop, to check it.
  • Clear removes every stop, to start again.

7 Choose the speed and the video size

Bottom of the Tour panel
  1. The first list is the tour's usual speed (each stop's Go on at can override it).
  2. The second is the video size: HD 1280 × 720 (good for email and phones), Full HD 1920 × 1080 (sharpest, for TVs and presentations), or the size of the 3D view.

8 Preview it

Tour → ▶ Play
  1. Click ▶ Play. The camera follows the path, switching views, moving the sun and showing captions. Click ■ Stop to stop early.
  2. Adjust any stop and play again until it flows.

9 Save the video

Tour → Save video…
  1. Click Save video…. The program draws the tour frame by frame at 30 frames a second, so the video is smooth even if the live preview isn't. A progress bar shows how far it's got. A 90-second HD tour takes a few minutes.
  2. When it's done, choose where to save the .mp4 file. It plays on any computer, phone or TV and can be emailed, posted or put in a presentation.
The tour is saved with the plan, so you can come back, change a stop and save a new video any time.

Tips for a good tour

  • Tell a story: start wide (the whole building), come closer (the street), go inside, show something special (X-ray, pipes and wires, roof off), and end on a wide shot.
  • Keep captions short, under about 50 characters, and pause 2–3 seconds so people can read them.
  • Fly outside, walk inside. Use Drone flight for the big moves and walking pace indoors.
  • Steer with path points through doorways and round corners. Put one in the middle of a room before rising or turning.
  • Let the sun move: give the first stop a morning time and the last one late afternoon, and the light sweeps across the building.
  • Switch views at a pause, so the change happens while the camera is still.

An example: a museum tour, stop by stop

#ShotCaption, view and light
1High over the corner of Fifth Avenue"The museum, modelled in Arc" · Full model · sun 9:00 AM · drone flight on
2Street view from Fifth Avenue (a saved view)"Saved views: from Fifth Avenue" · drone flight on
3On the sidewalk facing the door"Walk-through: up to the entrance"
4Just inside the doorpath point (no pause)
5On the rotunda floor, looking up"Inside the rotunda: six turns of ramp under a glass dome"
6Middle of the atrium, 40 ft uppath point: rising through the open centre
7On the ramp, halfway up"Halfway up the ramp, 36 feet above the floor"
8Back to the centrepath point
9Under the dome, looking down"Looking down from the top of the ramp"
10Up through the domepath point · drone flight on
11High from the south-east"X-ray: see through every surface" · Switch to X-ray · 1:00 PM
12Low from the east"Pipes and wires: sprinklers under every turn, ducts in the tower" · Switch to Pipes & wires check
13Over the tower"Roof off: look down into the top floor" · Switch to Roof off

To record a lesson that shows your clicks on the plan, use the Windows screen recorder instead. Press Win+Shift+R (Snipping Tool), drag round the Arc window, and follow the steps in Part 8.

10. Quick reference

All of this on one page to print and keep beside you: the quick reference card.

Keyboard shortcuts

KeyToolKeyTool
VSelectBBlocks library
WWallUDucts, pipes & wires
RRoomFRoof
DDoorGPorch
NWindowKPaint
SStairsM / CMove / Duplicate
AFloors & baseboardsQ / XRotate / Mirror
OOutdoor (site)ZZoom to fit
EMeasuring tapeYDecorate (palettes)
Ctrl+KSearch for any commandCtrl+,Preferences
Ctrl+Z / YUndo / RedoCtrl+SSave
Ctrl+C / VCopy / paste (also into another plan)Ctrl+XCut
F3–F11Snaps on and off (see Which snaps are on)Ctrl+GGroup (Ctrl+Shift+G takes it apart)

Change any key. Snap & units ▾ → Keyboard shortcuts… lists every shortcut by group. Click one and press the new key (Backspace for none, Esc to keep it). A key used twice turns red, and the first one listed wins until you change one of them. Changed keys show what they were; Reset all puts Arc's own back, and Print the key map prints them all on one page. Your keys are kept on this computer for every plan. Enter, Esc, Space, Delete, the arrows, Ctrl+K and Ctrl+, keep their own jobs.

Measuring tape

  • Measure (left toolbar, key E): click points to measure. Each leg shows its length; the readout gives the running total, how far across and up, and the angle. Click the first point again for the area and perimeter. Nothing is added to the plan unless you press D to keep the last leg as a dimension. Esc clears it.
  • In 3D: the Measure button on the 3D toolbar. Click two points on any surfaces for the straight distance, how far across and how far up.

Drawing ducts, pipes and wires in 3D

  • View locked (on while you draw): clicks only place points and the model doesn't turn. Turn the view with the right mouse button (two fingers on a tablet), pan with the middle button. Click View locked to let the left button turn it again.
  • ↑ Up a floor / ↓ Down a floor: takes the run straight up (or down) through the floor to the same working height on the next level, then you keep drawing there.
  • Riser: click a spot on a wall or the floor and a vertical run goes from this floor up to the floor above, set just off the wall face. Good for vent stacks, chases and wiring up a wall.

Suggested layouts (a starting point)

  • Design ▾ → Rooms for this floor: draw the outside walls of a floor, tick the rooms you want (bedrooms, baths, kitchen, living, laundry…) and get three layouts to compare. Each is scored for hallway space, room shapes and sizes, wet rooms sharing plumbing walls and bedrooms on outside walls; the best is marked most ergonomic. Pick one to add the interior walls, doors, rooms, any missing bedroom windows and a front door.
  • Design ▾ → Pipes & wires: outlets, wiring, plumbing, ducts: places outlets, switches, lights and smoke/CO alarms, drains, the vent stack and hot and cold supply, and supply registers, returns and ducts, from your rooms, doors, windows and fixtures. Outlets and switches sit beside studs; drains and ducts run with the floor joists. Choose Most ergonomic (outlets every 8' at 18", switches at 42", registers under windows, a return in each bedroom, PEX home runs) or Code minimum. Name your rooms first so each gets what it needs. With Wiring ticked it draws the wiring too, the way electrical plans show it: a curved line from outlet to outlet round each room for every circuit, dashed switch legs from each switch to its lights, linked smoke and CO alarms, and a home run toward the panel tagged with the circuit number (or drawn all the way to the panel). The circuits and numbers match Electrical: circuits & panel. Running it with only Wiring ticked redraws the wiring and keeps your outlets and lights.
  • Both add a note on the plan and in the design check: architect / engineer to review. They're ideas to start from, not stamped designs. Running one again replaces the earlier suggestion on that floor.

Copy parts between plans, arches and coffered ceilings

  • Copy and paste between plans: select anything, press Ctrl+C (or Modify ▾ → Copy to clipboard), open another plan and press Ctrl+V (Modify ▾ → Paste). Pieces land in the same place, or 2' over when pasted back into the same plan, and keep their sizes, finishes and photo materials. Handy for a parts library or a renovation copy of a house.
  • Arched openings: select a cased opening (Door tool → Cased opening (no door)) and set Arched top to how high the arch rises. Make the opening as wide as the space and lower its height for an arched bulkhead between rooms.
  • Coffered ceiling: select a room and choose the ceiling type Coffered (beams with recessed panels).

Your own photos as materials: tile, expand, fit or stamp

In the Paint tool, Add a photo material… turns any picture (a tile, wallpaper, a floor sample, a mural, a rug, artwork) into a material. Say how wide an area the photo shows, then choose how it goes on:

Apply asWhat it doesGood for
TileRepeats the photo across the surface at its real sizeFloor tiles, planks, wallpaper, fabric, stone
ExpandGrows a much bigger version of the pattern from your sample, so it doesn't visibly repeat, and fills with thatHardwood, stone, concrete, carpet, grass, cork
FitStretches one copy over each wall, floor or shape you click, edge to edgeA mural, a feature wall, a rug or floor design that fills the room
StampPlaces one copy where you click, at its real size, on the floor or on a wall faceAn inlay or medallion, a doormat, artwork, a tile panel
How Expand works: it cuts overlapping patches from your sample and fits them together so each matches its neighbours, joining them along the least visible line, then blends the edges so the bigger texture tiles without a seam. It notices rows of planks or boards and keeps them lined up. Try another grows a different variation; Grow a new variation… in the Paint panel does it later. Strict patterns (herringbone, checkerboard) are best left on Tile.
  • Change a photo's Apply as any time: pick it in the Paint tool (My colors & photos). Everything painted with it updates.
  • Use it in other plans: pick it and click Keep in My library, then From My library… in the Paint tool of any plan (see My library).
  • Click a stamp to select it. You can drag it (on the floor), set its width and height (it keeps the photo's shape unless you untick that), turn it, swap the photo, or press Real size. On a wall, set its centre height, where it sits along the wall, and which face it's on.
  • Walls read the right way round from the room side.

A clean bit of a photo, with the light evened out. After you pick the photo, drag a box round a clean patch of the counter, tile, wood or stone: away from reflections, shadows and things standing on it (drag the box to move it, or drag anywhere else for a new one). On the right it repeats 3 × 3, the way it will on a floor or wall, so a seam or a light patch shows straight away.

  • Even out the light (on to start with) takes out the slow change in brightness across the box (brighter by the window, darker in the corners) and keeps the grain, veins and grout lines. The note under the preview says how much the light changed across the box before and after.
  • Blend the edges fades each copy into the next, so there's no seam where they meet.
  • Use the whole photo skips the box. Then say how wide the box (or the photo) is, as before.
  • Change it later: pick the photo in the Paint tool (My colors & photos) and click Move the box or change the light… (Even out the light / use a box… on photos added before). The whole photo is kept, and everything painted with it updates. A pattern grown with Expand is grown again from the new box.

11. The Prairie & Mid-Century library

An homage to Frank Lloyd Wright and the mid-century modern houses that followed. It's a set of original furniture, materials and finishes drawn in that spirit: enough to build a Robie House or a Hollyhock House of your own.

Furniture and lights

Pick the Blocks tool and choose the category Prairie & Mid-Century.

Finishes include quartersawn and fumed oak, walnut, teak, Cherokee red, and mid-century orange, mustard, teal, olive and charcoal.

Materials and textures

  • Paint tool → Prairie & Mid-Century: Roman brick (tawny, iron-spot red, cream), dressed limestone, quartersawn and fumed oak, Usonian cypress board-and-batten, Cherokee red and gray scored concrete, two leaded art-glass designs, patterned textile block, a cast-stone hollyhock frieze and Hollyhock stucco, teak, grooved mahogany and walnut paneling, cork, and Prairie and mid-century paint and fabric colours.
  • Outside walls: select a wall → Exterior finish → Roman brick (Prairie), Usonian board & recessed batten or Textile block.
  • Floors tool: Scored concrete (Usonian) on a 4' grid in Cherokee red, gray or ochre, Cork tile, and quartersawn oak, fumed oak and teak wood floors.
  • Terraces and chimneys: Roman brick parapets and chimneys.
  • Friezes and bands: draw a thin box with Draw ▾ → 3D shape along the top of a wall and paint it Hollyhock frieze. Painted shapes show their pattern at its real size on every face.
There's also a timelapse video of the Robie House going up, piece by piece, in the program. It's in the docs folder as Robie House timelapse.mp4.

12. Good to know

ArcRes, the home edition

ArcRes is Arc for homes: the same program and the same .aarc plans, with everything for designing, decorating, budgeting and 3D printing a house, and the commercial and pro tools (commercial projects, quotes for a client, change orders, cut lists, price books and takeoff on a PDF) tucked away. The tools for designing a custom home are all there: the building code summary, the permit drawings check, the electrical panel, finishes and allowances, the ceiling plan, and DWG and IFC files for an architect or engineer. Its welcome screen has just three big choices, and its Budget menu keeps the cost estimate and the shopping list. A plan made in ArcRes opens in Arc, and the other way round. ArcRes is built from the same code, so every improvement to Arc reaches it too.

In ArcRes, the advanced drawing tools (lines and curves, hatches, section cuts, ducts, pipes and wires, and trim, offset, fillet and the rest of Modify ▾) show a small lock until you finish the first lesson, Getting started with the tools. Everything for designing your home is open from the start. Reach for a locked tool and you can start the lesson, or choose I know my way around: show all tools. Either way they stay unlocked from then on, and ArcRes doesn't ask again. To lock them again (for a new user on the same computer), use the switch in File ▾ → Preferences.

The code checks are a guide for early design. A licensed design professional and the building official have the final say.

  • Atrium enclosure. The atrium check covers sprinklers and smoke control. It doesn't check how the atrium is separated from the rooms round it (IBC 404.6: 1-hour walls, or glass with closely spaced sprinklers). Set those walls' fire rating yourself.
  • Ramp floors. Each floor's stretch of a spiral ramp runs halfway to the floors below and above. A door from the ramp counts where it opens onto the ramp's footprint and has no room on that side.
  • The core and the ramp. A shaft that rises through the ramp's footprint (like a museum's core) shows passing through the ramp slab in 3D. The ramp isn't cut round it.
